### LEWIS (LayEr WIse Sparsity) -- A Training Free Guided Model Merging Approach
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.03874v1)
- **Authors**: Hetarth Chopra, Vidhi Rambhia, Vikram Adve
- **Abstract**: As specialized large language models (LLMs) become increasingly prevalent, model merging methods are being used to combine them to create a single multi-task model without requiring any additional data or training. However, these approaches fall short when the objective of merging is to increase the downstream model's performance on a particular task-specific benchmark. In this work, we propose LEWIS (Layer Wise Sparsity), a guided model-merging framework that uses activation-based layer importance to dynamically adjust layer-wise task-vector sparsity required for the merge process. LEWIS uses a calibration dataset to prioritize critical layers during the task-vector pruning process required for model merging. This approach guides existing merging methods by preserving essential layer-wise task-specific knowledge while ensuring the merged model performs the best at benchmarks resembling the calibration dataset. Our experiments demonstrate the effectiveness of LEWIS with performance improvements of code instruction-following and math-solving models created through model merging up to 4 percent and 11.3 percent, respectively, outperforming unguided data-less model merging approaches that use uniform-sparsity.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces LEWIS (LayEr WIse Sparsity), a training-free guided model merging approach. It aims to improve the performance of merged large language models (LLMs) on specific tasks by dynamically adjusting layer-wise task-vector sparsity during the merging process. LEWIS utilizes a calibration dataset to prioritize critical layers based on activation-based layer importance. The approach enhances existing merging methods by preserving essential layer-wise task-specific knowledge, leading to improved performance on benchmarks related to the calibration dataset. Experiments demonstrate performance gains in code instruction-following and math-solving tasks by selectively preserving critical task-vector components.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its guided approach to model merging using layer-wise sparsity, informed by a calibration dataset. This is a departure from purely data-less or uniform sparsity approaches. The idea of using layer importance to guide the merging process is interesting and could potentially lead to improvements in model performance. The connection to Wanda pruning, focusing on outlier activations, is also relevant.

**Significance:** Model merging is a rapidly evolving area, and this paper contributes to the ongoing efforts to create multi-task models without additional training.  The potential to improve task-specific performance within a multi-task setting is significant. The gains reported on code instruction-following and math-solving are substantial, indicating practical utility. The approach's training-free nature is a key advantage. However, the reliance on a calibration dataset introduces a potential sensitivity to the choice of that dataset, and the experiments should demonstrate robustness to different calibration dataset compositions. The paper makes the implicit assumption that the optimal layer sparsity configuration remains the same for the same type of tasks. This may not hold for more heterogeneous or complex tasks.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Motivation:** The paper clearly articulates the limitations of existing model merging techniques and motivates the need for a more targeted approach.
*   **Intuitive Approach:** The idea of using activation-based layer importance to guide sparsity is intuitive and makes sense.
*   **Empirical Validation:**  The experiments demonstrate performance improvements on realistic tasks. Using Pass@1 and Pass@10 for code tasks and FE/SM for math tasks provides a clear evaluation.
*   **Training-Free:** The method is training-free, making it computationally efficient.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Calibration Dataset Dependence:** The approach is highly dependent on the calibration dataset. The sensitivity to the choice of the calibration dataset is not thoroughly examined. How to best construct this dataset for good generalizability is unclear.
*   **Limited Scope:** The experiments are limited to code instruction-following and math-solving tasks. It is uncertain how well LEWIS will generalize to other domains or types of models (e.g., multimodal or non-transformer architectures).
*   **Sparsity Bound Selection:** The choice of sparsity bounds [γ, ε] is empirical, requiring some tuning. An automatic way to determine these bounds would strengthen the method.
*   **Comparison Baselines:** While TIES and DARE are reasonable baselines, including comparisons to other guided-merging methods that also optimize at layer- or parameter-level would enhance the evaluation. The paper would be stronger if it included a baseline with a calibration dataset, using it to simply find the best global sparsity.
*   **Algorithm Clarity:**  The Algorithm 1 notation could be clearer and more concise.

**Potential Impact:**

If LEWIS proves robust to various calibration datasets and generalizes to other tasks, it could become a valuable tool for creating high-performing multi-task models in a cost-effective manner.  The method can be viewed as a pre-processing step to improve other data-free model merging techniques, which would improve its significance and impact.

**Score:** 6

**Rationale:**

LEWIS presents a novel and potentially useful approach to model merging by incorporating layer-wise importance.  The empirical results are encouraging.  However, the limitations related to calibration dataset dependence, empirical parameter tuning, and limited scope of experiments restrict the paper's overall impact. If the approach can be made more robust and generalized, its significance would increase. The current presentation shows promising initial results but requires further investigation to assess its broader applicability and robustness.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 6/10

### Pretrained LLMs as Real-Time Controllers for Robot Operated Serial Production Line
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.03889v1)
- **Authors**: Muhammad Waseem, Kshitij Bhatta, Chen Li, Qing Chang
- **Abstract**: The manufacturing industry is undergoing a transformative shift, driven by cutting-edge technologies like 5G, AI, and cloud computing. Despite these advancements, effective system control, which is crucial for optimizing production efficiency, remains a complex challenge due to the intricate, knowledge-dependent nature of manufacturing processes and the reliance on domain-specific expertise. Conventional control methods often demand heavy customization, considerable computational resources, and lack transparency in decision-making. In this work, we investigate the feasibility of using Large Language Models (LLMs), particularly GPT-4, as a straightforward, adaptable solution for controlling manufacturing systems, specifically, mobile robot scheduling. We introduce an LLM-based control framework to assign mobile robots to different machines in robot assisted serial production lines, evaluating its performance in terms of system throughput. Our proposed framework outperforms traditional scheduling approaches such as First-Come-First-Served (FCFS), Shortest Processing Time (SPT), and Longest Processing Time (LPT). While it achieves performance that is on par with state-of-the-art methods like Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning (MARL), it offers a distinct advantage by delivering comparable throughput without the need for extensive retraining. These results suggest that the proposed LLM-based solution is well-suited for scenarios where technical expertise, computational resources, and financial investment are limited, while decision transparency and system scalability are critical concerns.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores the use of Pre-trained Large Language Models (LLMs), specifically GPT-4, as real-time controllers for robot-operated serial production lines.  The authors propose an LLM-based control framework that assigns mobile robots to different machines to optimize system throughput. This framework is compared against traditional scheduling methods (FCFS, SPT, LPT) and a Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning (MARL) approach.  The results suggest that the LLM-based solution performs competitively with MARL while outperforming traditional scheduling techniques. A key claimed advantage is the LLM's ability to deliver comparable throughput without requiring extensive retraining, making it suitable for scenarios with limited technical expertise and computational resources.  Furthermore, the LLM provides human-readable explanations for its decisions, enhancing transparency.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The paper introduces a novel application of LLMs in a real-time control setting within manufacturing. While LLMs have seen increasing use in various fields, their direct application as controllers for production lines, especially with an emphasis on explainability, is a relatively unexplored area.  The use of structured prompts to guide the LLM's decision-making process is a reasonable design choice. However, the individual components (LLMs, prompts, robot scheduling) are not individually novel. The novelty lies in their specific integration and evaluation.

*   **Significance:** The potential significance of this work lies in making complex control systems more accessible. The reliance on pre-trained LLMs reduces the need for specialized AI expertise and extensive training, potentially democratizing advanced control strategies. The focus on explainability is crucial for adoption in real-world industrial settings where transparency and trust are paramount. The paper touches on real-world constraints such as limited computational resources which makes the study valuable in the manufacturing industry.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem definition and motivation.
    *   Well-defined LLM-based control framework with a logical structure (input, processing, output layers).
    *   Comparison against relevant benchmark policies (FCFS, SPT, LPT, MARL).
    *   Empirical evaluation with two different production line configurations.
    *   Discussion of the strengths and weaknesses of the approach, including computational efficiency, explainability, and generalization capabilities.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Scope:** The evaluation is primarily based on simulations with synthetic data. While the data is claimed to be derived from real-world automobile industry data, the results may not fully translate to more complex and unpredictable real-world scenarios. A case study on an actual production line, even a simplified one, would significantly strengthen the findings.
    *   **Lack of Deep Dive into Prompts:** While the paper mentions the prompt engineering process, more details about the specific prompts used and the impact of different prompt designs on performance are needed. The effectiveness of the approach hinges on the quality and design of the prompts.
    *   **Explainability could be enhanced:** While LLM's provide human-readable explanations, the nature of those explanations could be further examined. Are they actually useful and informative to shop floor workers? How does the quality of the explanation compare to explanations generated by other explainable AI (XAI) methods applied to MARL, for example?
    *   **MARL Baseline:** The choice of MAPPO as the MARL baseline is reasonable, but the MARL results should be analyzed in more depth and with appropriate hyperparameter tuning.

*   **Potential Influence:** If validated with more extensive real-world experiments, this work could influence the adoption of AI-driven control systems in manufacturing by lowering the barriers to entry. The emphasis on explainability could also encourage the development of more transparent and trustworthy AI solutions.

*   **Score Rationale:**

The paper presents a promising approach with practical implications. However, the limited empirical validation and lack of in-depth prompt analysis hinder its potential impact. While the integration of LLMs into manufacturing control is novel, further research is needed to solidify its advantages and address its limitations. In addition, since MAPPO and MARL frameworks have been used more widely, it's expected to outperform the LLM framework, and more effort should be invested on refining the prompts to match performance with the best-in-class control system framework.

Score: 6.5

- **Classification**: cs.RO
- **Score**: 6/10

### On the Convergence of Adam-Type Algorithm for Bilevel Optimization under Unbounded Smoothness
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.03908v1)
- **Authors**: Xiaochuan Gong, Jie Hao, Mingrui Liu
- **Abstract**: Adam has become one of the most popular optimizers for training modern deep neural networks, such as transformers. However, its applicability is largely restricted to single-level optimization problems. In this paper, we aim to extend vanilla Adam to tackle bilevel optimization problems, which have important applications in machine learning, such as meta-learning. In particular, we study stochastic bilevel optimization problems where the lower-level function is strongly convex and the upper-level objective is nonconvex with potentially unbounded smoothness. This unbounded smooth objective function covers a broad class of neural networks, including transformers, which may exhibit non-Lipschitz gradients. In this work, we introduce AdamBO, a single-loop Adam-type method that achieves $\widetilde{O}(\epsilon^{-4})$ oracle complexity to find $\epsilon$-stationary points, where the oracle calls involve stochastic gradient or Hessian/Jacobian-vector product evaluations. The key to our analysis is a novel randomness decoupling lemma that provides refined control over the lower-level variable. We conduct extensive experiments on various machine learning tasks involving bilevel formulations with recurrent neural networks (RNNs) and transformers, demonstrating the effectiveness of our proposed Adam-type algorithm.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

This paper introduces AdamBO, an Adam-type algorithm for bilevel optimization problems under unbounded smoothness, a setting relevant for training modern deep neural networks like transformers. The authors tackle stochastic bilevel optimization where the lower-level function is strongly convex, and the upper-level objective is nonconvex with potentially unbounded smoothness. They demonstrate an O(ε⁻⁴) oracle complexity for finding ε-stationary points. A key contribution is a novel randomness decoupling lemma to control the lower-level variable. Experimental results on RNNs and transformers in meta-learning and deep AUC maximization tasks show the algorithm's effectiveness, especially outperforming baselines for transformers.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in extending Adam's applicability to bilevel optimization under unbounded smoothness. Prior works on Adam focused on single-level optimization or different smoothness assumptions. The introduction of the randomness decoupling lemma is a non-trivial contribution that allows for refined analysis of lower-level error and hypergradient bias in the context of adaptive optimization in bilevel problems.  This method may be transferable to other adaptive optimization algorithms used in bilevel optimization.

*   **Significance:** Bilevel optimization is crucial in various machine learning applications (meta-learning, hyperparameter optimization, continual learning), and Adam is a workhorse optimizer.  Therefore, a provably convergent Adam-type algorithm for bilevel optimization under a realistic smoothness condition is significant.  The experiments demonstrate empirical gains, particularly with transformers, further supporting the relevance of the work. The improved AUC in transformers and the demonstration of the algorithm's efficacy in meta-learning will interest researchers training these architectures.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   The theoretical analysis provides a convergence guarantee (O(ε⁻⁴) oracle complexity), a solid foundation for the algorithm.
    *   The randomness decoupling lemma seems technically sound and is of independent interest.
    *   Experiments on real-world tasks and models (RNNs and transformers) validate the algorithm's practical value.
    *   The paper clearly outlines the challenges in extending Adam to bilevel optimization and details how AdamBO addresses these challenges.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The complexity dependence on λ⁻² could be a limitation, as λ is typically small. This point is acknowledged by the authors. While empirical analysis somewhat mitigates this concern, a complexity bound with a better dependence on λ would strengthen the work.
    *   The reliance on a small momentum parameter (β = Θ(ε²)) in the theoretical analysis, compared to the standard value (0.9), requires justification, although the authors address this point.  The differences in training between practice and theory merit further exploration.
    *   The paper could benefit from a more detailed discussion of the limitations of the unbounded smoothness assumption itself. While relevant for certain networks, are there scenarios where it is unsuitable, and what are the implications?
    *   The experiments should provide a deeper dive into the hyperparameter sensitivity of AdamBO and the other compared algorithms and explore the potential for other bilevel optimizers (e.g. those using trust regions) to achieve similar results.

*   **Potential Impact:** The paper can influence research in bilevel optimization and adaptive optimization.  The randomness decoupling lemma and the algorithm's performance on transformers are valuable additions to the field. Future work might build on this by improving the complexity bounds, exploring different adaptive optimizers in the bilevel setting, and extending the analysis to other scenarios and machine learning problems. It offers a solid benchmark for future adaptive bilevel optimization methods.

**Score: 7.5**

**Justification:**

The paper makes a significant and novel contribution by extending Adam to bilevel optimization with unbounded smoothness and introducing the randomness decoupling lemma.  The experimental validation supports the algorithm's practical value, especially for transformers.  However, the somewhat weak dependency of the oracle complexity on λ, as well as other potential limitations in the theoretical setup, hold the score back from being higher. While the work is relevant and technically sound, a stronger theoretical justification and a broader exploration of experimental settings, coupled with a deeper analysis of the parameter sensitivity, would enhance its significance and impact, justifying a higher rating.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Safe LLM-Controlled Robots with Formal Guarantees via Reachability Analysis
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.03911v1)
- **Authors**: Ahmad Hafez, Alireza Naderi Akhormeh, Amr Hegazy, Amr Alanwar
- **Abstract**: The deployment of Large Language Models (LLMs) in robotic systems presents unique safety challenges, particularly in unpredictable environments. Although LLMs, leveraging zero-shot learning, enhance human-robot interaction and decision-making capabilities, their inherent probabilistic nature and lack of formal guarantees raise significant concerns for safety-critical applications. Traditional model-based verification approaches often rely on precise system models, which are difficult to obtain for real-world robotic systems and may not be fully trusted due to modeling inaccuracies, unmodeled dynamics, or environmental uncertainties. To address these challenges, this paper introduces a safety assurance framework for LLM-controlled robots based on data-driven reachability analysis, a formal verification technique that ensures all possible system trajectories remain within safe operational limits. Our framework specifically investigates the problem of instructing an LLM to navigate the robot to a specified goal and assesses its ability to generate low-level control actions that successfully guide the robot safely toward that goal. By leveraging historical data to construct reachable sets of states for the robot-LLM system, our approach provides rigorous safety guarantees against unsafe behaviors without relying on explicit analytical models. We validate the framework through experimental case studies in autonomous navigation and task planning, demonstrating its effectiveness in mitigating risks associated with LLM-generated commands. This work advances the integration of formal methods into LLM-based robotics, offering a principled and practical approach to ensuring safety in next-generation autonomous systems.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a safety assurance framework for robots controlled by Large Language Models (LLMs). It addresses the safety concerns arising from the probabilistic nature and lack of formal guarantees in LLM-generated commands. The framework utilizes data-driven reachability analysis, a formal verification technique, to ensure that all possible robot trajectories remain within safe operational limits.  The method uses historical data to build reachable sets, which are then checked for collisions, without relying on precise analytical models of the robot's dynamics.  The authors validate their framework through simulations and experiments in autonomous navigation and task planning, demonstrating its effectiveness in mitigating risks associated with LLM-generated commands.  The key contributions are a unified safety framework with zero-shot learning for LLM-controlled systems and data-driven reachability analysis to avoid reliance on inaccurate robot models.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of combining LLMs with data-driven reachability analysis for safety verification is relatively novel. While reachability analysis and LLM-based control have been studied separately, integrating them in this specific manner to provide formal safety guarantees is a valuable contribution. The incorporation of zero-shot learning is also a significant advantage, allowing generalization to unseen tasks.
*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial challenge in the deployment of LLM-controlled robots: safety. The probabilistic and sometimes unpredictable nature of LLMs necessitates robust safety assurance mechanisms. By providing formal guarantees, the framework presented in this paper could enable the use of LLMs in safety-critical applications, such as healthcare and autonomous driving, where they are currently underutilized due to safety concerns.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Addressing a timely problem:**  The paper directly tackles a key limitation of LLM-controlled robotics.
    *   **Data-driven approach:**  The use of data-driven reachability analysis reduces the dependence on potentially inaccurate robot models, increasing the robustness of the approach.
    *   **Formal guarantees:** The framework provides formal safety guarantees, which are essential for safety-critical applications.
    *   **Experimental validation:**  The framework is validated through simulations and experiments, demonstrating its practical utility.
    *   **Zero-shot learning:** The LLM can be applied to novel situations without retraining.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Assumptions:**  The framework makes several assumptions, such as the ability to sense obstacles and the availability of failsafe maneuvers. These assumptions may not hold in all real-world scenarios, limiting the applicability of the approach. It also assumes static obstacles, which might not be practical in dynamic scenarios.
    *   **Computational cost:** The computational cost of reachability analysis can be significant, especially for complex systems with high-dimensional state spaces and long planning horizons. While the paper claims that the method is computationally efficient and scalable, more details are needed to support this claim. Furthermore, experiments are limited to relatively simple systems, raising questions on scalability to much more complex robot systems.
    *   **Limited scope:** The paper focuses on safety, but does not address other important aspects of robot control, such as efficiency, optimality, and robustness to disturbances.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field of LLM-based robotics by providing a principled approach to ensuring safety. The framework presented in this paper could be extended to incorporate other aspects of robot control, such as efficiency and robustness.

**Justification for Score:**

Considering the novelty of the approach, the significance of the problem being addressed, and the limitations of the framework, a score of 7 is warranted. The paper makes a valuable contribution by integrating LLMs with reachability analysis to provide formal safety guarantees. However, the assumptions made by the framework and the computational cost of reachability analysis limit its applicability in real-world scenarios, impacting the score. Despite these limitations, the paper has the potential to influence the field of LLM-based robotics by providing a solid foundation for future research on safe LLM-controlled robots.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.RO
- **Score**: 7/10

### GuardDoor: Safeguarding Against Malicious Diffusion Editing via Protective Backdoors
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.03944v1)
- **Authors**: Yaopei Zeng, Yuanpu Cao, Lu Lin
- **Abstract**: The growing accessibility of diffusion models has revolutionized image editing but also raised significant concerns about unauthorized modifications, such as misinformation and plagiarism. Existing countermeasures largely rely on adversarial perturbations designed to disrupt diffusion model outputs. However, these approaches are found to be easily neutralized by simple image preprocessing techniques, such as compression and noise addition. To address this limitation, we propose GuardDoor, a novel and robust protection mechanism that fosters collaboration between image owners and model providers. Specifically, the model provider participating in the mechanism fine-tunes the image encoder to embed a protective backdoor, allowing image owners to request the attachment of imperceptible triggers to their images. When unauthorized users attempt to edit these protected images with this diffusion model, the model produces meaningless outputs, reducing the risk of malicious image editing. Our method demonstrates enhanced robustness against image preprocessing operations and is scalable for large-scale deployment. This work underscores the potential of cooperative frameworks between model providers and image owners to safeguard digital content in the era of generative AI.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "GuardDoor: Safeguarding Against Malicious Diffusion Editing via Protective Backdoors" proposes a novel defense mechanism against unauthorized image editing using diffusion models. The method involves collaboration between image owners and model providers. The model provider fine-tunes the image encoder of a diffusion model to embed a protective backdoor. Image owners can then request the model provider to embed imperceptible triggers into their images. If unauthorized users attempt to edit these protected images using the modified diffusion model, the backdoor is activated, resulting in the generation of meaningless outputs, thus preventing malicious editing. The authors claim that GuardDoor is more robust against common image preprocessing techniques than existing adversarial perturbation-based defenses and offers scalability for large-scale deployment. The method leverages the inherent noise introduced by a pre-trained Variational Autoencoder (VAE) as the protective trigger.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of a collaborative approach between image owners and model providers for safeguarding against malicious image editing is relatively novel.  Leveraging the characteristics of a VAE to create the trigger and integrating a backdoor into the diffusion model is also innovative. Previous defenses have primarily focused on image-only adversarial perturbations. This moves the protection mechanism to the model itself.
*   **Significance:**  The threat of unauthorized image editing using diffusion models is a growing concern.  The vulnerability of existing adversarial defenses to image preprocessing techniques significantly limits their practical applicability. GuardDoor's claim of enhanced robustness addresses a crucial weakness in the current landscape of image protection techniques. Also, it scales better in large-scale applications.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Robustness:** The paper provides experimental evidence suggesting that GuardDoor is more resistant to common image preprocessing attacks (e.g., Gaussian blur, JPEG compression, DiffPure, and IMPRESS) compared to existing defenses like PhotoGuard and EditShield.
    *   **Scalability:** The mechanism of backdoor is created once and then it can be applied to any new incoming requests of image protection, thus addressing the scalability concerns of prior arts.
    *   **Collaboration:** The collaborative setting between image providers and model owners is a novel idea.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Reliance on Model Provider Cooperation:** The core of GuardDoor relies entirely on the cooperation of the diffusion model provider. If the model provider is malicious or compromised, the entire protection scheme fails. This is a significant practical limitation as it may be difficult to incentivize or enforce cooperation in many real-world scenarios. There is a trust issue.
    *   **Security Against Adaptive Attacks:** The paper does not address the potential for adaptive attacks where an adversary might try to probe and reverse-engineer the backdoor trigger and its associated target output. While the content-specific nature of the trigger is mentioned, further analysis of its security against sophisticated adversaries is needed. The paper mentions the query based vulnerability, where people can send requests for images, and see if they get protected images.
    *   **Meaningless Outputs:** The solution produces a meaningless output if backdoor is triggered. In some cases, the user might not be able to identify that the image editing is unauthorized.
    *   **Utility Loss:** The fine-tuning process, even with a utility loss, can potentially affect the overall quality and functionality of the diffusion model for other tasks. The results in tables 5 and 6 show the change. This could make model providers less willing to implement it, unless there is a strong enough incentive.
    *   **Evaluation:** The experiments were conducted on CelebA and WikiArt datasets, but generalizability on other types of data is unknown. The experiments were also limited, the image encoder can be fine-tuned with the same decoder.

*   **Potential Impact:**
    If the issues of model provider cooperation and adaptive attacks are adequately addressed, GuardDoor could have a significant impact on securing digital content in the generative AI era. It could influence the design of future image protection mechanisms and promote the development of collaborative frameworks between content creators and AI service providers.
*   **Clarity:** The paper is well-written and clearly explains the GuardDoor mechanism. The figures and algorithms are also well designed and described.

**Justification for Score:**

Considering the novelty of the collaborative approach and the potential for enhanced robustness compared to existing methods, but also acknowledging the critical weakness of reliance on model provider cooperation and the limited security analysis against adaptive attacks, a score of 6 is appropriate. While the experimental results show promise, the practical applicability of GuardDoor is highly contingent on resolving the trust issue and demonstrating resilience to sophisticated adversarial strategies.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 6/10

### COARSE: Collaborative Pseudo-Labeling with Coarse Real Labels for Off-Road Semantic Segmentation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.03947v1)
- **Authors**: Aurelio Noca, Xianmei Lei, Jonathan Becktor, Jeffrey Edlund, Anna Sabel, Patrick Spieler, Curtis Padgett, Alexandre Alahi, Deegan Atha
- **Abstract**: Autonomous off-road navigation faces challenges due to diverse, unstructured environments, requiring robust perception with both geometric and semantic understanding. However, scarce densely labeled semantic data limits generalization across domains. Simulated data helps, but introduces domain adaptation issues. We propose COARSE, a semi-supervised domain adaptation framework for off-road semantic segmentation, leveraging sparse, coarse in-domain labels and densely labeled out-of-domain data. Using pretrained vision transformers, we bridge domain gaps with complementary pixel-level and patch-level decoders, enhanced by a collaborative pseudo-labeling strategy on unlabeled data. Evaluations on RUGD and Rellis-3D datasets show significant improvements of 9.7\% and 8.4\% respectively, versus only using coarse data. Tests on real-world off-road vehicle data in a multi-biome setting further demonstrate COARSE's applicability.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the COARSE paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces COARSE, a semi-supervised domain adaptation framework for off-road semantic segmentation. It addresses the challenges of limited densely labeled data in off-road environments by leveraging sparse, coarse in-domain labels and densely labeled out-of-domain data. The framework uses pre-trained vision transformers (DINOv2) with complementary pixel-level and patch-level decoders, enhanced by a collaborative pseudo-labeling strategy on unlabeled data. The key idea is to train two decoders (PixelDecoder and PatchDecoder) on different data distributions and generate pseudo-labels based on their disagreement, thus focusing on areas where the models are uncertain. Evaluations on RUGD and Rellis-3D datasets demonstrate improved performance compared to using only coarse data, and real-world tests further showcase its applicability.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in the specific combination of techniques rather than a fundamentally new concept. Collaborative pseudo-labeling, domain adaptation, and the use of vision transformers are individually established approaches. However, COARSE's contribution comes from the way it integrates these elements to address the specific challenges of off-road semantic segmentation with coarse labels. The use of complementary pixel-level and patch-level decoders trained on different data distributions to generate more reliable pseudo-labels is a relatively interesting and potentially impactful idea. It attempts to address the inherent ambiguity and noise in off-road image labeling.

*   **Significance:** Off-road autonomous navigation is a growing field with significant applications. The scarcity of densely labeled data is a major bottleneck, and the paper tackles this problem head-on. Demonstrating a significant improvement in segmentation performance using coarse labels is practically valuable. The multi-biome real-world dataset and the real-world experiments, although briefly described, add credibility to the approach. The improvement of 9.7% and 8.4% on RUGD and Rellis-3D respectively is a noticeable result, especially considering the data limitations.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   The method addresses a relevant and pressing problem in off-road autonomy.
    *   The use of pre-trained DINOv2 leverages strong existing representations.
    *   The collaborative pseudo-labeling strategy is well-motivated and potentially more robust than single-model approaches.
    *   Experiments are conducted on standard datasets (RUGD, Rellis-3D) and real-world data, increasing the credibility of the results.
    *   The paper is well-written and structured, clearly explaining the approach and results.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The novelty of the individual components is limited. It relies on combining existing techniques.
    *   While the description of the pseudo-label generation strategy is clear, more ablation experiments could better elucidate the impact of each design choice (e.g., the specific architectures of decoders, the data pairings, the disagreement strategy).
    *   The description of real-world experiments is brief. More details on the data collection process, the "cut-and-paste" procedure, and the results would strengthen the paper.
    *   Although the models perform significantly better when using both coarse labels and a collaborative approach, the paper offers limited insights as to *why* the combined method outperforms simply adding dense, out-of-domain labels (e.g. a more detailed error analysis).
    *   No evaluation of uncertainty, and potential use cases, is explored.

*   **Potential Influence:**

    The paper has the potential to influence the development of more data-efficient methods for semantic segmentation in challenging outdoor environments. It can inspire further research on combining different levels of supervision (coarse and dense) and leveraging domain adaptation techniques to overcome data limitations.

**Score: 6.5**

**Justification:**

The score reflects the paper's solid contribution within a well-established area. While the paper's innovation is incremental, the specific combination of techniques to tackle the problem of off-road semantic segmentation with coarse labels shows good engineering intuition. The experimental results are convincing, and the paper addresses a practical need in an important domain. However, the paper could be improved with more ablation studies, deeper insights into the reasons behind the performance gains, and more detailed real-world evaluation. The combination of domain adaptation and semi-supervised learning in this scenario is not completely groundbreaking, but the particular implementation, evaluation, and positive results justify a score within the middle range. Its significance lies in presenting a practical solution that is effective, rather than in making radical new theoretical breakthroughs.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 6/10

### Performance Comparison of Large Language Models on Advanced Calculus Problems
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.03960v1)
- **Authors**: In Hak Moon
- **Abstract**: This paper presents an in-depth analysis of the performance of seven different Large Language Models (LLMs) in solving a diverse set of math advanced calculus problems. The study aims to evaluate these models' accuracy, reliability, and problem-solving capabilities, including ChatGPT 4o, Gemini Advanced with 1.5 Pro, Copilot Pro, Claude 3.5 Sonnet, Meta AI, Mistral AI, and Perplexity. The assessment was conducted through a series of thirty-two test problems, encompassing a total of 320 points. The problems covered various topics, from vector calculations and geometric interpretations to integral evaluations and optimization tasks. The results highlight significant trends and patterns in the models' performance, revealing both their strengths and weaknesses - for instance, models like ChatGPT 4o and Mistral AI demonstrated consistent accuracy across various problem types, indicating their robustness and reliability in mathematical problem-solving, while models such as Gemini Advanced with 1.5 Pro and Meta AI exhibited specific weaknesses, particularly in complex problems involving integrals and optimization, suggesting areas for targeted improvements. The study also underscores the importance of re-prompting in achieving accurate solutions, as seen in several instances where models initially provided incorrect answers but corrected them upon re-prompting. Overall, this research provides valuable insights into the current capabilities and limitations of LLMs in the domain of math calculus, with the detailed analysis of each model's performance on specific problems offering a comprehensive understanding of their strengths and areas for improvement, contributing to the ongoing development and refinement of LLM technology. The findings are particularly relevant for educators, researchers, and developers seeking to leverage LLMs for educational and practical applications in mathematics.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents a performance comparison of seven Large Language Models (LLMs) – ChatGPT 4o, Gemini Advanced with 1.5 Pro, Copilot Pro, Claude 3.5 Sonnet, Meta AI, Mistral AI, and Perplexity – on a set of 32 advanced calculus problems covering vector calculus, integral calculus, and optimization. The study evaluates the accuracy and step-by-step explanation capabilities of each model, and also examines the effectiveness of re-prompting to correct initial errors. The results highlight the strengths and weaknesses of each model in different areas of calculus. ChatGPT 4o and Mistral AI demonstrated the highest overall accuracy, while some models struggled with specific types of problems, such as complex integrals or optimization tasks. The paper emphasizes the importance of re-prompting and provides detailed analysis to contribute to the development and refinement of LLM technology for educational and practical applications in mathematics.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The paper's novelty is somewhat limited. While performance comparisons of LLMs are valuable, the specific problem domain (advanced calculus) and the chosen set of LLMs might not represent a groundbreaking advancement. Several studies already explore LLMs' capabilities in mathematics. The novelty lies primarily in the detailed problem-specific analysis and the comparison of *this specific set* of seven current LLMs on *these particular* calculus problems. The investigation of re-prompting offers incremental novelty.

**Significance:**

The paper contributes to the growing body of research evaluating LLMs in specialized domains. The in-depth analysis of each model's performance on specific problems offers practical insights for educators, researchers, and developers seeking to leverage LLMs in mathematics education and problem-solving. Identifying strengths and weaknesses of different models can inform targeted improvements in LLM algorithms and the development of effective AI-assisted learning tools. The emphasis on re-prompting highlights a practical strategy for improving LLM accuracy in challenging problems. However, the broader significance is somewhat constrained by the specific problem domain and the fact that LLM technology evolves rapidly. The results might become less relevant as models improve and new models emerge.

**Strengths:**

*   **Detailed Problem-Specific Analysis:** The paper provides a comprehensive breakdown of each model's performance on individual problems, revealing nuanced strengths and weaknesses.
*   **Focus on Re-Prompting:** The emphasis on the re-prompting mechanism and its impact on accuracy is a valuable contribution.
*   **Practical Insights:** The findings offer practical insights for educators, researchers, and developers.
*   **Clearly Defined Methodology:** The assessment structure and evaluation criteria are well-defined.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Novelty:** The study primarily offers a performance comparison within a well-trodden field.
*   **Limited Generalizability:** The rapid evolution of LLMs limits the long-term relevance of the specific performance figures.
*   **Selection of LLMs:** While the chosen LLMs are popular, there may be other relevant models not included.
*   **Problem Set Design:** The selected problems, while diverse, may not be fully representative of all advanced calculus topics, impacting the generalizability of the findings. The study provides no clear justification for why *these specific* 32 problems were chosen.
* The paper does not explicitly address the cost implications of using different LLMs. Certain models could be significantly more expensive to use than others, especially with re-prompting, and the paper lacks a discussion of the trade-offs between cost, accuracy, and speed.

**Justification for Score:**

Given the incremental nature of the novelty and the limitations in generalizability, the paper deserves a score of **6**. While the detailed analysis and practical insights are valuable, the study doesn't represent a breakthrough advancement in the field. It is a solid, well-executed performance evaluation that adds to our understanding of current LLMs in mathematics, but the lack of substantial novelty and the rapidly evolving landscape of LLMs limit its overall impact. Also, the cost of using different LLMs and the implications thereof is missing.

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### A Little Depth Goes a Long Way: The Expressive Power of Log-Depth Transformers
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.03961v1)
- **Authors**: William Merrill, Ashish Sabharwal
- **Abstract**: Recent theoretical results show transformers cannot express sequential reasoning problems over long input lengths, intuitively because their computational depth is bounded. However, prior work treats the depth as a constant, leaving it unclear to what degree bounded depth may suffice for solving problems over short inputs, or how increasing the transformer's depth affects its expressive power. We address these questions by analyzing the expressive power of transformers whose depth can grow minimally with context length $n$. We show even highly uniform transformers with depth $\Theta(\log n)$ can express two important problems: recognizing regular languages, which captures state tracking abilities, and graph connectivity, which underlies multi-step reasoning. Notably, both of these problems cannot be expressed by fixed-depth transformers under standard complexity conjectures, demonstrating the expressivity benefit of growing depth. Moreover, our theory quantitatively predicts how depth must grow with input length to express these problems, showing that depth scaling is more efficient than scaling width or chain-of-thought steps. Empirically, we find our theoretical depth requirements for regular language recognition match the practical depth requirements of transformers remarkably well. Thus, our results clarify precisely how depth affects transformers' reasoning capabilities, providing potential practical insights for designing models that are better at sequential reasoning.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "A Little Depth Goes a Long Way: The Expressive Power of Log-Depth Transformers" investigates the expressive power of transformers with a depth that grows logarithmically with the input sequence length (O(log n)). It demonstrates theoretically that such log-depth transformers can express problems like recognizing regular languages and graph connectivity, which are known to be beyond the capabilities of fixed-depth transformers. The paper contrasts the efficiency of scaling depth with scaling width and chain-of-thought steps, showing that depth scaling offers a more efficient way to tackle these reasoning problems.  Empirical validation provides support for the theoretical findings, demonstrating a correlation between depth/width and the effective context length.  The paper also highlights the potential practical implications of their findings for designing more efficient and capable transformers, particularly for long-context reasoning.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in precisely quantifying the impact of even a *minimal* increase in transformer depth (logarithmic) on expressive power. While it was known that fixed-depth transformers have limitations, this work establishes how much depth is needed to overcome those limitations for specific problems.  The comparison to scaling width and chain-of-thought is also a significant contribution, placing depth scaling in context within a wider design space. The use of "universal" or looped transformers to analyze dynamic depth offers a clever analytical framework, even if such architectures aren't necessarily standard.  Prior work had also established some of these theoretical results, so some of the content is an elaboration or refinement of existing knowledge.

*   **Significance:** The results have both theoretical and practical significance. Theoretically, the paper refines our understanding of the computational limits of transformers and the role of depth as a resource. Practically, it provides insights into model design, suggesting that even modestly increasing depth (logarithmically) can lead to substantial improvements in reasoning capabilities for problems that demand sequential processing or state tracking. The suggestion of dynamic depth as an inference-time strategy is relevant given the current focus on long-context LLMs. The paper bridges theory and practice quite effectively, offering experimentally-supported coefficients relating depth/width to context length.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Rigorous theoretical analysis with clear proofs.
    *   Relevant and well-chosen example problems (regular language recognition and graph connectivity).
    *   Quantitative comparison of depth, width, and chain-of-thought scaling.
    *   Empirical validation supports the theoretical predictions.
    *   Practical implications for transformer design are well-articulated.
    *   The analysis with respect to different complexity classes gives a deeper understanding.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The specific transformer architecture used ("universal" transformers) might not directly translate to implementations of standard transformer architectures. While this is a deliberate analytical choice, it's a simplification of real-world transformers.
    *   The experimental validation focuses primarily on a single synthetic task (A5 state tracking). While this provides controlled conditions, it would be stronger to include real-world language tasks to show similar trends occur in a more applied setting.
    *   The paper presents a somewhat simplified view of "depth" with the "universal" or looped architecture. Depth in a standard transformer architecture may have distinct effects due to non-uniform layer design, residual connections, and other architectural complexities.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper could influence the design of future transformer architectures, especially those targeting long-context reasoning tasks. It provides a solid theoretical basis for exploring dynamic depth and informs the trade-offs between depth, width, and inference-time compute.  The practical findings regarding the relationship between depth/width and context length could be useful for practitioners.

Score: 7

**Rationale:** The paper offers a significant contribution by rigorously analyzing the impact of log-depth scaling on the expressive power of transformers and comparing it to alternative scaling methods. Its strong theoretical analysis, empirical validation, and clear practical implications position it as an important work in the field. However, the analysis uses simplified architectures and tasks, limiting the practical impact in real-world LLM development, which has more complex architectures with many parameters. These limitations prevent it from receiving a higher score.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Generative Learning of Densities on Manifolds
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.03963v1)
- **Authors**: Dimitris G. Giovanis, Ellis Crabtree, Roger G. Ghanem, Ioannis G. kevrekidis
- **Abstract**: A generative modeling framework is proposed that combines diffusion models and manifold learning to efficiently sample data densities on manifolds. The approach utilizes Diffusion Maps to uncover possible low-dimensional underlying (latent) spaces in the high-dimensional data (ambient) space. Two approaches for sampling from the latent data density are described. The first is a score-based diffusion model, which is trained to map a standard normal distribution to the latent data distribution using a neural network. The second one involves solving an It\^o stochastic differential equation in the latent space. Additional realizations of the data are generated by lifting the samples back to the ambient space using Double Diffusion Maps, a recently introduced technique typically employed in studying dynamical system reduction; here the focus lies in sampling densities rather than system dynamics. The proposed approaches enable sampling high dimensional data densities restricted to low-dimensional, a priori unknown manifolds. The efficacy of the proposed framework is demonstrated through a benchmark problem and a material with multiscale structure.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper proposes a generative modeling framework that combines diffusion models and manifold learning techniques to efficiently sample data densities constrained to low-dimensional manifolds embedded within a high-dimensional space. The framework uses Diffusion Maps to discover the latent space representing the manifold. Two approaches are then employed to sample from the data density in the latent space: (1) a score-based diffusion model trained using a neural network, and (2) solving an Itô stochastic differential equation. Finally, Double Diffusion Maps are used to lift the generated samples from the latent space back to the original high-dimensional ambient space, ensuring the generated data respects the manifold structure. The efficacy of the framework is demonstrated on a synthetic S-shaped manifold and a composite material dataset.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in the integration of Double Diffusion Maps with modern generative models (diffusion models) to address the challenge of sampling from densities with underlying manifold structures. While diffusion models and manifold learning techniques exist independently, their combined application, especially with the double diffusion maps framework for "lifting," is a novel contribution. Prior work like PLOM also address manifold constrained sampling, but the use of modern score-based diffusion in the latent space is a newer approach.

*   **Significance:** The significance stems from the ability to efficiently generate data representative of complex systems where data lies on a lower-dimensional manifold. This is relevant to various scientific and engineering domains where datasets are limited and the underlying data structure is critical. The ability to generate more realistic samples respecting the manifold constraint can improve simulation accuracy and allow for better understanding of complex phenomena. The presented examples, particularly the composite material modeling, highlight the practical relevance. The reduction of complexity by performing sampling in the latent space is beneficial.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Statement:** The paper clearly identifies the limitations of traditional generative models when dealing with data with underlying manifold structure.
    *   **Well-Defined Methodology:** The proposed framework is presented in a structured manner with clear steps and algorithms.
    *   **Demonstrated Efficacy:** The examples provided demonstrate the performance improvements over standard generative models by visually comparing the generated samples and quantitatively comparing marginal densities.
    *   **Comprehensive approach:** Use of diffusion maps for dimensionality reduction and use of Geometric harmonics for the "lifting" stage allow for a more complete modelling.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Computational cost comparison to other manifold sampling methods:** It is difficult to fully compare the effectiveness of the method without a computational cost analysis, or a comparison against other manifold-based sampling methods. This is especially important for the m-SGM2 which relies on learning the SDE through a neural network.
    *   **Limited Datasets for Training Latent Density:** While manifold learning helps address this to some extent, reliance on supervised training of diffusion models in the latent space still requires sufficient data to learn the distribution well.
    *   **Reliance on Diffusion Map Quality:** The success of the framework hinges on the accurate identification of the manifold and the generation of a good latent space representation by Diffusion Maps. If the initial manifold learning stage is inaccurate, the subsequent steps will suffer. The robustness to suboptimal Diffusion Map results is not extensively investigated.
    *   **Parameter Sensitivity:** The hyperparameter sensitivity of Diffusion Maps and the various diffusion model parameters may pose a challenge for practical implementation.
    *   **Results only visually corroborated:** Quantitative comparisons should be provided. Specifically it is easy to make the generated data look better by choosing appropriate hyperparameters.
    *   **Clarity for "Lifting" stage**: The methods for "lifting" from the latent space back to the full space could be more clearly and precisely explained.

*   **Potential Impact:**

    *   The paper could inspire further research into combining manifold learning and generative modeling for more realistic data generation in scientific and engineering applications.
    *   The approach could be adopted for applications where limited datasets and the preservation of data geometry are crucial.
    *   The framework could serve as a basis for developing more efficient and robust generative models for high-dimensional data.

*   **Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel and potentially useful framework for sampling data constrained to manifolds. However, the limited computational cost comparison, requirement for robust initial manifold discovery, possible parameter sensitivity, and lack of extensive evaluation temper its impact at this stage. Specifically, while it presents a new method, the comparison to baseline methods is almost entirely qualitative, and it fails to address what situations are required for this method to be most helpful. Given these caveats, it's difficult to give this method a high score until a computational analysis is done.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 6/10

### All-atom Diffusion Transformers: Unified generative modelling of molecules and materials
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.03965v1)
- **Authors**: Chaitanya K. Joshi, Xiang Fu, Yi-Lun Liao, Vahe Gharakhanyan, Benjamin Kurt Miller, Anuroop Sriram, Zachary W. Ulissi
- **Abstract**: Diffusion models are the standard toolkit for generative modelling of 3D atomic systems. However, for different types of atomic systems - such as molecules and materials - the generative processes are usually highly specific to the target system despite the underlying physics being the same. We introduce the All-atom Diffusion Transformer (ADiT), a unified latent diffusion framework for jointly generating both periodic materials and non-periodic molecular systems using the same model: (1) An autoencoder maps a unified, all-atom representations of molecules and materials to a shared latent embedding space; and (2) A diffusion model is trained to generate new latent embeddings that the autoencoder can decode to sample new molecules or materials. Experiments on QM9 and MP20 datasets demonstrate that jointly trained ADiT generates realistic and valid molecules as well as materials, exceeding state-of-the-art results from molecule and crystal-specific models. ADiT uses standard Transformers for both the autoencoder and diffusion model, resulting in significant speedups during training and inference compared to equivariant diffusion models. Scaling ADiT up to half a billion parameters predictably improves performance, representing a step towards broadly generalizable foundation models for generative chemistry. Open source code: https://github.com/facebookresearch/all-atom-diffusion-transformer
- **Summary**: Okay, I've reviewed the paper and can provide a summary and critical evaluation.

**Summary:**

The paper introduces All-atom Diffusion Transformers (ADiT), a unified latent diffusion framework for generative modeling of both periodic materials (crystals) and non-periodic molecular systems. ADiT consists of a variational autoencoder (VAE) that maps all-atom representations of molecules and materials into a shared latent space, and a diffusion transformer (DiT) that generates new latent embeddings, which are then decoded by the VAE to sample new molecules or materials. The authors demonstrate that ADiT, when trained jointly on QM9 and MP20 datasets, achieves state-of-the-art results for generating realistic and valid molecules and materials, surpassing specialized models. They highlight the scalability and efficiency of ADiT due to its use of standard Transformers and demonstrate that performance improves with model size. They also present promising initial results for generating metal-organic frameworks (MOFs).

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**

    *   *Positive:* The core novelty lies in the **unified framework** for generating both molecules and materials using a single model. While diffusion models are well-established for both tasks individually, the idea of a shared latent space and a single generative model that handles periodicity and aperiodicity is a significant step forward.
    *   *Positive:* Using **standard Transformers** for both the autoencoder and diffusion model contributes novelty. Previous approaches for these tasks often use graph neural networks with equivariance to rotations and translations. This approach allows to more easily scale these models.
    *   *Negative:* While novel, the individual components (VAE, DiT, Transformers) are not groundbreaking on their own. The contribution is primarily in the integration and application to this specific problem.

*   **Significance:**

    *   *Positive:* Achieving **state-of-the-art performance** on established benchmarks (QM9, MP20) is a strong indicator of the method's potential. The significant improvements over prior methods on some validity metrics shows it's not just "me too" performance.
    *   *Positive:* The **demonstrated scalability and efficiency** compared to equivariant diffusion models is crucial. The speedups and scaling properties make ADiT more practically useful, especially for large-scale generative tasks.
    *   *Positive:* Showing transfer learning from molecules to materials (and vice-versa) is an indication of a more generalizable and potentially powerful model.
    *   *Negative:* The results on MOFs, while promising, are preliminary. The decrease in validity rates with joint training needs further investigation and improvement.
    *   *Negative:* The paper could more rigorously compare the strengths and weaknesses of using transformers for molecular generation compared to other approaches.

*   **Impact and Potential Influence:**

    *   *Positive:* The paper has the potential to influence the field by advocating for more generalizable and scalable generative models for chemical systems. The unified framework could inspire new architectures and training strategies.
    *   *Positive:* The demonstration of transfer learning could pave the way for models that leverage data from multiple domains, addressing the data scarcity problem in certain areas of materials science and drug discovery.
    *   *Negative:* The current limitations with MOFs and lack of conditional generation capabilities temper the immediate impact. Further research is needed to fully realize the potential.

*   **Rigorousness:**

    *   *Positive:* The evaluation metrics used are standard and well-defined. The comparisons to baselines are fair, and the ablation studies provide valuable insights into the model's behavior.

*   **Weakness:**

    * It is a bit unclear what representation is used for materials in the latent space (fractional coordinates and lattice parameters or cartesian coordinates).
    * While the experiments are well-designed, the dataset size should be bigger.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a significant step toward unified generative modeling of molecules and materials. The core idea of a shared latent space and a single generative model is compelling, and the demonstrated performance improvements and scalability are noteworthy. However, the reliance on existing building blocks (VAE, DiT, Transformers), the preliminary MOF results, and lack of conditional generation somewhat limit the overall impact.

The main reason for the low score is that some important information is missing. Is the approach scalable to bigger and more complex materials? What are the limitation of the approach?

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 6/10

### Model Behavior Specification by Leveraging LLM Self-Playing and Self-Improving
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.03967v1)
- **Authors**: Soya Park, J. D. Zamfirescu-Pereira, Chinmay Kulkarni
- **Abstract**: Training AI models is challenging, particularly when crafting behavior instructions. Traditional methods rely on machines (supervised learning) or manual pattern discovery, which results in not interpretable models or time sink. While Large Language Models (LLMs) simplify instruction writing through natural language, articulating intended model behavior still remains difficult. We introduce Visionary Tuning, a human-in-the-loop self-playing followed by automatic self-refinement to improve behavior specification. Our system helps users clarify desired behavior through self-playing and generates prompts through self-improving, Our first evaluation involves user study conducted on a system implementation of Visionary Tuning within the context of chatbot behavior. Our system self-play itself by simulating user interactions to identify patterns and create effective prompts based on the pattern. In a within-subject study (N=12), participants pinpointed more patterns through self-playing and crafted better prompts. Surprisingly, users felt more or less success level in specifying the model behavior. Follow-up crowd studies (N=60) confirmed that the chatbot adhered to instructions without sacrificing quality. Our second evaluation is a case study on a real-world implementation using a movie rating dataset with Visionary Tuning, demonstrating its effectiveness and robustness in modeling a critic's preferences across the spectrum of low to highly rated movies. Together, these results suggest how AI improves the design process of interactive AI systems. Furthermore, they suggest how the benefits of these tools may be non-obvious to end-users. We reflect on these findings and suggest future directions.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Model Behavior Specification by Leveraging LLM Self-Playing and Self-Improving":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Visionary Tuning, a novel human-in-the-loop method for improving LLM instruction writing. Visionary Tuning leverages LLM self-playing, where a target model interacts with itself under developer supervision, to surface diverse criteria and edge cases. User feedback is then used in a self-improving phase where the LLM rewrites the prompt. The authors evaluate their system, VISIONFORGE, through a user study and a case study demonstrating its effectiveness in improving prompt quality, particularly for tasks like negative behavior specification (avoiding certain topics). While users didn't always perceive a direct benefit, quantitative results suggest the system produces more robust and reliable prompts.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of using LLMs to *facilitate* prompt engineering via self-play and user feedback is moderately novel. While self-play and automated prompt refinement have been explored, the explicit human-in-the-loop component focused on surfacing *edge cases* and the focus on *negative* behavior specification is a differentiator. However, the individual components (self-play, LLM prompt refinement) are not entirely new.

*   **Significance:** The work has potential significance for the field of AI interaction design and prompt engineering.  Making prompt engineering more accessible and less reliant on expert intuition is important.  The focus on negative behavior specification is particularly valuable as safety and controllability become increasingly important for LLMs. However, several aspects limit the significance:

    *   **Limited user perception of benefit:** The discrepancy between quantitative results (improved prompt robustness) and user perception (no significant difference) raises concerns about the practical value and user experience of Visionary Tuning. If users don't *feel* the improvement, adoption is unlikely.
    *   **Specific tasks:** The evaluation focuses on relatively narrow tasks (poetry critique, graduate counseling, movie rating). It's unclear how well Visionary Tuning generalizes to more complex or open-ended domains.
    *   **Model dependence:** The system relies on the instruction-following capabilities of a specific LLM. While the authors claim model-agnosticism, the prompts and process might need substantial adaptation for other LLMs.
    *   **Lack of theoretical justification:** The paper lacks a strong theoretical underpinning for why Visionary Tuning works.  The intuition behind surfacing edge cases through self-play is reasonable, but more formal justification or analysis would strengthen the claims.
    *   **Evaluation limitations:** The user study's relatively small sample size (N=12 in the main study), while justified by best practices in social computing, limits the generalizability of the findings. Also, external validation of user generated chatbots are conducted using crowdworkers which may lead to different results compare with target population.
    *   **Competitive Landscape**: More recently, techniques such as Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback (RLHF) have become prominent in enhancing the alignment of language models with human preferences. The paper's effectiveness relative to these advanced techniques, which have become the industry standard, remains unclear.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Statement:** The paper clearly articulates the challenges of prompt engineering, particularly for complex behaviors and safety concerns.
    *   **Well-Defined Method:** Visionary Tuning is a well-defined, implementable method.
    *   **Empirical Evaluation:** The user study and case study provide empirical evidence for the system's effectiveness.
    *   **Code availability:** Code availability enhances reproducibility.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Discrepancy between quantitative and qualitative results:** The lack of user-perceived benefit is a major concern.
    *   **Limited Generalizability:** The tasks and models used in the evaluation limit the generalizability of the findings.
    *   **Lack of Theoretical Justification:** The paper lacks a strong theoretical underpinning.
    *   **The system's ability to reduce anti-behaviors that violate the base LLM alignment tuning:**  This limits the system's application in certain use cases.

*   **Potential Influence:**  The paper could influence future research on human-AI collaborative prompt engineering and the development of tools for specifying LLM behavior, especially if the user experience and generalizability are improved.

**Justification of Score:**

The score reflects the paper's moderate novelty, promising but limited significance, and identified weaknesses. Visionary Tuning is a sensible approach with some empirical support, but the lack of strong user-perceived benefit, the limited generalizability of the evaluation, and the dependence on specific LLM capabilities constrain its overall impact. The contribution is incremental rather than transformative.
