- **Score**: 7/10

### Uncovering inequalities in new knowledge learning by large language models across different languages
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.04064v1)
- **Authors**: Chenglong Wang, Haoyu Tang, Xiyuan Yang, Yueqi Xie, Jina Suh, Sunayana Sitaram, Junming Huang, Yu Xie, Zhaoya Gong, Xing Xie, Fangzhao Wu
- **Abstract**: As large language models (LLMs) gradually become integral tools for problem solving in daily life worldwide, understanding linguistic inequality is becoming increasingly important. Existing research has primarily focused on static analyses that assess the disparities in the existing knowledge and capabilities of LLMs across languages. However, LLMs are continuously evolving, acquiring new knowledge to generate up-to-date, domain-specific responses. Investigating linguistic inequalities within this dynamic process is, therefore, also essential. In this paper, we explore inequalities in new knowledge learning by LLMs across different languages and four key dimensions: effectiveness, transferability, prioritization, and robustness. Through extensive experiments under two settings (in-context learning and fine-tuning) using both proprietary and open-source models, we demonstrate that low-resource languages consistently face disadvantages across all four dimensions. By shedding light on these disparities, we aim to raise awareness of linguistic inequalities in LLMs' new knowledge learning, fostering the development of more inclusive and equitable future LLMs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Uncovering inequalities in new knowledge learning by large language models across different languages":

**Summary:**

The paper investigates inequalities in how large language models (LLMs) learn new knowledge across different languages. The study focuses on four key dimensions: effectiveness, transferability, prioritization (when knowledge conflicts), and robustness (resistance to incorrect information). The authors conduct experiments using both proprietary (GPT-4o-mini) and open-source (Llama-3.1-8B) models, utilizing both in-context learning and fine-tuning approaches. To address the challenge of knowing what the LLMs have already learned, they create a novel dataset of "fictional" new knowledge and a common-sense dataset.  The central finding is that LLMs consistently exhibit disparities in their ability to learn, transfer, prioritize, and resist errors across languages, with low-resource languages consistently facing disadvantages.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** While the importance of multilingualism in LLMs is increasingly recognized, the paper distinguishes itself by focusing on the *dynamic* process of new knowledge acquisition, rather than simply analyzing pre-existing knowledge or static capabilities. This is a valuable shift in perspective. The creation of a "fictional" knowledge dataset is a clever workaround to the problem of unknown pre-training data, enhancing the novelty of the empirical setup.  However, the use of machine translation (Google Translate) to create parallel datasets, while common, introduces potential artifacts that could influence results.  The dimensions of analysis (effectiveness, transferability, prioritization, robustness) are logical and comprehensive, providing a structured way to examine the problem.

*   **Significance:** The findings have important implications for fairness and accessibility in LLMs.  The paper highlights that reliance on these models can exacerbate existing linguistic inequalities, potentially marginalizing users of low-resource languages.  The paper calls for developers to allocate additional resources to improve LLM performance in low-resource languages and raises crucial ethical considerations around perpetuating linguistic hegemony. This contributes to the growing discussion around responsible AI development. The practical implications for developers in terms of allocating resources and designing better training strategies are concrete. The acknowledgement of limitations (limited set of models/datasets, reliance on fictional knowledge) strengthens the paper's credibility.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clear problem definition and research questions.
    *   Well-designed methodology with a novel approach to controlling for pre-existing knowledge.
    *   Comprehensive analysis across multiple dimensions.
    *   Use of both proprietary and open-source models for robustness.
    *   Discussion of ethical implications and actionable recommendations.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   Reliance on machine translation for dataset creation introduces potential biases and noise. While they tried to mitigate it with back-translation, it isn't a perfect solution.
    *   The use of "fictional" knowledge, while necessary, raises questions about the generalizability of the findings to real-world scenarios. How the LLM behaves with fictional knowledge may not be indicative of its behaviour with real world factual knowledge that exists to some degree.
    *   While multiple languages are considered, the number of models is low, limiting the scope of findings.
    *   The paper mainly highlights the problem but doesn't propose or evaluate solutions to mitigate the identified inequalities.

*   **Overall Impact:** The paper serves as an important reminder of the potential for LLMs to amplify existing social biases and contributes to a more nuanced understanding of multilingual AI. It's likely to inspire further research in this area, encouraging the development of more equitable and inclusive LLMs. The creation of the datasets, even with the caveats mentioned, offers a valuable resource for future studies. It is a strong paper contributing to the very important discussions around equity and inclusivity in large language models.

**Score: 7.5**

**Justification:** This paper makes a solid contribution to the field by shifting the focus to the dynamic aspect of knowledge acquisition in multilingual LLMs.  The research is well-executed and highlights important ethical concerns.  The strong methodological elements (especially the fictional knowledge dataset) are valuable. However, the limitations around machine translation, generalization from fictional knowledge, and lack of proposed solutions prevent a higher score. While the findings are significant, the paper is primarily diagnostic in nature and needs to be followed up by research offering concrete mitigations.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### FREAK: Frequency-modulated High-fidelity and Real-time Audio-driven Talking Portrait Synthesis
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.04067v1)
- **Authors**: Ziqi Ni, Ao Fu, Yi Zhou
- **Abstract**: Achieving high-fidelity lip-speech synchronization in audio-driven talking portrait synthesis remains challenging. While multi-stage pipelines or diffusion models yield high-quality results, they suffer from high computational costs. Some approaches perform well on specific individuals with low resources, yet still exhibit mismatched lip movements. The aforementioned methods are modeled in the pixel domain. We observed that there are noticeable discrepancies in the frequency domain between the synthesized talking videos and natural videos. Currently, no research on talking portrait synthesis has considered this aspect. To address this, we propose a FREquency-modulated, high-fidelity, and real-time Audio-driven talKing portrait synthesis framework, named FREAK, which models talking portraits from the frequency domain perspective, enhancing the fidelity and naturalness of the synthesized portraits. FREAK introduces two novel frequency-based modules: 1) the Visual Encoding Frequency Modulator (VEFM) to couple multi-scale visual features in the frequency domain, better preserving visual frequency information and reducing the gap in the frequency spectrum between synthesized and natural frames. and 2) the Audio Visual Frequency Modulator (AVFM) to help the model learn the talking pattern in the frequency domain and improve audio-visual synchronization. Additionally, we optimize the model in both pixel domain and frequency domain jointly. Furthermore, FREAK supports seamless switching between one-shot and video dubbing settings, offering enhanced flexibility. Due to its superior performance, it can simultaneously support high-resolution video results and real-time inference. Extensive experiments demonstrate that our method synthesizes high-fidelity talking portraits with detailed facial textures and precise lip synchronization in real-time, outperforming state-of-the-art methods.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents FREAK, a novel framework for audio-driven talking portrait synthesis. FREAK operates in the frequency domain, aiming to improve fidelity and lip-sync accuracy while maintaining real-time performance.  It introduces two key modules: the Visual Encoding Frequency Modulator (VEFM) to couple visual features in the frequency domain and the Audio Visual Frequency Modulator (AVFM) to modulate visual features with audio embeddings, enhancing audio-visual synchronization. FREAK supports both one-shot and video dubbing settings and optimizes the model in both pixel and frequency domains.  Experimental results demonstrate that FREAK outperforms state-of-the-art methods in terms of visual quality, lip-sync accuracy, and real-time performance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The main novelty lies in the introduction of frequency-domain learning to the task of audio-driven talking portrait synthesis. While frequency domain analysis has been applied in other areas of computer vision, its application to this specific problem is a novel contribution. The VEFM and AVFM modules are also novel, designed to leverage frequency information for better feature coupling and synchronization. The seamless integration of one-shot and video dubbing is another significant aspect of the framework. The network design itself follows previous work, but the novelty of the paper centers around the innovative use of frequency modulation.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses critical challenges in talking portrait synthesis: achieving high-fidelity, accurate lip-sync, and real-time performance simultaneously. Existing methods often compromise on one or more of these aspects.  By operating in the frequency domain, FREAK aims to overcome the limitations of pixel-domain approaches, which often struggle to capture subtle details and ensure naturalness. If the experimental results are robust and reproducible, FREAK could significantly advance the field, enabling more realistic and practical applications of talking portrait technology. The real-time capability and support for video dubbing increase the practical utility of the method.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Novel approach:** The use of frequency-domain learning is a fresh perspective in talking head generation.
    *   **Real-time performance:** Balancing high-fidelity with real-time inference is crucial for practical applications, and FREAK achieves this.
    *   **Versatility:** The support for both one-shot and video dubbing settings enhances the flexibility of the framework.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper provides extensive quantitative and qualitative evaluations, including ablation studies and a user study, to demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed modules.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Dataset Variety:** Experiments rely on a relatively small dataset (7 videos of 7 distinct individuals) extracted from existing works. Evaluation across a more diverse and challenging dataset would strengthen the paper's claims. A limitation mentioned by the authors concerns the method not working particularly well for non-frontal head positions.
    *   **Dependency on Pre-trained Models:** The Hubert feature extractor is a pre-trained model, which may limit the generalizability of FREAK to scenarios where the audio characteristics differ significantly from the data used to train the Hubert model.
    *   **Complexity:** FREAK introduces frequency-based modules, adding to the overall complexity of the architecture.

*   **Potential Influence:** If the method proves to be robust and generalizable, it could influence future research in talking portrait synthesis, shifting the focus towards frequency-domain analysis and modulation. The real-time capability and support for video dubbing could also make FREAK attractive for various applications, such as virtual assistants, online meetings, and content creation. The user study provided supports the benefits, but more extensive user studies would be appropriate to further evaluate the effectiveness of the system.

**Justification for Score:**

Given the novelty of the frequency domain approach, the simultaneous achievements in fidelity, lip-sync, and real-time performance, and the solid experimental evaluations, FREAK represents a significant contribution to the field of talking portrait synthesis. While the limitations regarding dataset variety, dependence on pre-trained models, and overall architectural complexity should be considered, the strengths outweigh the weaknesses. There is potential to have an impact on the field of talking-head generation, so the score is justified.

### Beyond Memorization: Evaluating the True Type Inference Capabilities of LLMs for Java Code Snippets
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.04076v1)
- **Authors**: Yiwen Dong, Zhenyang Xu, Yongqiang Tian, Chengnian Sun
- **Abstract**: Type inference is a crucial task for reusing online code snippets, often found on platforms like StackOverflow, which frequently lack essential type information such as fully qualified names (FQNs) and required libraries. Recent studies have leveraged Large Language Models (LLMs) for type inference on code snippets, showing promising results. However, these results are potentially affected by data leakage, as the benchmark suite (StatType-SO) has been public on GitHub since 2017 (full suite in 2023). Thus, it is uncertain whether LLMs' strong performance reflects genuine code semantics understanding or a mere retrieval of ground truth from training data. To comprehensively assess LLMs' type inference capabilities on Java code snippets, we conducted a three-pronged evaluation. First, utilizing Thalia, a program synthesis technique, we created ThaliaType--a new, unseen dataset for type inference evaluation. On unseen snippets, LLM performance dropped significantly, with up to a 59% decrease in precision and 72% in recall. Second, we developed semantic-preserving transformations that significantly degraded LLMs' type inference performance, revealing weaknesses in understanding code semantics. Third, we used delta debugging to identify the minimal syntax elements sufficient for LLM inference. While type inference primarily involves inferring FQNs for types in the code snippet, LLMs correctly infer FQNs even when the types were absent from the snippets, suggesting a reliance on knowledge from training instead of thoroughly analyzing the snippets. Our findings indicate that LLMs' strong past performance likely stemmed from data leakage, rather than a genuine understanding of the semantics of code snippets. Our findings highlight the crucial need for carefully designed benchmarks using unseen code snippets to assess the true capabilities of LLMs for type inference tasks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Beyond Memorization: Evaluating the True Type Inference Capabilities of LLMs for Java Code Snippets" investigates whether the strong performance of Large Language Models (LLMs) on Java type inference tasks, as previously reported, is due to genuine understanding or data leakage.  The authors create a new dataset, ThaliaType, designed to be unseen by LLMs, perform semantic-preserving code transformations, and use delta debugging to identify minimal code elements required for LLMs to make inferences.  Their findings suggest that LLMs' previous high performance was significantly influenced by data leakage, as they perform substantially worse on the new, unseen dataset and are highly sensitive to syntactic changes, suggesting a reliance on memorized patterns rather than semantic understanding. The study provides compelling evidence that existing benchmarks may overestimate the true type inference capabilities of LLMs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its rigorous evaluation of LLMs for type inference, specifically focusing on mitigating the risk of data leakage. The creation of the ThaliaType dataset and the use of semantic-preserving transformations are valuable contributions. While delta debugging is a known technique, its application in this context to reveal minimal code elements is insightful.  The core idea of questioning LLM performance due to potential memorization has been explored in other areas (math, question answering), but the systematic and detailed application to Java type inference is novel and valuable for the software engineering community.

* **Significance:**  The paper's findings are significant because they challenge the prevailing positive view of LLMs' ability to perform type inference and highlight the importance of creating benchmarks carefully controlled for data leakage. The findings suggest that existing benchmarks may be misleading, and that LLMs may not possess the level of semantic understanding previously attributed to them.  This has implications for the adoption of LLMs in software development tasks where semantic understanding is crucial. The methodology offers a framework for other researchers to assess LLMs in SE tasks.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Rigorous Methodology:** The multi-pronged approach using a new dataset, semantic transformations, and delta debugging provides a strong and compelling argument.
    * **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies and articulates the problem of data leakage and its potential impact.
    * **Well-Structured Experiments:**  The experiments are carefully designed and executed.
    * **Detailed Analysis:**  The results are analyzed in detail, providing insights into LLMs' weaknesses.
    * **Replication Package:** The availability of the code and dataset promotes reproducibility and further research.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Set of Transformations:** While the selected semantic-preserving transformations are reasonable, it is not exhaustive. Exploring other transformation types could further strengthen the findings.
    * **GPT-40-mini only for RQ3:** Due to computational costs, they only applied delta debugging to GPT-40-mini. While understandable, using more models in this evaluation would have improved the rigor of RQ3.
    * **Document Frequency as a Proxy:** Document frequency is used as a proxy for the prevalence of types in training data. While reasonable, it is not a direct measure of inclusion in the LLMs' training set.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper will likely influence the way LLMs are evaluated in the software engineering community, particularly for tasks requiring semantic understanding. It should motivate the creation of more carefully designed benchmarks and encourage researchers to consider the potential impact of data leakage. It also calls attention to the kinds of superficial syntactic relationships models are picking up on which should spur research into methods of grounding models in more semantically relevant features of code.

* **Justification of Score:**

The paper is well-written, and the core ideas are clearly explained. While the techniques themselves are not revolutionary, the combination of these techniques with the new dataset and the application to the crucial domain of type inference highlights the potential pitfalls of LLM evaluation practices. The paper presents a *solid* piece of work that directly responds to the enthusiasm around LLMs in SE by providing a cautionary, rigorous analysis. Although there may be opportunities to perform more transformations or increase the number of LLMs used in some of the experiments, the current work is still sufficient for demonstrating that LLMs have low semantic understanding for code snippets.

### PokéChamp: an Expert-level Minimax Language Agent
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.04094v1)
- **Authors**: Seth Karten, Andy Luu Nguyen, Chi Jin
- **Abstract**: We introduce Pok\'eChamp, a minimax agent powered by Large Language Models (LLMs) for Pok\'emon battles. Built on a general framework for two-player competitive games, Pok\'eChamp leverages the generalist capabilities of LLMs to enhance minimax tree search. Specifically, LLMs replace three key modules: (1) player action sampling, (2) opponent modeling, and (3) value function estimation, enabling the agent to effectively utilize gameplay history and human knowledge to reduce the search space and address partial observability. Notably, our framework requires no additional LLM training. We evaluate Pok\'eChamp in the popular Gen 9 OU format. When powered by GPT-4o, it achieves a win rate of 76% against the best existing LLM-based bot and 84% against the strongest rule-based bot, demonstrating its superior performance. Even with an open-source 8-billion-parameter Llama 3.1 model, Pok\'eChamp consistently outperforms the previous best LLM-based bot, Pok\'ellmon powered by GPT-4o, with a 64% win rate. Pok\'eChamp attains a projected Elo of 1300-1500 on the Pok\'emon Showdown online ladder, placing it among the top 30%-10% of human players. In addition, this work compiles the largest real-player Pok\'emon battle dataset, featuring over 3 million games, including more than 500k high-Elo matches. Based on this dataset, we establish a series of battle benchmarks and puzzles to evaluate specific battling skills. We further provide key updates to the local game engine. We hope this work fosters further research that leverage Pok\'emon battle as benchmark to integrate LLM technologies with game-theoretic algorithms addressing general multiagent problems. Videos, code, and dataset available at https://sites.google.com/view/pokechamp-llm.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the PokéChamp paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces PokéChamp, a novel AI agent for Pokémon battles that leverages Large Language Models (LLMs) within a minimax tree search framework.  PokéChamp replaces key components of the traditional minimax search – action sampling, opponent modeling, and value function estimation – with LLM-powered modules. This allows the agent to reduce the search space, handle partial observability, and incorporate human-like strategic reasoning without task-specific training. The agent achieves high win rates against both heuristic-based bots, existing LLM-based bots (including one powered by GPT-4o, even when PokéChamp uses a smaller, open-source LLM), and demonstrates expert-level performance against human players on the online Pokémon Showdown ladder.  The paper also contributes a large Pokémon battle dataset and benchmarks for evaluating battling skills and updates to the local game engine.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its *integration* of LLMs with a game-theoretic algorithm (minimax search) for a complex, partially observable game.  While LLMs have been used in games before, most approaches rely on end-to-end LLM control or simple prompting strategies.  PokéChamp's approach of using LLMs as modules *within* a well-established algorithm like minimax search is more sophisticated and allows the LLM to focus on specific tasks, rather than acting as a general-purpose game solver. The introduction of benchmarks for Pokemon battle skills is also a welcome contribution, addressing a gap in the evaluation landscape for AI in the field. Finally, the compilation of such a large dataset of real Pokemon Showdown battles represents a substantial contribution to the community.

**Significance:** The significance of this work is threefold. First, it demonstrates that LLMs can be effectively used to augment traditional AI algorithms, leading to improved performance in complex game environments. Second, it addresses the common problem of limited planning capabilities in text-based language agents. Instead of trying to brute-force planning with LLMs, the authors constrain the search space using human knowledge and gameplay history and use LLMs to fill in the missing pieces (opponent modeling, value estimation). Third, by achieving expert-level performance in Pokémon battles, PokéChamp provides a strong proof-of-concept for the potential of this hybrid approach. The project is accessible and has provided code, datasets, and instructions making it possible for other researchers to build upon its work.

**Strengths:**

*   **Strong performance:** PokéChamp demonstrably outperforms existing bots and achieves competitive results against human players. The results are compelling and clearly presented.
*   **Well-defined architecture:** The paper clearly explains how LLMs are integrated into the minimax framework and the benefits of this modular approach.
*   **Generalizability:** The framework is designed to be generalizable to other two-player competitive games.
*   **Resource availability:** The release of the dataset, benchmarks, and code makes this research highly valuable to the community.
*   **Thorough evaluation:** The authors conduct comprehensive experiments, including arena-style competitions, ladder battles, and puzzle scenarios.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Reliance on pre-existing LLM knowledge:** The agent relies heavily on the LLM's pre-existing knowledge of Pokémon, which may limit its applicability to games where such knowledge is unavailable. While the authors do not require additional LLM training, they assume a certain level of pre-existing knowledge.
*   **Black Box nature:** The paper does not provide a deep analysis of *why* the LLMs perform well in each module. Understanding the reasoning processes within the LLMs would provide valuable insights for future research.
*   **Limited analysis of LLM behavior:** The analysis of the cases where PokéChamp struggles, while present, could be more detailed. Specifically, why do the LLMs perform poorly when predicting actions or modeling opponents when stall or excessive switching tactics are present?
*   **Minor time constraint issues:** The paper admits that approximately 1/3 of the matches had to be removed because PokeChamp lost due to exceeding the time limit. This demonstrates potential areas of improvement for more efficient decision making.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a significant contribution to the field of AI in games by demonstrating a novel and effective approach to integrating LLMs with game-theoretic algorithms. The performance of PokéChamp is impressive, and the release of resources will undoubtedly foster further research in this area. However, the reliance on pre-existing LLM knowledge, limited insight into LLM reasoning processes, and a few minor methodological gaps prevent it from achieving a higher score.

### Chart-HQA: A Benchmark for Hypothetical Question Answering in Charts
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.04095v1)
- **Authors**: Xiangnan Chen, Yuancheng Fang, Qian Xiao, Juncheng Li, Jun Lin, Siliang Tang, Yi Yang, Yueting Zhuang
- **Abstract**: Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have garnered significant attention for their strong visual-semantic understanding. Most existing chart benchmarks evaluate MLLMs' ability to parse information from charts to answer questions.However, they overlook the inherent output biases of MLLMs, where models rely on their parametric memory to answer questions rather than genuinely understanding the chart content. To address this limitation, we introduce a novel Chart Hypothetical Question Answering (HQA) task, which imposes assumptions on the same question to compel models to engage in counterfactual reasoning based on the chart content. Furthermore, we introduce HAI, a human-AI interactive data synthesis approach that leverages the efficient text-editing capabilities of LLMs alongside human expert knowledge to generate diverse and high-quality HQA data at a low cost. Using HAI, we construct Chart-HQA, a challenging benchmark synthesized from publicly available data sources. Evaluation results on 18 MLLMs of varying model sizes reveal that current models face significant generalization challenges and exhibit imbalanced reasoning performance on the HQA task.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Chart-HQA: A Benchmark for Hypothetical Question Answering in Charts":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Chart-HQA, a novel benchmark designed to evaluate the hypothetical question-answering (HQA) capabilities of Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) when dealing with charts. The core idea is to address the "output bias" issue in existing chart benchmarks, where MLLMs often rely on parametric memory rather than truly understanding the visual content. Chart-HQA imposes counterfactual assumptions on questions, forcing models to engage in deeper reasoning and inferencing based on the chart. To create this benchmark, the authors propose HAI, a human-AI interactive data synthesis approach leveraging LLMs' text editing abilities and human expertise. They demonstrate that current MLLMs struggle with generalization and exhibit imbalanced reasoning performance on the HQA task, highlighting the need for improved models in this area.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in identifying and addressing the output bias problem in chart understanding benchmarks. While existing benchmarks focus on factoid QA, this paper introduces a more challenging hypothetical QA task that requires models to engage in counterfactual reasoning. The HAI data synthesis approach is also a novel contribution, combining LLM capabilities with human expertise to generate diverse and high-quality HQA data. The counterfactual proposal generator (CIG) and the human-feedback discriminator (HFD) modules are novel elements within the HAI framework.

*   **Significance:** The significance of this work stems from its potential to advance the field of multimodal reasoning. By highlighting the limitations of current MLLMs in chart understanding, the paper motivates the development of more robust and reliable models. The Chart-HQA benchmark provides a valuable tool for evaluating these models and driving progress in this area. The HAI data synthesis approach offers a scalable and cost-effective method for creating challenging datasets for multimodal tasks.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly defines the output bias problem and explains why it is important to address it in chart understanding benchmarks.
    *   **Novel Benchmark:** The Chart-HQA benchmark is well-designed and poses a significant challenge to current MLLMs.
    *   **Effective Data Synthesis:** The HAI data synthesis approach is innovative and produces high-quality HQA data.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper evaluates a wide range of MLLMs on the Chart-HQA benchmark, providing valuable insights into their strengths and weaknesses.
    *   **Detailed Analysis:** The paper provides a detailed analysis of the results, including a fine-grained evaluation of different answer types and a case study demonstrating the reasoning challenges posed by the HQA task.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Experimentation:** While the zero-shot evaluation is comprehensive, the paper could benefit from exploring few-shot or fine-tuning experiments to further assess the potential of MLLMs on the HQA task.
    *   **Dataset Scale:** While the dataset is a significant contribution, increasing the scale of Chart-HQA could provide more robust evaluation and training opportunities for MLLMs.
    *   **Generalizability of HAI:** While HAI appears effective for charts, a discussion of its applicability to other visual reasoning tasks would strengthen the paper.

*   **Potential Influence:** This paper has the potential to influence the field by:

    *   Shifting the focus of chart understanding research from factoid QA to more challenging reasoning tasks.
    *   Motivating the development of new MLLMs that are better equipped to handle counterfactual reasoning and avoid output bias.
    *   Providing a valuable benchmark for evaluating the progress of MLLMs in chart understanding.
    *   Offering a scalable and cost-effective method for creating challenging datasets for multimodal tasks.

**Justification for the Score:**

The paper makes a solid contribution by identifying a crucial limitation in existing chart understanding benchmarks and proposes a novel solution through the Chart-HQA benchmark and HAI data synthesis method. The experimental results clearly demonstrate the challenges posed by the HQA task and highlight the shortcomings of current MLLMs. While there are some limitations, such as dataset scale and the absence of few-shot/fine-tuning experiments, the overall novelty and significance of the work justify a high score.

### Disparities in LLM Reasoning Accuracy and Explanations: A Case Study on African American English
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.04099v1)
- **Authors**: Runtao Zhou, Guangya Wan, Saadia Gabriel, Sheng Li, Alexander J Gates, Maarten Sap, Thomas Hartvigsen
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) have demonstrated remarkable capabilities in reasoning tasks, leading to their widespread deployment. However, recent studies have highlighted concerning biases in these models, particularly in their handling of dialectal variations like African American English (AAE). In this work, we systematically investigate dialectal disparities in LLM reasoning tasks. We develop an experimental framework comparing LLM performance given Standard American English (SAE) and AAE prompts, combining LLM-based dialect conversion with established linguistic analyses. We find that LLMs consistently produce less accurate responses and simpler reasoning chains and explanations for AAE inputs compared to equivalent SAE questions, with disparities most pronounced in social science and humanities domains. These findings highlight systematic differences in how LLMs process and reason about different language varieties, raising important questions about the development and deployment of these systems in our multilingual and multidialectal world. Our code repository is publicly available at https://github.com/Runtaozhou/dialect_bias_eval.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Disparities in LLM Reasoning Accuracy and Explanations: A Case Study on African American English":

**Summary:**

This paper investigates dialectal disparities in Large Language Model (LLM) reasoning, focusing on African American English (AAE) versus Standard American English (SAE).  The authors develop a framework that includes LLM-based dialect conversion, comparing LLM performance across a range of reasoning tasks, examining accuracy, readability of explanations, psychological expression within explanations, and output consistency. The study finds that LLMs consistently produce less accurate responses and simpler explanations for AAE inputs compared to equivalent SAE prompts.  They also explore prompting strategies for mitigation and discuss the implications of their findings for equitable LLM deployment.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its systematic exploration of not just *accuracy* disparities in LLM performance across AAE and SAE, but also in analyzing the *explanations* generated by LLMs. While previous work has identified biases against AAE in tasks like toxicity detection, this paper delves deeper into the reasoning processes and communication styles of LLMs when handling different dialects. The detailed analysis of readability and psychological expressions in explanations is a notable contribution. It goes beyond typical benchmark accuracy metrics.

*   **Significance:** The significance of this work stems from the increasing deployment of LLMs in sensitive domains like education and healthcare. Biased reasoning and communication styles can have detrimental effects on AAE speakers. The paper highlights the potential for LLMs to reinforce existing societal biases even when providing correct answers, for example, by framing AAE grammatical constructions as "relaxed" versions of SAE. The identification of potentially effective mitigation strategies, even if preliminary, also increases the practical value of the research.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Systematic Approach:** The paper uses a well-defined experimental framework that combines LLM-based dialect conversion with established linguistic analyses and reasoning assessments.
    *   **Multi-Dimensional Analysis:** The study examines LLM reasoning across multiple dimensions: accuracy, readability, psychological expression, and consistency.
    *   **Practical Implications:** The paper discusses the potential implications of dialectal disparities for LLM deployment in sensitive domains and explores initial mitigation strategies.
    *   **Detailed Implementation:** The inclusion of prompt examples in the appendix and the availability of code is important for reproducibility and further research.
    *   **Human Evaluation:** The inclusion of AAE speakers in human evaluations bolsters the credibility of dialect conversion and understanding.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Reliance on LLM-based Dialect Conversion:** While the authors validate their LLM-based dialect converter, there is an inherent risk of imperfect translations affecting the LLM's performance.  Human-annotated AAE prompts, even if more resource-intensive, could provide a stronger baseline.
    *   **Limited Scope of Mitigation Strategies:** The mitigation strategies explored are preliminary and primarily focused on prompt engineering. More sophisticated techniques, such as fine-tuning or adversarial training, might be necessary for more substantial improvements.
    *   **Generalizability:** The study focuses on AAE, and while the findings likely extend to other dialects, further research is needed to confirm this.
    *   **Limited Model Architectures**: Analysis focuses on a set of established model architectures but may not fully reflect the complex dynamics of all LLMs currently available.
* **Justification of Score:** The work provides novel findings and highlights implications for future work. While the paper does rely on LLM-based conversion and the mitigation strategies are preliminary, the work is well-written, the methodologies sound, and the results are compelling.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CL

### LLMs Can Generate a Better Answer by Aggregating Their Own Responses
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.04104v1)
- **Authors**: Zichong Li, Xinyu Feng, Yuheng Cai, Zixuan Zhang, Tianyi Liu, Chen Liang, Weizhu Chen, Haoyu Wang, Tuo Zhao
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) have shown remarkable capabilities across tasks, yet they often require additional prompting techniques when facing complex problems. While approaches like self-correction and response selection have emerged as popular solutions, recent studies have shown these methods perform poorly when relying on the LLM itself to provide feedback or selection criteria. We argue this limitation stems from the fact that common LLM post-training procedures lack explicit supervision for discriminative judgment tasks. In this paper, we propose Generative Self-Aggregation (GSA), a novel prompting method that improves answer quality without requiring the model's discriminative capabilities. GSA first samples multiple diverse responses from the LLM, then aggregates them to obtain an improved solution. Unlike previous approaches, our method does not require the LLM to correct errors or compare response quality; instead, it leverages the model's generative abilities to synthesize a new response based on the context of multiple samples. While GSA shares similarities with the self-consistency (SC) approach for response aggregation, SC requires specific verifiable tokens to enable majority voting. In contrast, our approach is more general and can be applied to open-ended tasks. Empirical evaluation demonstrates that GSA effectively improves response quality across various tasks, including mathematical reasoning, knowledge-based problems, and open-ended generation tasks such as code synthesis and conversational responses.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Generative Self-Aggregation (GSA), a novel prompting method designed to improve the performance of Large Language Models (LLMs) without requiring explicit training for discriminative tasks. GSA operates in two stages: first, it generates multiple diverse responses to a given query; second, it uses these responses as context to prompt the model to synthesize an improved solution. The key advantage of GSA is that it leverages the inherent generative capabilities of LLMs to learn from and combine insights from multiple attempts, rather than relying on their often-flawed ability to directly compare and select the best response. Through experiments across various tasks, including mathematical reasoning, knowledge-based question answering, and open-ended generation, the authors demonstrate that GSA outperforms existing self-correction and choose-from-N methods, while achieving comparable or better results than self-consistency in relevant tasks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of aggregating multiple LLM responses to improve performance is not entirely new (e.g., self-consistency). However, GSA distinguishes itself by moving beyond simple majority voting or selection of the most likely candidate. The core novelty lies in using the LLM's generative capacity to *synthesize* a new response based on the context of multiple attempts. This is a clever way to circumvent the documented weaknesses of LLMs in discriminative judgment tasks. The connection to, and departure from, self-consistency is well articulated.

*   **Significance:** The potential significance of GSA is considerable. It offers a relatively simple, training-free way to boost LLM performance across a broad range of tasks. Its applicability to open-ended generation tasks, where self-consistency is not applicable, is a strong selling point. The empirical results support the claim that GSA is a robust and effective technique. If GSA proves to be widely applicable and easily implemented, it could become a standard prompting technique.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear and Well-Defined Method:** GSA is easy to understand and implement. The paper provides a good description of the method and its underlying rationale.
    *   **Strong Empirical Evaluation:** The experiments cover a diverse range of tasks and models, providing strong evidence for the effectiveness of GSA. Ablation studies shed light on the method's robustness to different sampling strategies.
    *   **Good Analysis:** The paper includes an analysis of likelihood distributions, further supporting the claim that LLMs are more confident in generating new responses than in selecting among existing ones.  The case study examples illustrate the method's strengths and how it combines information from different solutions.
    *   **Well written and clearly presented:** The paper is easy to follow and the results are clearly presented.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Computational Cost:** Generating multiple responses inherently increases computational cost. While the paper standardizes the number of model calls, the increased cost might be a barrier in some applications.  The paper does not directly address the efficiency of GSA compared to other techniques, and could include discussion of the additional computational cost compared to the performance gains
    *   **Prompt Engineering:** Like many prompting-based methods, GSA's performance is likely sensitive to prompt engineering. While the paper details the prompts used, further investigation into prompt robustness would be beneficial.
    *   **Limited theoretical analysis:** It could benefit from a deeper theoretical analysis that explains why GSA is effective compared to selecting one of the candidate responses.

*   **Potential Influence:** GSA has the potential to influence the field of LLM prompting and application development. Its simplicity and effectiveness could lead to its widespread adoption.  The generative aggregation approach may inspire other researchers to explore ways to leverage LLMs' generation capabilities for tasks beyond simple text generation. The technique could also be used to automatically generate high-quality training data.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel and effective prompting method that addresses a significant limitation of LLMs. The empirical results are compelling, and the analysis provides valuable insights into the method's behavior. While the increased computational cost and prompt engineering sensitivity are valid concerns, the potential benefits of GSA outweigh these drawbacks. Therefore, GSA represents a solid contribution to the field and likely to stimulate future research.

### InterChat: Enhancing Generative Visual Analytics using Multimodal Interactions
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.04110v1)
- **Authors**: Juntong Chen, Jiang Wu, Jiajing Guo, Vikram Mohanty, Xueming Li, Jorge Piazentin Ono, Wenbin He, Liu Ren, Dongyu Liu
- **Abstract**: The rise of Large Language Models (LLMs) and generative visual analytics systems has transformed data-driven insights, yet significant challenges persist in accurately interpreting users' analytical and interaction intents. While language inputs offer flexibility, they often lack precision, making the expression of complex intents inefficient, error-prone, and time-intensive. To address these limitations, we investigate the design space of multimodal interactions for generative visual analytics through a literature review and pilot brainstorming sessions. Building on these insights, we introduce a highly extensible workflow that integrates multiple LLM agents for intent inference and visualization generation. We develop InterChat, a generative visual analytics system that combines direct manipulation of visual elements with natural language inputs. This integration enables precise intent communication and supports progressive, visually driven exploratory data analyses. By employing effective prompt engineering, and contextual interaction linking, alongside intuitive visualization and interaction designs, InterChat bridges the gap between user interactions and LLM-driven visualizations, enhancing both interpretability and usability. Extensive evaluations, including two usage scenarios, a user study, and expert feedback, demonstrate the effectiveness of InterChat. Results show significant improvements in the accuracy and efficiency of handling complex visual analytics tasks, highlighting the potential of multimodal interactions to redefine user engagement and analytical depth in generative visual analytics.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the InterChat paper:

**Summary:**

The InterChat paper introduces a novel generative visual analytics system that enhances data exploration and insight discovery through multimodal interactions. It tackles the challenge of accurately interpreting user analytical intents in LLM-powered visual analytics.  InterChat integrates direct manipulation of visual elements (clicking, dragging, lassoing) with natural language inputs, enabling a more precise and intuitive way to convey complex analytical needs. The system uses a multi-agent LLM architecture for intent inference and visualization generation. The design is informed by a design space consisting of interaction, intent, and instruction. The authors demonstrate the effectiveness of InterChat through real-world usage scenarios, a user study, and expert feedback, showing improvements in accuracy and efficiency, particularly for complex tasks. The paper also identifies limitations and suggests directions for future research.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the *integration* of multimodal interactions (direct manipulation + natural language) within a *generative* visual analytics system leveraging LLMs. While LLMs for visualization and multimodal interaction are established areas, their fusion with a focus on addressing specific limitations in expressing complex analytical intent is a valuable contribution. DirectGPT used structured prompts before, and while this paper incorporates those, it moves beyond by integrating interaction linking and a Vision LLM-based component for intent interpretation.

*   **Significance:**

    *   **Impact on the field:** InterChat has the potential to reshape how users engage with generative visual analytics systems. The system tackles the black box issue of LLMs by providing greater transparency through interaction linking. The expressiveness of multimodal interfaces allows for more natural and efficient data analysis workflows which could lead to faster and richer insights. The work also offers practical insights for designing future visual analytics systems that effectively incorporate LLMs.
    *   **Practicality:** The evaluation is relatively thorough, with two realistic usage scenarios that demonstrate how this system can be employed. A user study provides some solid empirical evidence.
    *   **Generalizability:** The paper emphasizes the generalizability of the system, which is essential if it is to be employed in diverse domains. However, the evaluation does not specifically focus on this.
    *   **Weaknesses:** The evaluation is relatively limited in size (ten users), and task complexity could be raised. There are also certain aspects that the system depends on that have limited explanations, such as dataset descriptions and their impact. The paper would also benefit from a more detailed discussion of the limitations and failure cases.
    *   **Failure Analysis:** While the failure analysis provides valuable insights, it primarily focuses on two key categories: intent misinterpretation and visualization generation errors. A deeper investigation into the specific underlying causes of these issues could further enhance the system's robustness. Additionally, exploring how different user interaction patterns might contribute to failures could provide valuable guidance for future design improvements.

**Justification of Score:**

InterChat earns a score of **7/10**. Here's why:

*   **Strengths:** The idea of multimodal interaction with generative AI for visual analytics is promising and demonstrates a clear need to improve the usability and accuracy of these systems. The system's design is logical and well-informed. The evaluation provides initial empirical evidence of the system's effectiveness. The research question addressed and has the potential for broad applicability.
*   **Weaknesses:** The evaluation is limited, and future studies need to be conducted with a wider range of participants and tasks, as well as a comparison with the state of the art. Additionally, the paper could benefit from a deeper discussion of limitations and future work, including more in-depth analysis of error cases and potential mitigation strategies.
*   **Overall:** InterChat makes a significant contribution by highlighting the benefits of multimodal interaction in LLM-driven visual analytics. The system provides a solid foundation for future research and development in this area.

### Simple Self Organizing Map with Visual Transformer
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.04121v1)
- **Authors**: Alan Luo, Kaiwen Yuan
- **Abstract**: Vision Transformers (ViTs) have demonstrated exceptional performance in various vision tasks. However, they tend to underperform on smaller datasets due to their inherent lack of inductive biases. Current approaches address this limitation implicitly-often by pairing ViTs with pretext tasks or by distilling knowledge from convolutional neural networks (CNNs) to strengthen the prior. In contrast, Self-Organizing Maps (SOMs), a widely adopted self-supervised framework, are inherently structured to preserve topology and spatial organization, making them a promising candidate to directly address the limitations of ViTs in limited or small training datasets. Despite this potential, equipping SOMs with modern deep learning architectures remains largely unexplored. In this study, we conduct a novel exploration on how Vision Transformers (ViTs) and Self-Organizing Maps (SOMs) can empower each other, aiming to bridge this critical research gap. Our findings demonstrate that these architectures can synergistically enhance each other, leading to significantly improved performance in both unsupervised and supervised tasks. Code will be publicly available.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ViT-SOM, a novel framework that integrates Vision Transformers (ViTs) with Self-Organizing Maps (SOMs).  It addresses the limitations of ViTs (lack of inductive bias, especially on small datasets) and SOMs (poor feature abstraction). The framework leverages SOMs to impose topological constraints on ViT embeddings, while ViTs provide robust feature extraction for SOMs. Experiments on unsupervised clustering and supervised classification tasks demonstrate performance improvements compared to baseline and state-of-the-art models. The authors evaluate ViT-SOM against benchmarks like MNIST, Fashion-MNIST, CIFAR-10, and CIFAR-100.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The central idea of combining ViTs and SOMs to address their respective weaknesses is moderately novel. While individual components are well-established, their synergistic integration, specifically focusing on topological constraints and feature extraction roles, is a relatively new approach.  Prior work explored CNN-SOM combinations, but the shift to ViTs offers different advantages and challenges. However, the method itself is relatively simple: feeding ViT embeddings into a standard SOM. The core innovation lies more in the *application* of this combination rather than a radical algorithmic change.
* **Significance:** The paper's significance lies in its potential to improve the performance of ViTs on small datasets and to enhance SOMs with more powerful feature representations.  Empirical results show improvements over established baselines, including ResNet and DESOM, but the gains are not exceptionally large in most cases.  The UMAP visualizations provide some qualitative insight into the learned embeddings and topological organization. The method's simplicity is a strength, making it potentially easy to adopt and extend. However, this same simplicity might limit the magnitude of achievable improvements. The work successfully demonstrates the mutual benefit of the combination.
* **Strengths:**
    * Clear problem statement and well-defined objective.
    * Relatively simple and easily understandable approach.
    * Strong experimental validation across several datasets.
    * Demonstrates consistent improvements over baselines.
* **Weaknesses:**
    * The approach, while effective, is relatively straightforward. The integration is not deeply interwoven, but rather an embedding and connection of two distinct stages.
    * Performance gains, although consistent, are not always substantial, especially on already high-performing datasets. The absolute improvements, while existing, may not lead to the widespread adoption of the proposed method.
    * Limited exploration of the hyperparameter space and the trade-offs between ViT and SOM architectures.
    * Lack of in-depth analysis of why the method works. The UMAP visualizations are helpful, but a deeper understanding of the feature representations and the topological constraints is needed.
    * The potential negative effects of a rigid SOM grid on the latent topology are only briefly mentioned. Adaptive SOMs or other approaches to address this limitation could be explored.
* **Impact:** The paper is likely to have a moderate impact on the field. It opens a new avenue for research by demonstrating the potential of combining ViTs and SOMs. It provides a solid baseline for future work in this area. However, the relatively small performance gains and the simplicity of the approach might limit its widespread adoption.
* **Rigorous rationale:** The novelty is moderate because the core components are well-known and the interaction mechanism is relatively basic. The significance is limited by the incremental nature of the improvements. While the paper demonstrates empirical evidence, the lack of deeper analysis and the relatively small performance gains temper the potential impact. While this is a starting point, a truly significant leap in the field would require more significant performance gains.
