### Diff-Reg v2: Diffusion-Based Matching Matrix Estimation for Image Matching and 3D Registration
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.04127v1)
- **Authors**: Qianliang Wu, Haobo Jiang, Yaqing Ding, Lei Luo, Jin Xie, Jian Yang
- **Abstract**: Establishing reliable correspondences is crucial for all registration tasks, including 2D image registration, 3D point cloud registration, and 2D-3D image-to-point cloud registration. However, these tasks are often complicated by challenges such as scale inconsistencies, symmetry, and large deformations, which can lead to ambiguous matches. Previous feature-based and correspondence-based methods typically rely on geometric or semantic features to generate or polish initial potential correspondences. Some methods typically leverage specific geometric priors, such as topological preservation, to devise diverse and innovative strategies tailored to a given enhancement goal, which cannot be exhaustively enumerated. Additionally, many previous approaches rely on a single-step prediction head, which can struggle with local minima in complex matching scenarios. To address these challenges, we introduce an innovative paradigm that leverages a diffusion model in matrix space for robust matching matrix estimation. Our model treats correspondence estimation as a denoising diffusion process in the matching matrix space, gradually refining the intermediate matching matrix to the optimal one. Specifically, we apply the diffusion model in the doubly stochastic matrix space for 3D-3D and 2D-3D registration tasks. In the 2D image registration task, we deploy the diffusion model in a matrix subspace where dual-softmax projection regularization is applied. For all three registration tasks, we provide adaptive matching matrix embedding implementations tailored to the specific characteristics of each task while maintaining a consistent "match-to-warp" encoding pattern. Furthermore, we adopt a lightweight design for the denoising module. In inference, once points or image features are extracted and fixed, this module performs multi-step denoising predictions through reverse sampling.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Diff-Reg v2: Diffusion-Based Matching Matrix Estimation for Image Matching and 3D Registration":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Diff-Reg v2, a novel approach that utilizes a diffusion model in the matching matrix space for image matching and 3D registration tasks. The core idea is to treat correspondence estimation as a denoising diffusion process, where Gaussian noise is progressively added to the ground truth matching matrix in the forward process. The reverse denoising process then iteratively refines the noisy matrix back to the optimal one. The method proposes a lightweight denoising module adaptable to different registration tasks and offers adaptive matching matrix embedding tailored to the specific characteristics of each task. The model is tested on 2D image matching, 3D point cloud registration, and 2D-3D registration tasks using real-world datasets. The experiments aim to demonstrate the effectiveness of this diffusion-based matching matrix estimation approach.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The paper presents a few novel elements:

1.  **Diffusion in Matching Matrix Space:**  Applying diffusion models directly to the matching matrix is a relatively unexplored avenue in registration. Existing diffusion model-based registration methods usually operate in SE(3) space or flow field space. Directly manipulating and refining the matching matrix itself provides more direct control over the correspondence estimation process.
2.  **Lightweight Denoising Module:** Designing a generalized lightweight denoising module suitable for different registration tasks (2D, 3D, 2D-3D) while maintaining the "match-to-warp" encoding is beneficial for practical applications. The paper argues this allows for faster convergence.
3.  **Consistent "Match-to-Warp" Encoding:**  Implementing the matching matrix embedding module consistently across 3D registration, 2D-3D registration, and 2D image matching tasks is a significant contribution as it reduces task-specific engineering.

**Significance:**

The paper contributes to the field by addressing common challenges in registration like scale inconsistencies, large deformations, and ambiguous matches. The use of a diffusion model provides a way to escape local minima, and the matrix space operation offers potential advantages over methods operating directly in SE(3) or flow field spaces. The results show improvements in accuracy and robustness on standard datasets.

**Strengths:**

*   **Addresses a core problem:** Accurate correspondence estimation is fundamental in many applications.
*   **Novel approach:**  Diffusion models in matching matrix space are a novel concept.
*   **Comprehensive evaluation:** The method is evaluated on a variety of tasks and datasets, providing evidence of its versatility.
*   **Lightweight design:** Claimed computational efficiency which is important in practice.
*  **Good writing & clear exposition**: The paper is well-written and explains complex ideas clearly

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Computational Cost:** Though the denoising module is lightweight, the inherent iterative nature of diffusion models adds computational overhead. Table I indicates that *Diff-Reg* runs slower than the semi-dense baselines. Improvements and advancements in diffusion model optimization would make it faster.
*   **Incremental Improvement:**  The improvements, while consistent, seem incremental, especially compared to the existing state-of-the-art methods in some cases.  It's not a paradigm shift; instead it is more of a fine-tuning
*  **Performance in limited overlap** The 3DLoMatch results were good but not outstanding.

**Potential Influence:**

The paper could influence future research by:

*   Inspiring further exploration of diffusion models in matching matrix space.
*   Providing a foundation for developing more efficient diffusion-based registration methods.
*   Encouraging the use of consistent "match-to-warp" encoding strategies across different registration tasks.

**Justification for Score:**

Overall, the paper presents a solid contribution with clear novelty in its approach. However, while the results are positive and the idea is interesting, the performance gains are not revolutionary, and the computational cost remains a relevant concern.

Score: 7

**Rationale**: The paper showcases a novel approach to image and 3D registration by applying diffusion models directly to the matching matrix, offering a potential escape from local minima and providing greater control over correspondence estimation. The results demonstrate effectiveness across multiple tasks and datasets. However, the improvements over existing methods are incremental rather than transformative, and the inherent computational demands of diffusion models are also concerning. While this work contributes meaningfully to the field, the limited performance gains and computational cost prevent it from achieving a significantly higher rating.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Token-Efficient Long Video Understanding for Multimodal LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.04130v1)
- **Authors**: Jindong Jiang, Xiuyu Li, Zhijian Liu, Muyang Li, Guo Chen, Zhiqi Li, De-An Huang, Guilin Liu, Zhiding Yu, Kurt Keutzer, Sungjin Ahn, Jan Kautz, Hongxu Yin, Yao Lu, Song Han, Wonmin Byeon
- **Abstract**: Recent advances in video-based multimodal large language models (Video-LLMs) have significantly improved video understanding by processing videos as sequences of image frames. However, many existing methods treat frames independently in the vision backbone, lacking explicit temporal modeling, which limits their ability to capture dynamic patterns and efficiently handle long videos. To address these limitations, we introduce STORM (\textbf{S}patiotemporal \textbf{TO}ken \textbf{R}eduction for \textbf{M}ultimodal LLMs), a novel architecture incorporating a dedicated temporal encoder between the image encoder and the LLM. Our temporal encoder leverages the Mamba State Space Model to integrate temporal information into image tokens, generating enriched representations that preserve inter-frame dynamics across the entire video sequence. This enriched encoding not only enhances video reasoning capabilities but also enables effective token reduction strategies, including test-time sampling and training-based temporal and spatial pooling, substantially reducing computational demands on the LLM without sacrificing key temporal information. By integrating these techniques, our approach simultaneously reduces training and inference latency while improving performance, enabling efficient and robust video understanding over extended temporal contexts. Extensive evaluations show that STORM achieves state-of-the-art results across various long video understanding benchmarks (more than 5\% improvement on MLVU and LongVideoBench) while reducing the computation costs by up to $8\times$ and the decoding latency by 2.4-2.9$\times$ for the fixed numbers of input frames. Project page is available at https://research.nvidia.com/labs/lpr/storm
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces STORM (Spatiotemporal Token Reduction for Multimodal LLMs), a novel architecture designed to improve the efficiency and performance of video-based multimodal large language models (Video-LLMs), particularly when dealing with long videos. STORM incorporates a dedicated temporal encoder (using Mamba State Space Model) between the image encoder and the LLM. This encoder integrates temporal information into image tokens, generating enriched representations that capture inter-frame dynamics.  The enriched encoding facilitates effective token reduction strategies like test-time sampling and training-based spatial/temporal pooling. By reducing the computational burden on the LLM, STORM enhances video reasoning capabilities, improves performance, and reduces training and inference latency, enabling efficient video understanding over extended contexts. Experiments on various benchmarks demonstrate state-of-the-art results and significant computational cost reduction.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper demonstrates novelty primarily in its architectural design by strategically placing a Mamba-based temporal encoder between the image encoder and the LLM. While Mamba itself is not a new invention, its use within this specific architecture and integration with token reduction techniques in the context of video LLMs shows innovation. The specific combination of Mamba with token reduction to handle long video sequences more efficiently also constitutes a novel contribution. This isn't simply slapping Mamba onto an existing pipeline; it's about understanding where and how to use it for maximum impact.

*   **Significance:** The significance of this work lies in addressing the computational bottleneck that limits the performance of video LLMs when processing long sequences. The efficient token reduction strategies enable Video-LLMs to be trained and used more effectively on long videos. This makes the use of LLMs practical for applications requiring analyzing long videos and understanding the relationships between visual dynamics, as it has the potential to enable longer context processing capabilities in resource constrained settings. The improvements in performance compared to existing methods indicate this is a significant contribution.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Strong performance:** The experimental results convincingly demonstrate state-of-the-art performance across multiple long video understanding benchmarks.
    *   **Efficiency:** The token reduction strategies lead to substantial computational cost reductions, making it feasible to process long videos.
    *   **Well-designed architecture:** The strategic placement of the Mamba-based temporal encoder enhances both performance and efficiency.
    *   **Extensive evaluations:** The paper presents thorough ablations and comparisons with existing methods.
    *   **Clear and well-written:** The paper is generally well-written and explains the approach and results effectively.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Dependency on Mamba:** The architecture relies heavily on the Mamba State Space Model. Future work should explore the use of alternative architectures.
    *   **Incremental Improvement:** The paper presents a useful approach to long video understanding, but it might be considered incremental rather than a radical departure. It builds upon existing techniques (token reduction) with a Mamba layer, rather than introducing entirely new concepts.
    *   **System Specialization:** While the paper emphasizes efficiency, the architecture is geared towards systems with GPUs. Other techniques optimized for CPU usage or edge computing would expand the usefulness of the tool.

*   **Potential Influence:**  STORM has the potential to influence the field by providing a practical approach to long video understanding. This could influence future video LLM research by demonstrating the importance of explicit temporal encoding and efficient token reduction. It also provides a benchmark for further research efforts within the field of video LLMs.

**Score: 7**

**Justification:**

A score of 7 reflects the paper's solid contributions and potential impact, while acknowledging certain limitations. The novelty lies in the specific architecture and the integration of the Mamba model within that architecture, combined with token reduction for improved efficiency. This is more than an incremental step as the benchmarks show increased accuracy on several tasks. The paper provides an extensive amount of comparison data and a detailed architectural overview, which allows others to both recreate and build upon this work. The weaknesses mentioned, such as the reliance on Mamba architecture and limited system optimization, affect the long-term and broad usefulness of this work. Overall, the paper represents a valuable addition to the field.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Biological Sequence with Language Model Prompting: A Survey
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.04135v1)
- **Authors**: Jiyue Jiang, Zikang Wang, Yuheng Shan, Heyan Chai, Jiayi Li, Zixian Ma, Xinrui Zhang, Yu Li
- **Abstract**: Large Language models (LLMs) have emerged as powerful tools for addressing challenges across diverse domains. Notably, recent studies have demonstrated that large language models significantly enhance the efficiency of biomolecular analysis and synthesis, attracting widespread attention from academics and medicine. In this paper, we systematically investigate the application of prompt-based methods with LLMs to biological sequences, including DNA, RNA, proteins, and drug discovery tasks. Specifically, we focus on how prompt engineering enables LLMs to tackle domain-specific problems, such as promoter sequence prediction, protein structure modeling, and drug-target binding affinity prediction, often with limited labeled data. Furthermore, our discussion highlights the transformative potential of prompting in bioinformatics while addressing key challenges such as data scarcity, multimodal fusion, and computational resource limitations. Our aim is for this paper to function both as a foundational primer for newcomers and a catalyst for continued innovation within this dynamic field of study.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a concise summary and a rigorous critical evaluation of the paper.

**Summary:**

This paper presents a survey of how large language models (LLMs) are being used with prompt engineering to analyze biological sequences like DNA, RNA, and proteins. It explores how prompts allow LLMs to tackle tasks like promoter prediction, protein structure modeling, and drug-target binding, especially when labeled data is scarce. The survey categorizes prompting methods based on their applications in bioinformatics. The paper also discusses challenges like data scarcity, multimodal data integration, and computational resource limitations, and suggests future research directions in areas like data-centric synthetic annotation, multimodal prompt fusion, and lightweight adaptation techniques.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its systematic compilation and categorization of prompt engineering techniques applied to biological sequence analysis using LLMs. While individual applications of LLMs to bioinformatics are already documented, this survey brings together a diverse set of approaches under a unified "prompt engineering" lens. This provides a valuable overview of the current state-of-the-art. However, it doesn't introduce any fundamentally new *methodological* contributions; it's primarily a review and organization of existing work. The section on future directions, while insightful, is speculative.

**Significance:**  The paper's significance is in its ability to:

*   **Provide a clear overview for newcomers:** It acts as a good starting point for researchers entering the field of LLMs in bioinformatics. It lays out the foundational concepts of biological sequences, prompt engineering, and how they can be combined.
*   **Highlight the transformative potential:** It showcases the ability of LLMs and prompt engineering to address data scarcity, a persistent challenge in bioinformatics.
*   **Catalyze further innovation:** By identifying current limitations and suggesting future directions, the survey can potentially inspire new research endeavors in multimodal fusion, efficient adaptation, and data augmentation strategies.

**Strengths:**

*   **Well-organized and clearly written:**  The paper is easy to follow and presents a structured overview of the topic.
*   **Comprehensive coverage:** It covers a broad range of applications across DNA, RNA, proteins, and drug discovery.
*   **Addresses key challenges:** It identifies crucial issues that need to be tackled for LLMs to be more effectively utilized in bioinformatics.
*   **Provides concrete examples:** It includes illustrative examples of how prompts are designed and used for specific tasks.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Lack of in-depth technical analysis:** The paper primarily provides a high-level overview without delving deeply into the mathematical formulations or algorithmic details of the surveyed methods. This limits its utility for researchers already working in the field who might need a more nuanced analysis.
*   **Limited critical evaluation of the effectiveness of different prompting strategies:** While it mentions several methods, it doesn't provide a thorough comparative analysis of their performance or limitations.
*   **Future directions are somewhat generic:** The suggestions for future work, while relevant, lack concrete specifics and are somewhat broad.
*   **Emphasis on positive aspects; needs more critical assessment:** The paper generally emphasizes the positive aspects of LLMs in bioinformatics and could benefit from a more critical assessment of potential biases, limitations, and ethical considerations.

**Justification for the Score:**

I am assigning a score of **7**.

**Rationale:** This paper is a valuable resource for the bioinformatics community, particularly for those new to LLMs. It provides a well-structured overview of how prompt engineering is being applied to biological sequence analysis. Its comprehensiveness, clear writing, and identification of key challenges contribute significantly to its usefulness. However, its limited technical depth, lack of comparative analysis, and slightly generic future directions prevent it from achieving a higher score. While it doesn't introduce groundbreaking new research, it effectively synthesizes existing work and highlights promising avenues for future investigation, making it a worthwhile contribution to the field. The score reflects the survey's merit as a comprehensive overview, balanced against the need for more critical evaluation and in-depth analysis.
Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Dynamic Benchmarking of Reasoning Capabilities in Code Large Language Models Under Data Contamination
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.04149v1)
- **Authors**: Simin Chen, Pranav Pusarla, Baishakhi Ray
- **Abstract**: The rapid evolution of code largelanguage models underscores the need for effective and transparent benchmarking of their reasoning capabilities. However, the current benchmarking approach heavily depends on publicly available, human-created datasets. The widespread use of these fixed benchmark datasets makes the benchmarking process to be static and thus particularly susceptible to data contamination, an unavoidable consequence of the extensive data collection processes used to train Code LLMs. Existing approaches that address data contamination often suffer from human effort limitations and imbalanced problem complexity. To tackle these challenges, we propose \tool, a novel benchmarking suite for evaluating Code LLMs under potential data contamination. Given a seed programming problem, \tool employs multiple agents to extract and modify the context without altering the core logic, generating semantically equivalent variations. We introduce a dynamic data generation methods and conduct empirical studies on two seed datasets across 21 Code LLMs. Results show that \tool effectively benchmarks reasoning capabilities under contamination risks while generating diverse problem sets to ensure consistent and reliable evaluations.
- **Summary**: Here is a concise summary and a rigorous critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces DyCodeEval, a novel benchmarking suite for code large language models (LLMs) designed to address the problem of data contamination in existing static benchmarks. DyCodeEval dynamically generates semantically diverse programming problems with controlled complexity by modifying the context of seed problems using LLM-based agents. This approach aims to provide a more reliable and transparent evaluation of the reasoning capabilities of code LLMs, mitigating the risk of inflated performance due to models memorizing training data. The authors conduct empirical studies on contaminated models and real-world models, demonstrating that DyCodeEval effectively reflects the reasoning capabilities of code LLMs and provides stable benchmarking results, even under data contamination scenarios. They also propose a new metric called DivPass that leveraged the dynamic nature of DyCodeEval and is more robust than the Pass@K metric.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The core idea of dynamically generating benchmarks to combat data contamination is not entirely new. There are existing works such as DyVal and TreeEval, and LiveCodeBench that have attempted to address this issue. However, DyCodeEval differentiates itself through its specific approach of using LLM agents to modify the *context* of existing problems while preserving the core *algorithmic complexity*. This separation of concerns (context vs. algorithm) is a key contribution, offering a more controlled way to generate diverse benchmarks. The use of metamorphic testing principles to generate problems is also a relatively novel application in the code LLM evaluation domain. The proposal of DivPass as a new metric that considers the diversity of benchmark problems is also a novel idea.

**Significance:**

The problem of data contamination in LLM benchmarks is a real and growing concern. If models are evaluated on data they have seen during training, it gives a false impression of their true capabilities. DyCodeEval directly addresses this problem by providing a method for generating benchmarks that are less likely to be contaminated. The experimental results demonstrate that DyCodeEval can reveal the inflated performance caused by data contamination in traditional benchmarks. This is valuable for researchers and developers who want to accurately assess the capabilities of code LLMs. Also, the empirical result that the QWEN2.5-CODER-7B model consistently falls outside the 95% confidence interval of the regression area is significant and can potentially lead to further work to understand the model's behavior.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the problem of data contamination and the limitations of existing benchmarking approaches.
*   **Well-Defined Methodology:** The DyCodeEval methodology is well-defined, with clear descriptions of the different agents and the overall workflow.
*   **Empirical Validation:** The authors conduct thorough empirical studies, including experiments on contaminated models and real-world models.
*   **New Robust Metric:** The DivPass metric is a robust metric for assessing code LLM's reasoning capabilities.
*   **Open Sourced Tool:** The method will contribute to the LLM benchmark community.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Reliance on LLMs:** The method relies heavily on the capabilities of a strong LLM for scenario generation and validation. The quality and consistency of DyCodeEval are therefore directly tied to the performance of the underlying LLM. A change in the underlying LLM can lead to changes in DyCodeEval.
*   **Computational Cost:** Using LLMs to generate benchmarks is computationally expensive, which can limit the scalability and accessibility of the approach.
*   **Potential Bias:** There is a risk of introducing bias during context generation and validation if the LLM is not carefully prompted.
*   **Human Verification:** The human evaluation rate is relatively low, only up to 95%.
*   **Limited Benchmark:** The benchmark is only tested on MBPP and HumanEval.
*   **Potential for Leakage:** Although the paper has taken the consideration to avoid the potential for information leakage, one can still identify potential leakage.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a valuable contribution to the field of code LLM benchmarking by addressing the important problem of data contamination. The DyCodeEval methodology is well-defined and empirically validated, and is an improvement on existing dynamic benchmark generating methodology due to the separation between complexity and problem context. While the approach has some limitations related to computational cost and reliance on LLMs, the potential benefits of more accurate and reliable evaluations outweigh these drawbacks. The paper introduces DivPass, a new metric that helps address the lack of code benchmarks. It is significant to have a robust and reliable metric that is resistant to data contamination. Furthermore, I believe the approach is beneficial and insightful to the code LLM community.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 7/10

### Ticktack : Long Span Temporal Alignment of Large Language Models Leveraging Sexagenary Cycle Time Expression
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.04150v1)
- **Authors**: Xue Han, Qian Hu, Yitong Wang, Wenchun Gao, Lianlian Zhang, Qing Wang, Lijun Mei, Chao Deng, Junlan Feng
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) suffer from temporal misalignment issues especially across long span of time. The issue arises from knowing that LLMs are trained on large amounts of data where temporal information is rather sparse over long times, such as thousands of years, resulting in insufficient learning or catastrophic forgetting by the LLMs. This paper proposes a methodology named "Ticktack" for addressing the LLM's long-time span misalignment in a yearly setting. Specifically, we first propose to utilize the sexagenary year expression instead of the Gregorian year expression employed by LLMs, achieving a more uniform distribution in yearly granularity. Then, we employ polar coordinates to model the sexagenary cycle of 60 terms and the year order within each term, with additional temporal encoding to ensure LLMs understand them. Finally, we present a temporal representational alignment approach for post-training LLMs that effectively distinguishes time points with relevant knowledge, hence improving performance on time-related tasks, particularly over a long period. We also create a long time span benchmark for evaluation. Experimental results prove the effectiveness of our proposal.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "Ticktack," a methodology designed to address temporal misalignment issues in Large Language Models (LLMs), especially across long time spans. This problem stems from the sparse and uneven distribution of temporal information in the vast training datasets of LLMs. Ticktack proposes using the sexagenary cycle (a 60-year cycle used in Chinese calendars) instead of the Gregorian calendar to achieve a more uniform distribution of years.  It uses polar coordinates to represent the sexagenary cycle and incorporates temporal encoding to enhance LLM comprehension. A temporal representational alignment approach is then applied through post-training to distinguish time points, aiming to improve performance on time-related tasks. The authors also create a new long-span benchmark dataset, TempLS, for evaluation. Experiments on various open-source LLMs demonstrate the effectiveness of Ticktack.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a reasonably novel approach. The use of the sexagenary cycle is an interesting idea to provide a more uniform temporal representation compared to the Gregorian calendar.  The polar coordinate encoding and temporal alignment techniques are also relatively unique within this specific context of long-span temporal reasoning for LLMs. While there are existing methods for temporal alignment, this paper's method appears to be well targeted towards the specifics of long range time periods and the distribution challenges presented by the nature of such data.

*   **Significance:** The issue of temporal misalignment in LLMs is significant and recognized as a limitation affecting their performance on tasks requiring temporal reasoning. The proposed methodology tackles this problem directly by addressing the uneven distribution of training data, which can result in insufficient or catastrophic forgetting for LLMs. Improving temporal alignment could have a broad impact on applications relying on LLMs, such as historical analysis, event forecasting, and knowledge base maintenance.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Problem Definition:** The paper clearly defines the problem of long-span temporal misalignment in LLMs and provides a solid rationale for addressing it.
    *   **Methodology:**  The proposed Ticktack methodology is well-structured, with clear explanations of each component (sexagenary cycle representation, polar coordinate encoding, temporal alignment).
    *   **Empirical Evaluation:** The paper includes experimental results on multiple open-source LLMs and a newly created benchmark dataset (TempLS), demonstrating the effectiveness of Ticktack in improving performance on time-related tasks.
    *   **Ablation study:** The ablation study strengthens the paper by showing that the temporal encoding module really helps when using the sexagenary year representation.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Dataset Limitations:**  While the TempLS dataset is a valuable contribution, its reliance on Baidu Baike raises potential concerns about bias and generalizability to other cultural or information domains. The limited sample size is another weakness, but it is clearly identified by the authors.
    *   **Scope:**  The paper primarily focuses on year-level granularity. While the authors acknowledge this limitation, extending the approach to finer granularities (months, days) would increase its applicability.
    *   **Limited baselines**: The baselines mainly focus on the original models, not the adapted ones. It would be beneficial to add a temporal alignment method of LLM, especially on long temporal span.
    *   **Method Applicability:** A main disadvantage is the proposed method can only be applied to Chinese related tasks.

*   **Potential Impact:** If the method proves robust and generalizable across datasets and LLMs, it could lead to improved temporal reasoning abilities in LLMs, influencing various applications and further research on temporal knowledge representation and alignment. This study inspires a novel approach to represent timestamps leveraging an interesting source.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:**

The paper offers a novel approach to a significant problem in the field of LLMs: temporal misalignment. It proposes a well-defined methodology (Ticktack) and provides empirical evidence demonstrating its effectiveness. However, limitations regarding dataset scope, granularity, and focus on Chinese related tasks temper the paper's overall impact. Therefore, while it represents a valuable contribution, its relatively narrow scope and the noted dataset limitations prevent it from receiving a higher score.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### KidneyTalk-open: No-code Deployment of a Private Large Language Model with Medical Documentation-Enhanced Knowledge Database for Kidney Disease
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.04153v1)
- **Authors**: Yongchao Long, Chao Yang, Gongzheng Tang, Jinwei Wang, Zhun Sui, Yuxi Zhou, Shenda Hong, Luxia Zhang
- **Abstract**: Privacy-preserving medical decision support for kidney disease requires localized deployment of large language models (LLMs) while maintaining clinical reasoning capabilities. Current solutions face three challenges: 1) Cloud-based LLMs pose data security risks; 2) Local model deployment demands technical expertise; 3) General LLMs lack mechanisms to integrate medical knowledge. Retrieval-augmented systems also struggle with medical document processing and clinical usability. We developed KidneyTalk-open, a desktop system integrating three technical components: 1) No-code deployment of state-of-the-art (SOTA) open-source LLMs (such as DeepSeek-r1, Qwen2.5) via local inference engine; 2) Medical document processing pipeline combining context-aware chunking and intelligent filtering; 3) Adaptive Retrieval and Augmentation Pipeline (AddRep) employing agents collaboration for improving the recall rate of medical documents. A graphical interface was designed to enable clinicians to manage medical documents and conduct AI-powered consultations without technical expertise. Experimental validation on 1,455 challenging nephrology exam questions demonstrates AddRep's effectiveness: achieving 29.1% accuracy (+8.1% over baseline) with intelligent knowledge integration, while maintaining robustness through 4.9% rejection rate to suppress hallucinations. Comparative case studies with the mainstream products (AnythingLLM, Chatbox, GPT4ALL) demonstrate KidneyTalk-open's superior performance in real clinical query. KidneyTalk-open represents the first no-code medical LLM system enabling secure documentation-enhanced medical Q&A on desktop. Its designs establishes a new framework for privacy-sensitive clinical AI applications. The system significantly lowers technical barriers while improving evidence traceability, enabling more medical staff or patients to use SOTA open-source LLMs conveniently.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "KidneyTalk-open: No-code Deployment of a Private Large Language Model with Medical Documentation-Enhanced Knowledge Database for Kidney Disease":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces KidneyTalk-open, a desktop application designed for privacy-preserving medical decision support in the area of kidney disease. The system aims to enable localized deployment of large language models (LLMs) with enhanced clinical reasoning capabilities by offering a no-code solution. It integrates three key components: (1) easy deployment of open-source LLMs like DeepSeek-r1 and Qwen2.5 using a local inference engine, (2) a medical document processing pipeline for creating a knowledge database with context-aware chunking and filtering, and (3) an adaptive retrieval and augmentation pipeline (AddRep) to improve the recall of medical documents. A user-friendly graphical interface facilitates document management and AI-powered consultations without requiring technical expertise. Experimental results on nephrology exam questions and comparative case studies demonstrate the system's effectiveness in integrating knowledge, suppressing hallucinations, and performing superior to other localized LLM applications.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Addressing a Practical Need:** The paper tackles a crucial problem: enabling privacy-sensitive and accessible AI-driven medical support, particularly in resource-constrained environments. The no-code deployment aspect directly addresses the challenge of limited technical expertise among clinicians.
    *   **Technical Innovation:** The integration of various components, especially the AddRep pipeline, is technically sound. The use of multi-agent collaboration to refine queries and improve knowledge retrieval seems promising, and the experimental results confirm this. The architecture also emphasizes practical considerations, like document chunking, filtering, and HNSW indexing.
    *   **Experimental Validation:** The use of the Chinese Nephrology Medical Exam MCQ dataset (CNME-MCQ) provides a standardized benchmark for evaluating performance. The comparative case studies with existing systems highlight the advantages of KidneyTalk-open in real-world clinical scenarios.
    *   **Privacy Focus:** The emphasis on localized deployment and privacy is a significant strength. This is increasingly important in healthcare applications where data security is paramount.
    *   **Open Source Approach:** Making the software and development code open-source promotes reproducibility, collaboration, and further innovation within the medical AI community.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Scope:** While KidneyTalk-open focuses on kidney disease, its generalizability to other medical domains is unclear. The paper would benefit from discussing how the system can be adapted for other specialties.
    *   **Dependency on Specific LLMs:** The system's reliance on particular open-source LLMs might limit its flexibility. A more modular design that allows for easier integration of other LLMs would be valuable.
    *   **MCQ Validation Limitations:** While the CNME-MCQ dataset provides quantitative results, it does not fully capture the complexity of real-world clinical decision-making. More comprehensive qualitative evaluations with clinicians would strengthen the validation.
    *   **Generalisability of results**: Experiments are performed on a Chinese Nephrology Medical Exam, but it is not sure whether the same conclusions would be achieved for other languages or medical domains.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

    The novelty lies in the *integration* of several existing technologies (open-source LLMs, vector databases, RAG techniques) into a *user-friendly, no-code* system for *privacy-preserving* medical Q&A. The specific focus on local deployment and document-enhanced medical knowledge makes KidneyTalk-open a significant contribution to the field. While individual components may not be entirely novel, the combination and practical implementation are. By addressing the accessibility barriers to AI in healthcare, this work has the potential to impact clinical practice positively.
    This offers an alternative approach to the privacy concerns of cloud-based LLMs, and the knowledge retrieval mechanisms.

**Score Justification:**

The paper makes a tangible contribution by developing a practical tool to increase the accessibility of LLMs in a medical environment. While individual components are not groundbreaking, the practical application, emphasis on user experience, local deployment, and the evaluation of multiple components leads me to believe that it is worthy of a high rating.

### Semantic Retrieval Augmented Contrastive Learning for Sequential Recommendation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.04162v1)
- **Authors**: Ziqiang Cui, Yunpeng Weng, Xing Tang, Xiaokun Zhang, Dugang Liu, Shiwei Li, Peiyang Liu, Bowei He, Weihong Luo, Xiuqiang He, Chen Ma
- **Abstract**: Sequential recommendation aims to model user preferences based on historical behavior sequences, which is crucial for various online platforms. Data sparsity remains a significant challenge in this area as most users have limited interactions and many items receive little attention. To mitigate this issue, contrastive learning has been widely adopted. By constructing positive sample pairs from the data itself and maximizing their agreement in the embedding space,it can leverage available data more effectively. Constructing reasonable positive sample pairs is crucial for the success of contrastive learning. However, current approaches struggle to generate reliable positive pairs as they either rely on representations learned from inherently sparse collaborative signals or use random perturbations which introduce significant uncertainty. To address these limitations, we propose a novel approach named Semantic Retrieval Augmented Contrastive Learning (SRA-CL), which leverages semantic information to improve the reliability of contrastive samples. SRA-CL comprises two main components: (1) Cross-Sequence Contrastive Learning via User Semantic Retrieval, which utilizes large language models (LLMs) to understand diverse user preferences and retrieve semantically similar users to form reliable positive samples through a learnable sample synthesis method; and (2) Intra-Sequence Contrastive Learning via Item Semantic Retrieval, which employs LLMs to comprehend items and retrieve similar items to perform semantic-based item substitution, thereby creating semantically consistent augmented views for contrastive learning. SRA-CL is plug-and-play and can be integrated into standard sequential recommendation models. Extensive experiments on four public datasets demonstrate the effectiveness and generalizability of the proposed approach.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary and a critical evaluation of the paper "Semantic Retrieval Augmented Contrastive Learning for Sequential Recommendation".

**Summary**

The paper addresses the data sparsity problem in sequential recommendation by proposing a novel contrastive learning framework called Semantic Retrieval Augmented Contrastive Learning (SRA-CL).  SRA-CL enhances contrastive learning by using Large Language Models (LLMs) to retrieve semantically similar users and items, thereby creating more reliable positive sample pairs for both cross-sequence and intra-sequence contrastive learning. The framework consists of two main components: 1) Cross-Sequence Contrastive Learning via User Semantic Retrieval, which leverages LLMs to understand user preferences and synthesize positive samples from semantically similar users. 2) Intra-Sequence Contrastive Learning via Item Semantic Retrieval, which employs LLMs to comprehend items and substitute them with similar items to generate semantically consistent augmented views. The paper demonstrates the effectiveness and generalizability of SRA-CL through extensive experiments on four public datasets.

**Critical Evaluation**

**Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its specific application of LLMs to improve *contrastive learning* in sequential recommendation, rather than using LLMs as direct recommenders or as simple feature extractors.  The use of semantic retrieval to build more reliable positive pairs in both cross-sequence and intra-sequence contrastive learning is a key contribution. Existing contrastive methods rely on sparse signals or random augmentations, while this paper leverages LLMs to bring in external semantic knowledge to generate reliable positive samples. To my knowledge, using LLMs for *retrieval-augmented* contrastive learning in the *specific* context of sequential recommendation has not been explored.

**Significance:** The paper addresses a critical problem in sequential recommendation (data sparsity) and offers a potentially effective solution.  If the proposed framework is genuinely model-agnostic and leads to consistent improvements across different datasets and base models (which the paper attempts to demonstrate), it can significantly impact practical recommendation systems.  The results suggest a method for more effectively leveraging LLMs to enhance existing recommendation models, which is a valuable direction for research.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies the limitations of existing contrastive learning approaches in sequential recommendation.
*   **Well-Defined Solution:** SRA-CL is well-motivated and clearly explained. The two main components are logically sound and address specific weaknesses of previous methods.
*   **Comprehensive Experiments:** The paper presents a thorough experimental evaluation on multiple datasets, comparing against a wide range of baselines (including other contrastive learning methods and LLM-based approaches).
*   **Ablation Study:**  The ablation study effectively demonstrates the contribution of each component of SRA-CL.
*   **Model-Agnostic Validation:** The validation of SRA-CL's model-agnostic characteristic strengthens the impact of its work.
*   **Robustness Analysis:** Examining the method's performance under varying degrees of data sparsity enhances the paper's practical value.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Reliance on LLM APIs:** The paper relies on a public LLM API (DeepSeek-V3) and does not fine-tune a model specifically for the task. This could limit the method's reproducibility, given API changes and cost implications. While SimCSE-ROBERTa, an open-source model, is used for text embeddings, the reliance on an external LLM API is a notable dependency. It's difficult to quantify the cost/benefit trade-offs.  The paper acknowledges this limitation. More justification of the trade-offs inherent in the architecture chosen would improve the paper.
*   **Limited Details on LLM Prompting:**  While the prompt templates are shown in Figure 3, the paper could provide more detail on the experimentation conducted to arrive at these specific prompts. The choice of prompts significantly affects the quality of the semantic information extracted by the LLM, and further discussion would be beneficial.
*   **Marginal gains on some datasets:** While the gains are generally consistent, they are sometimes marginal on some datasets (especially Office in terms of HR@20), which might raise questions about its effectiveness in all scenarios.
*   **Impact of hyperparameter Tuning is small:** The Hyperparameter study indicates that the effect on model performance is small. Thus, it's not clear how the method is sensitive to the choice of parameters.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a valuable contribution to the field of sequential recommendation by proposing a novel and well-motivated framework for contrastive learning. The use of LLMs to address the limitations of existing contrastive methods is a promising direction. The thorough experimental evaluation and ablation study provide strong evidence of the effectiveness of SRA-CL.

However, the reliance on external LLM APIs (particularly DeepSeek-V3), limits its reproducibility, and the gains compared to SOTA baselines, while consistent, are not always overwhelming. These limitations prevent the paper from achieving a higher score.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.IR
- **Score**: 7/10

### TIMER: Temporal Instruction Modeling and Evaluation for Longitudinal Clinical Records
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.04176v1)
- **Authors**: Hejie Cui, Alyssa Unell, Bowen Chen, Jason Alan Fries, Emily Alsentzer, Sanmi Koyejo, Nigam Shah
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have emerged as promising tools for assisting in medical tasks, yet processing Electronic Health Records (EHRs) presents unique challenges due to their longitudinal nature. While LLMs' capabilities to perform medical tasks continue to improve, their ability to reason over temporal dependencies across multiple patient visits and time frames remains unexplored. We introduce TIMER (Temporal Instruction Modeling and Evaluation for Longitudinal Clinical Records), a framework that incorporate instruction-response pairs grounding to different parts of a patient's record as a critical dimension in both instruction evaluation and tuning for longitudinal clinical records. We develop TIMER-Bench, the first time-aware benchmark that evaluates temporal reasoning capabilities over longitudinal EHRs, as well as TIMER-Instruct, an instruction-tuning methodology for LLMs to learn reasoning over time. We demonstrate that models fine-tuned with TIMER-Instruct improve performance by 7.3% on human-generated benchmarks and 9.2% on TIMER-Bench, indicating that temporal instruction-tuning improves model performance for reasoning over EHR.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "TIMER: Temporal Instruction Modeling and Evaluation for Longitudinal Clinical Records":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces TIMER, a framework for evaluating and improving the temporal reasoning capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs) when processing longitudinal clinical records (Electronic Health Records, EHRs). TIMER consists of two components:

1.  **TIMER-Bench:**  A benchmark designed to evaluate temporal reasoning. It involves generating instruction-response pairs with explicit temporal evidence, enabling assessment of LLMs across different points in a patient's timeline. Unlike existing benchmarks, TIMER-Bench allows for controlled sampling of instructions with different temporal distributions (e.g., recency-focused, edge-focused, uniform).

2.  **TIMER-Instruct:**  A methodology for instruction tuning that aims to improve an LLM's longitudinal reasoning through temporal-aware training data. It involves generating instruction-response pairs that are grounded to different parts of the EHR and exploring the effects of different temporal distributions of training data.

The paper highlights that existing benchmarks often suffer from limited temporal coverage and recent-context bias. Through experiments, the authors demonstrate that models fine-tuned with TIMER-Instruct show improved performance on both human-generated benchmarks and the TIMER-Bench, suggesting enhanced temporal reasoning skills.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper demonstrates novelty in several key areas:
    *   **Explicit focus on temporal reasoning:** While LLMs have been applied to healthcare, the explicit focus on evaluating and improving *temporal* reasoning is relatively new. Most prior work has focused on tasks like question answering or knowledge retrieval, without deeply considering how models handle longitudinal data and temporal dependencies.
    *   **TIMER-Bench's controlled temporal distributions:**  The ability to evaluate models with instructions sampled from different temporal distributions is a significant advancement. This allows for a more nuanced understanding of how models perform across the entire patient timeline, rather than being biased toward recent events.
    *   **TIMER-Instruct and its exploration of temporal distributions in training data:** The methodology for instruction tuning, coupled with the investigation of how different training data distributions impact performance, is a valuable contribution.  It moves beyond simple instruction tuning and starts to address the specific challenges of longitudinal data.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a critical limitation of LLMs in healthcare: their ability to reason over time. EHRs are inherently longitudinal, and the ability to synthesize information across multiple visits and time frames is crucial for many clinical tasks. By developing TIMER, the authors are providing a tool and a methodology that can help bridge the gap between isolated question-answering performance and practical utility in healthcare settings. The results show promising improvements through temporal-aware instruction tuning, demonstrating the potential of this approach. The effort of contributing the code and benchmark is also commendable and should encourage more research in the area.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Well-defined problem and clear methodology:** The paper clearly articulates the challenges of temporal reasoning and presents a well-defined framework to address them.
    *   **Thorough experimental evaluation:**  The authors conduct a comprehensive set of experiments, comparing different baseline models and instruction-tuning strategies on both human-generated and model-generated benchmarks. The use of multiple evaluation metrics and the validation of the LLM-as-Judge with human annotations strengthen the results.
    *   **Practical relevance:** The paper focuses on a real-world problem with significant implications for healthcare.
    *   **Open source:** The authors state that they will release their code and benchmark data. This will allow other researchers to build upon their work and contribute to further advancements in the field.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Reliance on model-generated data:**  While the authors use clinical validation to ensure the quality of the model-generated benchmarks, there's always a risk that the generated data might not fully capture the complexities and nuances of real-world clinical records. A more significant investment in human annotation could be beneficial.
    *   **Limited scope of the study:** The focus is primarily on a single institution's EHR data.  It would be valuable to evaluate the framework on data from other healthcare systems to assess its generalizability.
    *   **The temporal aspects of the approach could have been explored in more detail** The authors could have gone deeper into studying the type of temporal relations captured by the method.

*   **Potential Influence:** The TIMER framework has the potential to significantly influence the field by:
    *   Encouraging further research on temporal reasoning in healthcare.
    *   Providing a valuable tool for evaluating and improving LLMs for longitudinal clinical data.
    *   Inspiring the development of new instruction-tuning methodologies that are specifically tailored to the challenges of temporal data.
    *   Giving more emphasis in the research community on the temporal aspects of long-context models.

**Score: 8**

**Justification:**

The paper makes a novel and significant contribution to the field of LLMs in healthcare by focusing on the critical problem of temporal reasoning and provides a well-designed framework to address it. The experimental results are promising, and the potential impact on clinical applications is substantial. While there are some limitations, the strengths of the paper outweigh its weaknesses, making it a valuable and influential contribution. The score would have been even higher had the human validation been more extensive and there has been a stronger focus on the temporal relations captured by the method.
- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 8/10

### Measuring temporal effects of agent knowledge by date-controlled tool use
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.04188v1)
- **Authors**: R. Patrick Xian, Qiming Cui, Stefan Bauer, Reza Abbasi-Asl
- **Abstract**: Temporal progression is an integral part of knowledge accumulation and update. Web search is frequently adopted as grounding for agent knowledge, yet its inappropriate configuration affects the quality of agent responses. Here, we construct a tool-based out-of-sample testing framework to measure the knowledge variability of large language model (LLM) agents from distinct date-controlled tools (DCTs). We demonstrate the temporal effects of an LLM agent as a writing assistant, which can use web search to help complete scientific publication abstracts. We show that temporal effects of the search engine translates into tool-dependent agent performance but can be alleviated with base model choice and explicit reasoning instructions such as chain-of-thought prompting. Our results indicate that agent evaluation should take a dynamical view and account for the temporal influence of tools and the updates of external resources.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the temporal sensitivity of LLM agents equipped with web search tools. It proposes a "tool-based out-of-sample" (OOS) testing framework where the date parameters of the web search tool are manipulated to simulate different knowledge states of the agent. The task used for evaluation is scientific abstract completion, leveraging a newly constructed dataset (SciBreak) of scientific breakthroughs and associated publications. The experiments demonstrate that the temporal accuracy of the agent's responses is affected by the date-controlled search tool, with performance varying depending on whether the search is conducted before or after the publication date of the relevant scientific findings. The paper also explores the use of chain-of-thought prompting to improve the agent's ability to reason about the relevance of information from different time periods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper introduces a valuable framework for evaluating the temporal awareness and knowledge grounding of LLM agents. While OOS testing and temporal evaluation of LLMs exist, the focus on tool-based manipulation and the specific application to scientific knowledge present a novel approach.  The SciBreak dataset is a useful contribution.  However, the specific task (abstract completion) and the implementation (ReAct agent with Google Search API) are relatively standard.

* **Significance:** The findings highlight a crucial vulnerability of LLM agents: their dependence on the temporal accuracy of external tools. The paper's results underscore the need for careful consideration of the temporal grounding of knowledge when deploying LLM agents in applications that require up-to-date information, such as scientific research or real-time decision-making.  The exploration of CoT prompting as a mitigation strategy is a positive step, although the improvements seem modest and not universally applicable.

* **Strengths:**
    * Well-defined framework with clear definitions (DCT, tool-based OOS test).
    * Creation of a relevant dataset (SciBreak).
    * Demonstrates a practically relevant problem (temporal sensitivity of LLM agents).
    * Addresses a critical aspect of LLM agent evaluation - dependence on the temporal properties of external tools.
    * Investigates one potential solution (CoT prompting), even if its effect is not universally beneficial.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * The observed effects, while relevant, are not dramatically large.
    * The study is largely empirical and lacks deep theoretical insights into the underlying mechanisms of temporal knowledge representation in LLMs.
    * The improvement from CoT is not consistent and seems to depend on model capacity. The conclusions about this mitigation are not especially strong.
    * Could benefit from more robust statistical analysis to determine the significance of the results.
    * Limited exploration of different types of temporal reasoning or more sophisticated approaches to date selection.

* **Potential Influence:**  The paper will likely stimulate further research on the temporal grounding of LLM agents and the development of more robust evaluation frameworks. The SciBreak dataset could serve as a valuable resource for future research in this area. The paper's findings could also influence the design of LLM agents for knowledge-intensive tasks, leading to more reliable and accurate systems. However, the impact might be limited due to the modest effect sizes observed.

**Rigorous Rationale for Score:**

I am assigning a score of **6**.

* **Justification:**  The paper presents a *novel* perspective on an important problem - the temporal sensitivity of LLM agents using a newly developed dataset. While existing work has examined similar topics, the focus on tool-based OOS testing and the application to scientific knowledge are innovative. The *significance* lies in highlighting the potential for inaccuracies in LLM agent outputs due to reliance on outdated or biased information from external tools. However, the *magnitude* of the observed effects is not overwhelming, and the proposed solution (CoT prompting) shows limited and inconsistent improvements. The paper also lacks deeper theoretical analysis and more robust statistical validation. For these reasons it cannot be rated higher. Despite these limitations, the paper is well-written, clearly presents its methodology, and contributes to our understanding of the challenges involved in building temporally aware LLM agents. It has the potential to encourage additional investigation and advancement in this field.
Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### MASTER: Multimodal Segmentation with Text Prompts
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.04199v1)
- **Authors**: Fuyang Liu, Shun Lu, Jilin Mei, Yu Hu
- **Abstract**: RGB-Thermal fusion is a potential solution for various weather and light conditions in challenging scenarios. However, plenty of studies focus on designing complex modules to fuse different modalities. With the widespread application of large language models (LLMs), valuable information can be more effectively extracted from natural language. Therefore, we aim to leverage the advantages of large language models to design a structurally simple and highly adaptable multimodal fusion model architecture. We proposed MultimodAl Segmentation with TExt PRompts (MASTER) architecture, which integrates LLM into the fusion of RGB-Thermal multimodal data and allows complex query text to participate in the fusion process. Our model utilizes a dual-path structure to extract information from different modalities of images. Additionally, we employ LLM as the core module for multimodal fusion, enabling the model to generate learnable codebook tokens from RGB, thermal images, and textual information. A lightweight image decoder is used to obtain semantic segmentation results. The proposed MASTER performs exceptionally well in benchmark tests across various automated driving scenarios, yielding promising results.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces MASTER (Multimodal Segmentation with Text Prompts), a novel architecture for RGB-Thermal semantic segmentation. MASTER leverages large language models (LLMs) to fuse information from RGB, thermal images, and textual prompts. The architecture uses a dual-path ViT encoder to extract image features, projects these features into the LLM's language space, and then uses the LLM to generate learnable codebook tokens containing target information.  A lightweight image decoder is used to produce the final semantic segmentation results. The authors claim MASTER achieves state-of-the-art performance on the MFNet benchmark and demonstrates superior performance in segmenting small objects and reducing noise compared to previous methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The primary novelty of this paper lies in the integration of LLMs into the RGB-Thermal fusion pipeline for semantic segmentation. While other works have explored RGB-Thermal fusion, and LLMs have been used in various vision-language tasks, this is presented as the first attempt to combine these techniques specifically for this modality fusion task in autonomous driving. The dual-path Vision Transformer (ViT) structure for fusion, while not fundamentally new, is tailored to this specific problem and incorporates the LLM effectively.  The specific method of projecting visual features into the language space and using the LLM to generate codebook tokens is a significant contribution.

*   **Significance:** RGB-Thermal semantic segmentation is a crucial task for autonomous driving in challenging weather and lighting conditions. By improving the accuracy and robustness of this task, the paper contributes to the broader goal of safer and more reliable autonomous systems. The use of textual prompts to guide segmentation allows for greater flexibility and control over the system's behavior. The performance gains demonstrated on the MFNet dataset suggest that MASTER has the potential to be a valuable tool for researchers and practitioners in this field. The focus on segmenting smaller objects such as guardrails and bumps also points to enhanced safety awareness in the proposed method.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper clearly articulates the problem and motivates the proposed solution.
    *   The MASTER architecture is well-described and relatively simple, allowing for easier understanding and implementation.
    *   The experimental results demonstrate significant improvements over existing methods, particularly in segmenting small objects and reducing noise.
    *   The ablation studies (if any) provide insights into the contribution of different components of the architecture. (Not clear if the paper has ablation studies from the prompt.)

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The performance is only evaluated on one dataset (MFNet). While MFNet is a relevant benchmark, evaluating the performance on other datasets would strengthen the paper's claims.
    *   The paper could benefit from a more detailed analysis of the limitations of MASTER. For example, how does the performance vary with different types of textual prompts? How robust is the system to noisy or ambiguous prompts?  What are the computational costs associated with using an LLM in this context?
    *   The reliance on a pre-trained LLM raises questions about the generalizability and adaptability of the approach. Fine-tuning LORA may not be enough to handle edge cases. More ablation studies and comparisons using different backbone LLMs will increase the value of the study.
    *   The paper does not clearly describe whether the experiments were using LLM for zero-shot generalization or LLM fine-tuning for better result. It is better if the study can provide more insights of both approaches.
*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in RGB-Thermal semantic segmentation and multimodal fusion. The idea of leveraging LLMs to fuse information from different modalities is a promising one, and MASTER provides a concrete example of how this can be done effectively. The success of MASTER could inspire other researchers to explore similar approaches for other tasks and modalities.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel and significant contribution to the field of RGB-Thermal semantic segmentation. While the novelty comes more from combining pre-existing technologies, the specific manner in which they're combined (the use of an LLM-guided codebook) and the performance gains achieved justify a relatively high score. The paper also has some weaknesses, including limited evaluation and a lack of detailed analysis of limitations. Therefore, a score of 7 reflects both the strengths and weaknesses of the paper.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Knowledge-Decoupled Synergetic Learning: An MLLM based Collaborative Approach to Few-shot Multimodal Dialogue Intention Recognition
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.04201v1)
- **Authors**: Bin Chen, Yu Zhang, Hongfei Ye, Ziyi Huang, Hongyang Chen
- **Abstract**: Few-shot multimodal dialogue intention recognition is a critical challenge in the e-commerce domainn. Previous methods have primarily enhanced model classification capabilities through post-training techniques. However, our analysis reveals that training for few-shot multimodal dialogue intention recognition involves two interconnected tasks, leading to a seesaw effect in multi-task learning. This phenomenon is attributed to knowledge interference stemming from the superposition of weight matrix updates during the training process. To address these challenges, we propose Knowledge-Decoupled Synergetic Learning (KDSL), which mitigates these issues by utilizing smaller models to transform knowledge into interpretable rules, while applying the post-training of larger models. By facilitating collaboration between the large and small multimodal large language models for prediction, our approach demonstrates significant improvements. Notably, we achieve outstanding results on two real Taobao datasets, with enhancements of 6.37\% and 6.28\% in online weighted F1 scores compared to the state-of-the-art method, thereby validating the efficacy of our framework.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Knowledge-Decoupled Synergetic Learning (KDSL), a novel framework designed to improve few-shot multimodal dialogue intention recognition in e-commerce. The authors identify a "seesaw effect" in multi-task learning for this domain, caused by knowledge interference during training. KDSL addresses this by decoupling knowledge: using smaller models to generate interpretable rules (through Monte Carlo Tree Search) and applying post-training to larger models.  The framework combines the rule library generated by the smaller model with predictions from the fine-tuned larger model to achieve state-of-the-art results on two real-world Taobao datasets.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a well-articulated problem and a novel solution. The identified "seesaw effect" in multi-task multimodal learning is an important observation. While the use of MCTS for rule generation and fine-tuning MLLMs are individually known techniques, the synergistic combination and the specific application to few-shot e-commerce dialogue are novel. Decoupling knowledge and model training in this manner appears to be an effective approach. The idea of using smaller models to create rules and large models to generalize them has some potential.

*   **Significance:** The paper demonstrates significant performance improvements on real-world datasets, which is a strong indicator of its practical value. Few-shot learning is a critical area in MLLMs given the data scarcity issues that exist in specialized domains. The e-commerce domain benefits substantially from improved customer service. The paper shows potential to improve existing large language models by combining with decoupled knowledge.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem definition and motivation.
    *   Well-defined framework with a logical architecture.
    *   Strong experimental results on real-world datasets.
    *   Ablation studies that demonstrate the contribution of each component.
    * The paper provides clear evidence of the "seesaw effect" on multi-modal tasks.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   While the paper shows good performance, a deeper analysis of *why* the KDSL framework is effective would have been helpful. For instance, what types of rules are being generated and how do these interact with the large language model’s capabilities?
    *   The paper is somewhat light on implementation details. The Monte Carlo Tree Search (MCTS) algorithm could be described in more detail, specifically how the virtual MLLM evaluates the generated rules with a reward score and a confidence score.
    *   The specific type of data augmentation is horizontal flipping. The paper would benefit from discussion of how this type of augmentation affects the underlying multi-modal data.
    *   The work primarily focuses on Taobao e-commerce. While the results are encouraging, the generalizability of KDSL to other e-commerce platforms or domains should be discussed.

*   **Potential Influence:** The KDSL framework could influence future research in few-shot multimodal learning, especially for domain-specific applications. The idea of decoupling knowledge acquisition and model training may be applicable to other scenarios where multi-task interference is a problem. The method also shows an opportunity to combine smaller LLMs with larger LLMs for knowledge generation and transfer to improve multi-modal tasks.

*   **Rigorous Rationale:** The paper provides compelling evidence of the effectiveness of KDSL through both comparative and ablation studies. The gains demonstrated over existing methods are substantial, suggesting that the approach is more than an incremental improvement. The observation of the "seesaw effect" is also a significant contribution to the field and worth noting.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:**
The paper has a solid foundation with a novel combination of existing techniques to solve a current, relevant problem. The experimental results are impressive, supporting the claimed performance improvements. However, a more in-depth analysis of *why* the method works and a discussion of its limitations would make it even stronger. While well-executed, it lacks the theoretical depth or breakthrough innovation to warrant a higher score.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Energy-Guided Optimization for Personalized Image Editing with Pretrained Text-to-Image Diffusion Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.04215v1)
- **Authors**: Rui Jiang, Xinghe Fu, Guangcong Zheng, Teng Li, Taiping Yao, Xi Li
- **Abstract**: The rapid advancement of pretrained text-driven diffusion models has significantly enriched applications in image generation and editing. However, as the demand for personalized content editing increases, new challenges emerge especially when dealing with arbitrary objects and complex scenes. Existing methods usually mistakes mask as the object shape prior, which struggle to achieve a seamless integration result. The mostly used inversion noise initialization also hinders the identity consistency towards the target object. To address these challenges, we propose a novel training-free framework that formulates personalized content editing as the optimization of edited images in the latent space, using diffusion models as the energy function guidance conditioned by reference text-image pairs. A coarse-to-fine strategy is proposed that employs text energy guidance at the early stage to achieve a natural transition toward the target class and uses point-to-point feature-level image energy guidance to perform fine-grained appearance alignment with the target object. Additionally, we introduce the latent space content composition to enhance overall identity consistency with the target. Extensive experiments demonstrate that our method excels in object replacement even with a large domain gap, highlighting its potential for high-quality, personalized image editing.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel energy-guided optimization framework (EGO-Edit) for personalized image editing using pre-trained text-to-image diffusion models. The method aims to address challenges in existing approaches, such as shape mismatch issues and identity inconsistency, especially in cross-class object replacement scenarios.  EGO-Edit formulates the editing task as an optimization problem in the latent space, guided by both text and image energy functions.  It employs a coarse-to-fine strategy: initially using text guidance for overall semantic alignment, followed by feature-level image guidance for finer appearance alignment. The method also uses latent space content composition to improve identity preservation and gradient scaling techniques to maintain background information during the edit. The authors demonstrate the effectiveness of EGO-Edit through experiments, including object swapping and inpainting tasks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper demonstrates a good level of novelty.
    *   Formulating personalized image editing as a latent space optimization problem driven by *both* text and image energy functions is a valuable contribution. It moves away from simply relying on inversion or training.
    *   The coarse-to-fine strategy is also a useful innovation. Separating the semantic alignment (text) from fine-grained appearance matching (image features) helps to avoid common problems in the field.
    *   The latent space content composition addresses the critical issue of identity preservation, enhancing the coherence of edited images.
    *   However, the method builds upon existing techniques like SDS and classifier-free guidance. While the combination and adaptation are novel, the individual components are not entirely new.

*   **Significance:** The paper offers a significant contribution to the field of personalized image editing.
    *   The proposed method appears to handle difficult cases, particularly cross-class object replacement, better than prior techniques. This expands the practical applicability of diffusion-based image editing.
    *   The training-free nature of EGO-Edit is advantageous. It makes the method more accessible and adaptable to new objects or styles without requiring retraining.
    *   The results in the paper (both qualitative and quantitative) suggest that the method is effective and generates high-quality edited images.
    *   The ablation studies provide insights into the importance of each component, which helps to better understand and optimize the editing process.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The paper could benefit from a more thorough comparison against the latest and most competitive baselines, including those published very recently.
    *   The implementation details in the appendix should be clear and easy to reproduce.
    *   Computational costs and limitations are insufficiently discussed. While training-free is an advantage, the computational overhead of iterative latent space optimization needs to be analyzed.
    *   While the method addresses identity preservation, there might be specific challenging cases that the paper doesn't fully explore (e.g., dramatic pose changes of the object).

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in personalized image editing and diffusion models.  The framework offers a good alternative to training-based or inversion-based techniques. The proposed strategy of a combined text and image energy guidance can be useful in other diffusion-based tasks.

**Score:** 7.5

**Justification:** The paper provides a reasonably novel and significant contribution to the field of personalized image editing. The combination of latent space optimization with text and image energy guidance, coarse-to-fine strategy, and latent space content composition enhances both the quality and flexibility of editing results. The method's ability to handle challenging cases such as cross-class object replacement is a significant advantage. However, the paper could have been strengthened by a more comprehensive comparison against recent baselines, a more detailed discussion of computational costs and limitations, and deeper discussion of failure modes of the model.
