- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 5/10

### Mapping AI Benchmark Data to Quantitative Risk Estimates Through Expert Elicitation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.04299v1)
- **Authors**: Malcolm Murray, Henry Papadatos, Otter Quarks, Pierre-François Gimenez, Simeon Campos
- **Abstract**: The literature and multiple experts point to many potential risks from large language models (LLMs), but there are still very few direct measurements of the actual harms posed. AI risk assessment has so far focused on measuring the models' capabilities, but the capabilities of models are only indicators of risk, not measures of risk. Better modeling and quantification of AI risk scenarios can help bridge this disconnect and link the capabilities of LLMs to tangible real-world harm. This paper makes an early contribution to this field by demonstrating how existing AI benchmarks can be used to facilitate the creation of risk estimates. We describe the results of a pilot study in which experts use information from Cybench, an AI benchmark, to generate probability estimates. We show that the methodology seems promising for this purpose, while noting improvements that can be made to further strengthen its application in quantitative AI risk assessment.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores the nascent field of AI risk assessment, specifically focusing on how AI benchmark data, particularly from cybersecurity benchmarks like Cybench, can be translated into quantitative risk estimates.  The authors conduct a pilot study where cybersecurity experts estimate the probability of successful malware development given information about the capabilities of language models (LLMs) as measured by Cybench. They found significant divergence in expert opinions, noted a slight cyber uplift from current LLMs, and predicted a much more substantial uplift if LLMs could saturate Cybench. The paper aims to bridge the gap between abstract benchmark scores and real-world harm, arguing that risk models provide a framework for creating model evaluations and enabling cumulative progress. The authors use expert elicitation based on the IDEA protocol to gather and aggregate probability estimates.  The paper identifies limitations of the current methodology and suggests future research directions including more detailed risk models and evaluations that directly measure capabilities relevant to specific steps in the models.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper addresses a relevant and important gap in the field: the lack of direct connections between AI benchmarks and quantitative risk assessment. While expert elicitation is not a novel methodology in itself, its application to translating LLM benchmark results into cybersecurity risk probabilities is a worthwhile contribution. The idea of connecting AI capabilities, measured by benchmarks, to tangible real-world harms through expert assessment is conceptually valuable. However, the reliance on a single benchmark (Cybench) and the small sample size of experts limits the generalizability of the findings. While it offers a preliminary demonstration, it’s not groundbreaking in its methods. The core contribution lies in highlighting the *need* for such connections and demonstrating one way to forge them.

**Significance:** The significance of this paper lies in its potential to influence the development of AI risk assessment frameworks. By demonstrating a pathway for translating benchmark data into concrete risk estimates, the paper could encourage researchers and developers to create more operationally relevant benchmarks and more detailed risk models. The emphasis on understanding the link between capabilities and harms has important regulatory implications. If future AI regulation relies on real-world data rather than abstract capabilities, the methodology this paper introduces may be valuable.  However, the study's limited scope and the divergent expert opinions indicate that the area is far from mature, and much more work is required to establish reliable quantitative risk estimates. It serves as a crucial first step, calling for a shift from measuring model capabilities alone to directly measuring risk. However, the small sample size and the reliance on expert elicitation, which is subjective, means that the immediate impact on risk modeling is limited. The paper rightly acknowledges these limitations and suggests future research to address them. The paper sets the stage and proposes a concrete method that could, with improvements, prove to be quite useful in the future.

**Strengths:**

*   Addresses a critical gap in the AI safety research landscape.
*   Provides a clear and actionable methodology for connecting benchmarks to risk.
*   Highlights the importance of expert elicitation in AI risk assessment.
*   Identifies limitations and suggests future research directions.
*   Offers a practical example using a well-defined cybersecurity benchmark.

**Weaknesses:**

*   Small sample size of experts, limiting the statistical power and generalizability of the findings.
*   High degree of divergence among experts, indicating the need for more structured elicitation protocols and clearer risk scenarios.
*   Reliance on a single benchmark (Cybench) potentially biases the results.
*   Limited exploration of alternative risk modeling techniques.
*   The methodology relies on expert *estimates* rather than direct empirical evidence, which remains a significant limitation.

**Overall:** The paper represents a valuable initial step in a crucial direction. It offers a practical approach and highlights the challenges inherent in quantifying AI risk. However, the significant limitations – particularly the small sample size and the subjectivity of expert elicitation – mean it's not a definitive contribution. The paper lays a foundation for more rigorous research.

**Score: 6**

**Rationale:** The paper scores a 6 because while it tackles an important and timely problem with a relatively novel approach, it is significantly limited by its small-scale pilot nature, expert dependence, and data reliability. It provides a starting point for future research by highlighting the need for real-world data-based evaluations for AI models to prevent or at least mitigate potential risks. More work and validation are needed to solidify these preliminary results and make them actionable.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 6/10

### Malware Detection at the Edge with Lightweight LLMs: A Performance Evaluation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.04302v1)
- **Authors**: Christian Rondanini, Barbara Carminati, Elena Ferrari, Antonio Gaudiano, Ashish Kundu
- **Abstract**: The rapid evolution of malware attacks calls for the development of innovative detection methods, especially in resource-constrained edge computing. Traditional detection techniques struggle to keep up with modern malware's sophistication and adaptability, prompting a shift towards advanced methodologies like those leveraging Large Language Models (LLMs) for enhanced malware detection. However, deploying LLMs for malware detection directly at edge devices raises several challenges, including ensuring accuracy in constrained environments and addressing edge devices' energy and computational limits. To tackle these challenges, this paper proposes an architecture leveraging lightweight LLMs' strengths while addressing limitations like reduced accuracy and insufficient computational power. To evaluate the effectiveness of the proposed lightweight LLM-based approach for edge computing, we perform an extensive experimental evaluation using several state-of-the-art lightweight LLMs. We test them with several publicly available datasets specifically designed for edge and IoT scenarios and different edge nodes with varying computational power and characteristics.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and a critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the feasibility of using lightweight Large Language Models (LLMs) for malware detection at the edge. It addresses the challenges of deploying LLMs in resource-constrained environments and proposes an architecture that leverages their strengths while mitigating limitations like reduced accuracy and insufficient computational power. The authors perform an extensive experimental evaluation using several state-of-the-art lightweight LLMs on publicly available datasets designed for edge and IoT scenarios. The experiments assess accuracy, adaptability (cross-dataset validation), and feasibility (on-device performance) using Raspberry Pi 3 and Jetson Nano. The results show that fine-tuning is necessary to achieve satisfactory performance and that models like TinyBERT and TinyT5 are promising candidates for edge deployment, balancing accuracy and resource requirements.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Relevant Problem:** The problem of malware detection at the edge, particularly with the rise of IoT devices, is critical and timely.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper presents a comprehensive experimental evaluation of lightweight LLMs, covering various datasets, models, and performance metrics. The inclusion of cross-dataset validation and on-device performance assessment is commendable.
    *   **Systematic Approach:** The methodology is well-defined, systematically addressing key challenges related to feasibility, accuracy, and adaptability. The discussions around fine-tuning and the trade-offs involved are valuable.
    *   **Practical Insights:** The paper provides practical insights into selecting appropriate models and hardware for edge deployment, considering factors like model size, resource consumption, and classification time.
    *   **Clear Structure and Presentation:** The paper is well-organized and clearly written, making it accessible to researchers in the field.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Novelty (Architecture):** The core idea of using lightweight LLMs for malware detection is not entirely novel. While the paper presents a good evaluation, the proposed architecture itself seems incremental, focusing primarily on parameter tuning and model selection rather than introducing groundbreaking architectural innovations.
    *   **Dataset Dependence:** The paper acknowledges that performance is dataset-dependent, especially in the cross-validation experiments. This suggests that the models might not generalize well to completely unseen environments, and the study doesn't deeply explore how to address this domain shift. The transfer learning approach might require significant adaptation in real-world deployments.
    *   **Limited Depth of Architecture Customization:** The study primarily focuses on pre-trained models with some fine-tuning. Exploring custom architectures designed specifically for edge-based malware detection might yield more substantial improvements. The paper does not provide a comparative analysis to justify the approach adopted over the creation of new architectural approaches.
    *   **Scalability of Fine-tuning on Edges:** While the paper shows that good results can be achieved for fine-tuning on edge devices, there is limited discussion of the scalability of this process and its impact on long-term deployment. Would fine-tuning have to occur for all devices, or can transfer learning be used across similar deployments?

*   **Significance:**

The paper is significant because it provides a detailed performance analysis of lightweight LLMs in the context of edge-based malware detection.  While the architecture is incremental, the comprehensive experimental results and practical insights contribute to the knowledge base, making it a useful resource for researchers and practitioners interested in this area.  The work helps to identify promising models and highlight key considerations for deploying LLMs in resource-constrained environments.  It serves as a baseline for future research and development in this field.

*   **Novelty Analysis:**

The study’s architecture and evaluation methods are similar to existing approaches, and the study doesn't provide convincing justification for the adoption of well-known architectures and not building new ones. Because of the above weaknesses, the overall novelty is deemed moderate.

**Score: 6.5**

**Justification:**

The paper is well-executed and addresses an important problem, but its limited novelty (especially in architecture design) prevents it from receiving a higher score. While the systematic evaluation and practical insights are valuable, the paper could have been strengthened by exploring more innovative architectural solutions or delving deeper into the challenges of domain shift and scalability in real-world edge deployments.  The score reflects the paper's practical contributions and relevance to the field, counterbalanced by its incremental nature. The work could benefit from more in-depth analysis of transfer learning or novel architectural solutions for edge deployment to achieve high accuracy and performance metrics.

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 6/10

### Solving Word-Sense Disambiguation and Word-Sense Induction with Dictionary Examples
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.04328v1)
- **Authors**: Tadej Škvorc, Marko Robnik-Šikonja
- **Abstract**: Many less-resourced languages struggle with a lack of large, task-specific datasets that are required for solving relevant tasks with modern transformer-based large language models (LLMs). On the other hand, many linguistic resources, such as dictionaries, are rarely used in this context despite their large information contents. We show how LLMs can be used to extend existing language resources in less-resourced languages for two important tasks: word-sense disambiguation (WSD) and word-sense induction (WSI). We approach the two tasks through the related but much more accessible word-in-context (WiC) task where, given a pair of sentences and a target word, a classification model is tasked with predicting whether the sense of a given word differs between sentences. We demonstrate that a well-trained model for this task can distinguish between different word senses and can be adapted to solve the WSD and WSI tasks. The advantage of using the WiC task, instead of directly predicting senses, is that the WiC task does not need pre-constructed sense inventories with a sufficient number of examples for each sense, which are rarely available in less-resourced languages. We show that sentence pairs for the WiC task can be successfully generated from dictionary examples using LLMs. The resulting prediction models outperform existing models on WiC, WSD, and WSI tasks. We demonstrate our methodology on the Slovene language, where a monolingual dictionary is available, but word-sense resources are tiny.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenge of limited resources for NLP tasks in less-resourced languages, specifically focusing on word-sense disambiguation (WSD) and word-sense induction (WSI) in Slovene. It proposes a novel methodology leveraging pre-trained large language models (LLMs) to augment dictionary examples for training a word-in-context (WiC) classifier. The WiC classifier, trained on LLM-generated sentence pairs derived from dictionary definitions, is then used to perform WSD and WSI. The paper demonstrates that LLM-augmented data improves performance on WiC, WSD, and WSI tasks compared to using only dictionary snippets or existing resources. The key idea is to avoid the need for large, manually annotated datasets for WSD/WSI directly, instead creating a more easily-obtainable WiC dataset.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of using LLMs to generate sentence pairs from dictionary definitions to train a WiC classifier and then adapt that classifier for WSD/WSI has some novelty. While LLMs have been used for data augmentation before, this specific pipeline for WSD/WSI, particularly in the context of a less-resourced language and making use of commonly available linguistic resources such as dictionaries, is a noteworthy contribution.  The focus on leveraging the more easily obtainable WiC task as an intermediate step is also a valuable aspect. A strong emphasis should be placed on the "novelty" in how the tasks are performed, not just if it has been done. It is more impactful if a more difficult task is reformulated as an easier one, or solved differently, than the task being done.

*   **Significance:** The significance lies in providing a practical approach to improve NLP performance in less-resourced languages, where large, task-specific datasets are scarce.  By leveraging readily available dictionaries and LLMs, the methodology enables the creation of useful WSD and WSI models. The empirical results, demonstrating improved performance on Slovene, further strengthen the significance. Furthermore, the paper tackles an evaluation issue. Many WSD/WSI models need a unified vocabulary or sense inventory, and it is often not the case that vocabularies are unified in smaller, less-resourced languages. By using a WIC model, it may be possible to avoid using the unified inventories and get more reliable or accurate results.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The approach is well-motivated, addressing a real-world problem in NLP.
    *   The methodology is clearly explained and easy to follow.
    *   The use of LLMs for data augmentation is justified and appropriate.
    *   The WiC task provides a convenient intermediate step, circumventing the need for pre-constructed sense inventories.
    *   The empirical evaluation demonstrates the effectiveness of the approach, particularly in out-of-vocabulary scenarios.
    *   The qualitative analysis of LLM-generated sentences provides insights into the quality and limitations of the approach.
    *   The use of readily available dictionaries makes the method more readily applicable to other low-resource languages.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The evaluation is limited to the Slovene language. While this demonstrates the approach's potential, further validation on other less-resourced languages would strengthen the findings.
    *   The quality of the LLM-generated sentences is crucial for the success of the approach. The paper acknowledges potential errors in the generated sentences. While automatic filtering is applied, some errors may still persist and negatively impact performance.
    *   The performance gains are relative to a baseline using dictionary snippets. While this demonstrates the value of LLM augmentation, comparing against more sophisticated baselines (e.g., cross-lingual transfer learning) would provide a more comprehensive assessment.
    *   The evaluation methodology relies on aligning senses based on WiC scores, which may introduce some approximation errors. A more direct evaluation against a manually annotated WSD/WSI dataset (if available) would be ideal.
    * The data size limitations restrict the generalization of the results.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence research in NLP for less-resourced languages by providing a practical and effective data augmentation strategy. The methodology could be adopted and extended by other researchers to improve performance on various NLP tasks. The focus on readily available resources such as dictionaries further enhances the potential impact. The discussion about the impact of "OOV" items also showcases the value of the method, as this is an issue in less-resourced languages.

*   **Score Justification:**
    Given the novelty of the specific pipeline for WSD/WSI leveraging LLMs and dictionary definitions in the context of a less-resourced language, the clear methodology, the significant empirical results, and the potential influence on NLP research for less-resourced languages, but taking into account the limitation to a single language, the need for high-quality generated sentences, and the limited number of baseline comparisons, a score of **7.5** is appropriate. This reflects a solid contribution with demonstrated practical value, but also acknowledges areas for further research and improvement.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### The Challenge of Identifying the Origin of Black-Box Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.04332v1)
- **Authors**: Ziqing Yang, Yixin Wu, Yun Shen, Wei Dai, Michael Backes, Yang Zhang
- **Abstract**: The tremendous commercial potential of large language models (LLMs) has heightened concerns about their unauthorized use. Third parties can customize LLMs through fine-tuning and offer only black-box API access, effectively concealing unauthorized usage and complicating external auditing processes. This practice not only exacerbates unfair competition, but also violates licensing agreements. In response, identifying the origin of black-box LLMs is an intrinsic solution to this issue. In this paper, we first reveal the limitations of state-of-the-art passive and proactive identification methods with experiments on 30 LLMs and two real-world black-box APIs. Then, we propose the proactive technique, PlugAE, which optimizes adversarial token embeddings in a continuous space and proactively plugs them into the LLM for tracing and identification. The experiments show that PlugAE can achieve substantial improvement in identifying fine-tuned derivatives. We further advocate for legal frameworks and regulations to better address the challenges posed by the unauthorized use of LLMs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenge of identifying the origin of "black-box" large language models (LLMs) that have been fine-tuned and are accessed only through APIs. The authors highlight the problem of unauthorized use and violation of licensing agreements due to the lack of transparency in these black-box models. They first demonstrate the limitations of existing passive and proactive identification techniques.  Then, the authors propose a new proactive technique called PlugAE, which involves optimizing adversarial token embeddings and plugging them into the LLM for tracing and identification without altering model weights, thereby preserving utility. The paper provides theoretical analysis and experimental results to show that PlugAE improves the identification of fine-tuned LLM derivatives compared to existing methods.  Finally, it advocates for legal frameworks and regulations to better address challenges posed by LLM unauthorized use.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in the PlugAE technique. While adversarial example-based methods for identifying LLMs exist, the move from discrete token optimization to continuous token embedding optimization, coupled with a non-weight-altering "plug-in" mechanism, is a notable contribution. The theoretical analysis formalizing the limitations of existing AE-based methods under a unified loss function is also valuable. However, the use of adversarial examples for LLM fingerprinting, in general, isn't entirely new. The innovation resides in how adversarial methods are implemented and enhanced specifically for the fine-tuned derivatives identification problem.

**Significance:** The problem tackled by this paper is highly relevant. The increasing commercialization and unauthorized repurposing of LLMs make identifying their origins crucial for enforcing licenses, preventing unfair competition, and ensuring transparency. Showing how existing methods falter in the face of fine-tuning is significant, as it highlights a practical weakness. PlugAE's potential to improve identification robustness makes it a useful addition to the toolbox for LLM governance. The discussion around legal and regulatory frameworks adds a further dimension that contributes to the overall impact.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly defines the problem and its importance.
*   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The experiments are relatively comprehensive, covering different base models, suspect models, and APIs.
*   **Theoretical Justification:** Providing a theoretical analysis supporting PlugAE's advantages over existing methods strengthens the argument.
*   **Practical Considerations:** The emphasis on maintaining model utility is commendable. A watermarking system that significantly degrades performance will not be adopted.
*   **Addresses a timely and relevant issue** given the increase in the use of proprietary LLMs.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Real-World Validation:** While the experimental setup uses real-world black-box APIs, further validation in more diverse and realistic scenarios is needed. For example, the assumption that attackers rarely modify token embeddings requires further validation as they might fine-tune embedding layers to evade detection.
*   **Reliance on Specific Assumptions:** PlugAE's effectiveness relies on the assumption that the embedding layer is injective and the attacker does not significantly perturb the copyright token during fine-tuning. If these assumptions are violated, performance could degrade.  Furthermore, how well does the technique scale to larger models or different architectures?
*   **Potential for Circumvention:** A determined adversary could potentially discover and remove or neutralize PlugAE's embedded "watermark" with sufficient effort.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper provides a valuable contribution by addressing a critical challenge in the age of LLMs. While it builds upon existing adversarial example techniques, the proposed PlugAE method represents a significant improvement in terms of robustness and theoretical justification. The paper's comprehensive evaluation and consideration of practical factors further enhance its value. However, it is also important to recognize that more validation with real-world scenarios and different model architectures is required, as well as further exploring its robustness. The PlugAE is potentially vulnerable to a sufficiently resourceful adversary which leads to a reduction in the score. Taking these factors into account, the novelty and significance of the paper warrant a score of:

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 7/10

### In-depth Analysis of Graph-based RAG in a Unified Framework
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.04338v1)
- **Authors**: Yingli Zhou, Yaodong Su, Youran Sun, Shu Wang, Taotao Wang, Runyuan He, Yongwei Zhang, Sicong Liang, Xilin Liu, Yuchi Ma, Yixiang Fang
- **Abstract**: Graph-based Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) has proven effective in integrating external knowledge into large language models (LLMs), improving their factual accuracy, adaptability, interpretability, and trustworthiness. A number of graph-based RAG methods have been proposed in the literature. However, these methods have not been systematically and comprehensively compared under the same experimental settings. In this paper, we first summarize a unified framework to incorporate all graph-based RAG methods from a high-level perspective. We then extensively compare representative graph-based RAG methods over a range of questing-answering (QA) datasets -- from specific questions to abstract questions -- and examine the effectiveness of all methods, providing a thorough analysis of graph-based RAG approaches. As a byproduct of our experimental analysis, we are also able to identify new variants of the graph-based RAG methods over specific QA and abstract QA tasks respectively, by combining existing techniques, which outperform the state-of-the-art methods. Finally, based on these findings, we offer promising research opportunities. We believe that a deeper understanding of the behavior of existing methods can provide new valuable insights for future research.
- **Summary**: Here is a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "In-depth Analysis of Graph-based RAG in a Unified Framework":

**Summary:**

The paper presents a comprehensive analysis of graph-based Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) methods.  It introduces a unified framework to encapsulate various existing graph-based RAG techniques, facilitating a systematic comparison. The authors conduct extensive experiments across diverse question-answering datasets (specific and abstract questions) to evaluate the performance of representative graph-based RAG approaches. As a result of their analysis, the authors identify novel variants of these methods that outperform the existing state-of-the-art and propose promising research opportunities for the future. The paper emphasizes a deeper understanding of the behavior of existing methods to guide future research directions.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Unified Framework:** The paper's primary strength lies in its proposal of a unified framework for graph-based RAG. This framework provides a valuable abstraction that allows researchers to compare and contrast different techniques on a common footing, moving beyond ad-hoc comparisons and offering a more structured understanding.
*   **Comprehensive Experimental Analysis:** The paper features an extensive set of experiments across a variety of datasets, including both specific and abstract question types.  This breadth helps to reveal the strengths and weaknesses of different graph-based RAG methods in diverse scenarios. The evaluation metrics are appropriate, and the analysis is thorough.
*   **Identification of Novel Variants:** The authors successfully identify new hybrid approaches that outperform existing state-of-the-art methods. This demonstrates the value of their analysis and provides concrete contributions to the field.
*   **Focus on Abstract QA:** The paper's inclusion of abstract QA tasks is significant. This is an area that is relatively under-explored in the RAG literature, and the paper's findings shed light on the effectiveness of different graph-based methods for handling higher-level, conceptual questions.
*   **Open-Source Testbed:** The availability of the open-source testbed is a valuable contribution that promotes reproducibility and facilitates further research in this area.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Incremental Novelty in Methods:** While the identified method variants are promising, their core novelty is often an intelligent combination of existing techniques, and may be more a testament to the modularity and analysability enabled by the unified framework. This could limit the overall impact if seen as just a re-arrangement.
*   **Limited Exploration of Graph Quality:** The paper acknowledges that graph quality plays a crucial role but doesn't fully address evaluating graph quality *before* question answering takes place. This represents a missed opportunity for further analysis and potential methodological innovation.  Having a graph evaluation metric would add substantial value.
*   **Resource Limitations:** The authors acknowledge some resource constraints in terms of model size and dataset selection. While this is understandable, it does limit the scope of the study and the generalizability of some of the findings. Specifically, using higher-end models (GPT-4o), and better control for prompt tuning may significantly improve results for less robust methods.
*   **Dependence on LLMs for Graph Construction:** LLM-based graph construction is powerful, but potentially expensive. As an area of further work, they can include a study that considers non-LLM approaches to building graphs, or a cost-benefit calculation of LLM approaches.

**Significance and Potential Influence:**

This paper has the potential to significantly influence the field of RAG by:

*   Providing a clearer understanding of the design space of graph-based RAG methods.
*   Guiding future research towards more effective and adaptable RAG techniques.
*   Facilitating the development of new graph-based RAG applications in diverse domains.
*   Providing benchmark and source code to further improve the comparability of methods going forward.

**Overall:**

The paper offers a valuable contribution to the field through its systematic analysis and structured approach to evaluating graph-based RAG methods. The proposed framework enables insights that are crucial for the future and helps guide research in the area. Although there are resource based and novelty constraints, the methodological rigour and comprehensive nature of the work are commendable.

Score: 8

**Justification:** The paper merits a score of 8 because of its significant contributions to the field through its unified framework and systematic evaluation methodology. The paper identifies and analyzes various components of the RAG pipeline and provides the basis to build and test new architectures more reliably going forward. However, the absence of LLM-agnostic approaches to graph generation or analysis, limited exploration of LLM sizes (and a single "best" one), and a primary focus on existing architectures, rather than novel contributions, prevents it from achieving a higher score.

- **Classification**: cs.IR
- **Score**: 8/10

### LEDiT: Your Length-Extrapolatable Diffusion Transformer without Positional Encoding
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.04344v1)
- **Authors**: Shen Zhang, Yaning Tan, Siyuan Liang, Linze Li, Ge Wu, Yuhao Chen, Shuheng Li, Zhenyu Zhao, Caihua Chen, Jiajun Liang, Yao Tang
- **Abstract**: Diffusion transformers(DiTs) struggle to generate images at resolutions higher than their training resolutions. The primary obstacle is that the explicit positional encodings(PE), such as RoPE, need extrapolation which degrades performance when the inference resolution differs from training. In this paper, we propose a Length-Extrapolatable Diffusion Transformer(LEDiT), a simple yet powerful architecture to overcome this limitation. LEDiT needs no explicit PEs, thereby avoiding extrapolation. The key innovations of LEDiT are introducing causal attention to implicitly impart global positional information to tokens, while enhancing locality to precisely distinguish adjacent tokens. Experiments on 256x256 and 512x512 ImageNet show that LEDiT can scale the inference resolution to 512x512 and 1024x1024, respectively, while achieving better image quality compared to current state-of-the-art length extrapolation methods(NTK-aware, YaRN). Moreover, LEDiT achieves strong extrapolation performance with just 100K steps of fine-tuning on a pretrained DiT, demonstrating its potential for integration into existing text-to-image DiTs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "LEDIT: Your Length-Extrapolatable Diffusion Transformer without Positional Encoding":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces LEDiT, a novel diffusion transformer architecture designed to generate high-quality images at resolutions exceeding the training resolution.  LEDiT overcomes the limitations of traditional Diffusion Transformers (DiTs) that rely on positional encodings (PEs), which degrade performance when extrapolating to higher resolutions.  LEDiT eliminates explicit PEs and instead employs causal attention to implicitly encode positional information. Additionally, the architecture incorporates a low-cost convolution with a multi-dilation training strategy to enhance local fine-grained details. The authors demonstrate that LEDiT can generate images at 4x the training resolution with improved quality compared to existing length extrapolation methods. Further, they show that fine-tuning LEDiT on pretrained DiTs can achieve strong performance, suggesting ease of integration with existing text-to-image models.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of removing explicit positional encodings and using causal attention for implicit positional encoding in the context of diffusion transformers is novel.  The combination with the multi-dilation convolution strategy for enhancing locality adds another layer of innovation.  The theoretical justification provided for how causal attention can implicitly encode positional information through variance reduction is a strong point.

*   **Significance:** The ability to extrapolate diffusion models to higher resolutions is a significant problem in the field.  The quadratic cost of self-attention and limited availability of high-resolution datasets make it difficult to train models directly at high resolutions. LEDiT directly addresses this limitation, offering a practical approach to generating high-resolution images with lower computational costs and resource requirements during training.  The demonstration that it can be fine-tuned from pre-trained DiTs is also important since this facilitates integrating with existing powerful, general diffusion models.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Strong Experimental Results:** The paper provides compelling qualitative and quantitative results demonstrating that LEDiT outperforms existing methods (NTK-aware, YaRN) in length extrapolation.  The ablation studies offer a clear understanding of the contribution of each component (causal attention, convolution). They evaluate across a wide variety of aspects (resolutions, aspect ratios, training protocols) and baselines.
    *   **Theoretical Justification:**  The paper attempts to ground the approach with a formal explanation based on the statistical properties of the causal attention mechanism.
    *   **Practical Applicability:**  The method seems relatively easy to implement and the results show that it can be fine-tuned on top of existing DiT models.
    *   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-written and easy to follow, with clear explanations of the architecture and experimental setup.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope of Theoretical Justification:**  While the theoretical justification is a strong point, the assumptions made for the variance reduction proof may not always hold in practice, and the paper acknowledges the deviations from the theorem in later denoising steps. A more robust or empirically driven analysis of causal attention's implicit positional encoding would be valuable. It is not clear how implicit is the positional information (i.e., does it learn something useful or just something that seems like positional information?). The theory could be explored more fully.
    *   **Multi-dilation could be better explored.** They perform minimal ablation on the probability `p` that controls dilation. A more rigorous exploration of dilation parameter space could find better performing settings.
    *   **Dependency on Causal Attention:** The architecture relies heavily on causal attention and convolution. While the experiments show their effectiveness, the reliance is high. Exploring alternative attention mechanisms or even non-attention-based components that might offer better extrapolation capabilities or lower computational cost could be an area for future research.
    *   **Computational Burden for Evaluation:** The paper acknowledges the quadratic computational cost of evaluating the model at high resolutions. This limitation impacted the number of images generated for quantitative comparison (10k images for 512x512, while 50k is usually used for 256x256). This could potentially skew the comparisons, especially if the 10k sample set has lower variance.

*   **Potential Influence:** LEDiT has the potential to influence research on diffusion models by shifting the focus away from complex positional encodings and toward more implicit encoding mechanisms. The improved extrapolation capabilities could enable researchers to train models at lower resolutions, reducing computational costs and memory requirements, without sacrificing image quality. The ease of integration with pre-trained models further increases its potential impact.

**Score:** 7.5

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a novel architecture with a strong empirical evaluation demonstrating significant improvements in length extrapolation for diffusion transformers.  The implicit positional encoding through causal attention, combined with multi-dilation convolutions, is a clever approach that offers a practical solution to a challenging problem. The theoretical grounding is a plus, but could be strengthened. While there are some weaknesses regarding the scope of the theory, dependence on causal attention, and computational cost for rigorous evalution, the overall contribution is valuable and potentially impactful to the field. Therefore, a score of 7.5 reflects this strong contribution with room for future improvement and more rigorous analysis.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Large Language Models for Zero-shot Inference of Causal Structures in Biology
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.04347v1)
- **Authors**: Izzy Newsham, Luka Kovačević, Richard Moulange, Nan Rosemary Ke, Sach Mukherjee
- **Abstract**: Genes, proteins and other biological entities influence one another via causal molecular networks. Causal relationships in such networks are mediated by complex and diverse mechanisms, through latent variables, and are often specific to cellular context. It remains challenging to characterise such networks in practice. Here, we present a novel framework to evaluate large language models (LLMs) for zero-shot inference of causal relationships in biology. In particular, we systematically evaluate causal claims obtained from an LLM using real-world interventional data. This is done over one hundred variables and thousands of causal hypotheses. Furthermore, we consider several prompting and retrieval-augmentation strategies, including large, and potentially conflicting, collections of scientific articles. Our results show that with tailored augmentation and prompting, even relatively small LLMs can capture meaningful aspects of causal structure in biological systems. This supports the notion that LLMs could act as orchestration tools in biological discovery, by helping to distil current knowledge in ways amenable to downstream analysis. Our approach to assessing LLMs with respect to experimental data is relevant for a broad range of problems at the intersection of causal learning, LLMs and scientific discovery.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores the capability of Large Language Models (LLMs) to infer causal relationships in biology in a zero-shot setting.  It evaluates LLMs using real-world interventional data (perturbation screens) for a hundred genes, analyzing thousands of causal hypotheses. The authors tested various prompting and retrieval-augmentation strategies, including the use of scientific articles. The findings suggest that, with tailored augmentation and prompting, even relatively small LLMs can capture aspects of causal structure in biological systems. The study proposes LLMs as potentially valuable orchestration tools for biological discovery, aiding in distilling knowledge into actionable forms.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Novelty:** The paper addresses a timely and relevant question: Can LLMs contribute to causal discovery in biology? Evaluating LLMs *directly* against interventional experimental data is a novel approach. This moves beyond simply using LLMs for text summarization or embedding generation in biological contexts.
    *   **Rigorous Evaluation:** The use of Perturb-seq data to create a ground truth for causal relationships provides a strong basis for evaluating LLM performance. Considering different prompting strategies and retrieval-augmentation methods offers a systematic exploration of how to best utilize LLMs for this task.
    *   **Practical Relevance:**  The study focuses on a problem of high practical significance. Efficiently identifying causal relationships is a key bottleneck in biological research, especially in drug discovery and understanding disease mechanisms. The paper's findings may inspire tools to help reduce the experimental search space.
    *   **Negative Results Add Value:** The paper presents evidence that, under certain conditions, gene-specific information may not be helpful, which contributes to our understanding of what information is more relevant to causal inference. Similarly, the exploration into why chain-of-thought reasoning reduces performance offers insight in how to improve prompting strategies.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **LLM Scale:** The experiments are limited to a relatively small LLM (Gemma2-9B). While this choice is justified by resource constraints, it raises questions about how the results would generalize to larger, more powerful models.
    *   **AUROC as Sole Metric:** Relying solely on AUROC might not capture the nuances of causal inference. Precision and recall at different operating points could provide a more detailed picture of LLM performance.
    *   **Context Limitations:** While different experimental contexts were tested, there are more possible contexts (e.g., different cell types, other diseases) that have not been addressed. It is not clear how robust the results are with respect to changes in these contexts.
    *   **STRING Comparison:**  Using STRING as a zero-shot baseline, without adapting it, puts STRING at a clear disadvantage.  STRING is designed to provide *association scores* and would have benefitted by a transformation that better accounts for directionality (e.g.,  considering the difference in association scores for geneA->geneB and geneB->geneA).

*   **Significance:**

    The paper represents an important step towards using LLMs for automated causal discovery in biology. It demonstrates that, even in their current form, LLMs can provide valuable priors for causal structure learning, especially given the experimental search spaces involved. The limitations identified (e.g., limited helpfulness of gene-specific data, poorer chain-of-thought performance, limits of smaller models) point toward directions for future research to improve LLM-based causal discovery.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper is novel in its approach of evaluating LLMs against interventional data for causal inference in biology. The results are promising, yet also highlight the limitations of current LLMs and prompting strategies. The practicality of the approach, if the LLM output can be used to narrow the experimental search space, adds to the significance.  However, the use of a small LLM and reliance on only AUROC for evaluation limits the impact to a certain degree. Furthermore, the STRING baseline has been tested without tuning, putting STRING at a disadvantage. Overall, the paper's strengths outweigh its weaknesses, but significant improvements are needed before LLMs could be deployed as "orchestration tools."

### Layer-Specific Scaling of Positional Encodings for Superior Long-Context Modeling
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.04355v1)
- **Authors**: Zhenghua Wang, Yiran Ding, Changze Lv, Zhibo Xu, Tianlong Li, Tianyuan Shi, Xiaoqing Zheng, Xuanjing Huang
- **Abstract**: Although large language models (LLMs) have achieved significant progress in handling long-context inputs, they still suffer from the ``lost-in-the-middle'' problem, where crucial information in the middle of the context is often underrepresented or lost. Our extensive experiments reveal that this issue may arise from the rapid long-term decay in Rotary Position Embedding (RoPE). To address this problem, we propose a layer-specific positional encoding scaling method that assigns distinct scaling factors to each layer, slowing down the decay rate caused by RoPE to make the model pay more attention to the middle context. A specially designed genetic algorithm is employed to efficiently select the optimal scaling factors for each layer by incorporating Bezier curves to reduce the search space. Through comprehensive experimentation, we demonstrate that our method significantly alleviates the ``lost-in-the-middle'' problem. Our approach results in an average accuracy improvement of up to 20% on the Key-Value Retrieval dataset. Furthermore, we show that layer-specific interpolation, as opposed to uniform interpolation across all layers, enhances the model's extrapolation capabilities when combined with PI and Dynamic-NTK positional encoding schemes.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper, along with a novelty/significance score:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the "lost-in-the-middle" problem in large language models (LLMs) when handling long contexts. It proposes a layer-specific positional encoding scaling method that assigns different scaling factors to each layer, aiming to slow down the decay of Rotary Position Embedding (RoPE) and enhance attention to the middle context. The authors use a genetic algorithm, constrained by Bézier curves, to efficiently find optimal scaling factors for each layer. They demonstrate improvements in accuracy on the Key-Value Retrieval dataset and MDQA dataset, and also show improved extrapolation capabilities.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Addresses an Important Problem:** The "lost-in-the-middle" issue is a significant bottleneck in long-context LLMs, and this paper provides a direct approach to mitigating it.
    *   **Layer-Specific Approach:** Moving beyond uniform scaling strategies and proposing layer-specific scaling is a valuable contribution. It acknowledges the potentially varying roles and sensitivities of different layers within a transformer model.
    *   **Efficient Optimization:** Using a genetic algorithm with Bézier curve constraints provides a computationally feasible way to search the large space of possible layer-specific scaling factors. The experimental section supports the claims of efficiency.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The paper provides experimental results on standard datasets that demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed method. The performance gains are notable on some datasets, especially on the Key-Value Retrieval tasks, reaching up to 20% improvement on Key-Value Retrieval Dataset.
    *   **Extrapolation Improvements:** The paper demonstrates that their layer-specific method can improve model performance for sequence extrapolation which further underlines the strength of the method.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Model Sizes:** The experiments are limited to 7B parameter models. While the authors acknowledge this limitation, scaling experiments to larger models would significantly strengthen the claims. It's possible that the optimal scaling factors and the benefits of layer-specific scaling could differ substantially in much larger models.
    *   **Hyperparameter Sensitivity:** The genetic algorithm has hyperparameters that need to be tuned. While the authors claim efficiency, the sensitivity of the results to these hyperparameters is not thoroughly explored.
    *   **Limited Generalizability Evidence:** While some positive results on multiple models is presented, more evidence is needed to establish that the layer-specific scaling factors discovered generalize well across a broader range of models and tasks. It is unclear if the approach requires significant re-tuning for each new model and dataset.
    *   **Overclaiming:** The paper claims there is a reasonable explanation of why the approach mitigates the lost-in-the-middle-problem in the introduction, but doesn't delve into this aspect in great detail. The rationale given in the discussion section, while plausible, is not rigorously proven.
    *   **Baseline comparison**: The paper compares against strong, recent baselines but doesn't investigate simple alternatives to RoPE or different types of position encodings, which makes the contribution less clear.
*   **Novelty:**
    *   Applying layer-specific scaling to positional encodings is a novel approach. While head-specific scaling has been explored, this paper's focus on layer-specificity is a distinct contribution.
    *   The combination of a genetic algorithm and Bézier curves for efficiently optimizing scaling factors is an interesting and potentially reusable technique.
*   **Significance:**
    *   The paper's findings are relevant to the growing interest in long-context LLMs. Improving the handling of long contexts is crucial for many real-world applications.
    *   The proposed method is relatively simple to implement and computationally efficient, which could lead to its adoption by other researchers and practitioners.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a sound, novel approach to a significant problem in the field. The layer-specific scaling, along with the efficient optimization strategy, is a useful contribution. However, the limitations regarding model size and generalizability, as well as questions regarding algorithm and baseline comparison, prevent it from being a truly exceptional work. The empirical results are encouraging but need to be replicated on larger models and across a wider range of tasks.

### Lost in Literalism: How Supervised Training Shapes Translationese in LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.04369v1)
- **Authors**: Yafu Li, Ronghao Zhang, Zhilin Wang, Huajian Zhang, Leyang Cui, Yongjing Yin, Tong Xiao, Yue Zhang
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have achieved remarkable success in machine translation, demonstrating impressive performance across diverse languages. However, translationese, characterized by overly literal and unnatural translations, remains a persistent challenge in LLM-based translation systems. Despite their pre-training on vast corpora of natural utterances, LLMs exhibit translationese errors and generate unexpected unnatural translations, stemming from biases introduced during supervised fine-tuning (SFT). In this work, we systematically evaluate the prevalence of translationese in LLM-generated translations and investigate its roots during supervised training. We introduce methods to mitigate these biases, including polishing golden references and filtering unnatural training instances. Empirical evaluations demonstrate that these approaches significantly reduce translationese while improving translation naturalness, validated by human evaluations and automatic metrics. Our findings highlight the need for training-aware adjustments to optimize LLM translation outputs, paving the way for more fluent and target-language-consistent translations. We release the data and code at https://github.com/yafuly/LLM_Translationese.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Lost in Literalism: How Supervised Training Shapes Translationese in LLMs" investigates the presence of translationese (overly literal and unnatural translations) in large language models (LLMs) despite their pre-training on vast, natural language corpora. The authors systematically evaluate the prevalence of translationese in LLM-generated translations and attribute it to biases introduced during supervised fine-tuning (SFT). They propose mitigation strategies, including polishing golden references and filtering unnatural training instances. Empirical evaluations on English-Chinese and German-English translation tasks demonstrate that these approaches significantly reduce translationese while improving translation naturalness, as validated by human evaluations and automatic metrics. The paper releases the data and code used in the study.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in its systematic investigation of *translationese* specifically within the context of modern LLMs. While the phenomenon of translationese itself is not new, its resurgence and potential origins in the fine-tuning procedures applied to LLMs are less explored. The paper provides empirical evidence that SFT introduces or exacerbates the issue despite the models' inherent capacity for natural language generation. The proposed mitigation strategies are relatively straightforward (polishing references and filtering data), but their effectiveness, rigorously demonstrated, represents a valuable practical contribution. The methods themselves are also relatively novel in the context of addressing translationese. Prior work focuses on specialized algorithms and prompt engineering; refining the training data is a more direct approach.

*   **Significance:** The significance of the paper stems from the increasingly widespread use of LLMs for machine translation. Identifying and addressing limitations in translation style beyond adequacy is crucial for producing high-quality translations that are suitable for diverse real-world applications. The finding that SFT can degrade translation quality in terms of naturalness has important implications for how these models are trained and deployed. The release of data and code will further facilitate research in this area.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper employs a rigorous methodology, combining quantitative analysis (Translationese Span Ratio - TSR) with qualitative human evaluations.
    *   The authors systematically investigate potential causes of translationese, focusing on biases during SFT.
    *   The proposed mitigation strategies are practical and demonstrably effective, leading to improvements in translation naturalness.
    *   The study is well-written and clearly presents its findings, methodology, and limitations.
    *   The experiments are conducted across two language pairs, increasing the generalizability of the results.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   While the mitigation strategies are effective, they are not particularly sophisticated. The "polishing" approach relies on another LLM (GPT-4), which raises concerns about circularity and potential biases in the polishing process itself.
    *   The analysis is limited to English-Chinese and German-English translation. While this offers some generalizability, conclusions may not hold across all language pairs, especially those with very different linguistic structures or resource availability.
    *   The study focuses primarily on translationese related to overly literal translation patterns (unnatural sentence and phrase flow). Culture-specific and terminology-related errors are mentioned but not explored in detail.

*   **Potential Influence:** This paper has the potential to influence the training and evaluation of LLMs for machine translation. The findings underscore the need to carefully consider the impact of SFT data on translation style and to incorporate methods for improving and assessing naturalness. The proposed mitigation strategies can be readily adopted in existing training pipelines, and the released data can serve as a valuable resource for future research.

**Justification for the Score:**

The paper provides a solid, systematic investigation of a practically important problem (translationese in LLMs). The results are convincingly demonstrated through rigorous experiments and address a gap in the understanding of how LLMs are affected by fine-tuning. However, the mitigation methods are relatively simple and the scope of language pairs is limited. On the whole, this work makes a significant contribution by bringing a known problem in machine translation into the spotlight of LLM research and offering practical solutions.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### TRACT: Regression-Aware Fine-tuning Meets Chain-of-Thought Reasoning for LLM-as-a-Judge
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.04381v1)
- **Authors**: Cheng-Han Chiang, Hung-yi Lee, Michal Lukasik
- **Abstract**: The LLM-as-a-judge paradigm uses large language models (LLMs) for automated text evaluation, where a numerical assessment is assigned by an LLM to the input text following scoring rubrics. Existing methods for LLM-as-a-judge use cross-entropy (CE) loss for fine-tuning, which neglects the numeric nature of score prediction. Recent work addresses numerical prediction limitations of LLM fine-tuning through regression-aware fine-tuning, which, however, does not consider chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning for score prediction. In this paper, we introduce TRACT (Two-stage Regression-Aware fine-tuning with CoT), a method combining CoT reasoning with regression-aware training. TRACT consists of two stages: first, seed LLM is fine-tuned to generate CoTs, which serve as supervision for the second stage fine-tuning. The training objective of TRACT combines the CE loss for learning the CoT reasoning capabilities, and the regression-aware loss for the score prediction. Experiments across four LLM-as-a-judge datasets and two LLMs show that TRACT significantly outperforms existing methods. Extensive ablation studies validate the importance of each component in TRACT.
- **Summary**: Here is a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "TRACT: Regression-Aware Fine-tuning Meets Chain-of-Thought Reasoning for LLM-as-a-Judge."

**Summary:**

The paper introduces TRACT (Two-stage Regression-Aware fine-tuning with CoT), a novel method for fine-tuning Large Language Models (LLMs) to improve their performance as automated text evaluators ("LLM-as-a-judge"). TRACT combines chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning with regression-aware fine-tuning to address the limitations of traditional cross-entropy loss-based fine-tuning for numerical score prediction. It operates in two stages: first, the LLM is fine-tuned to generate CoTs, which then serve as supervision for the second stage. The training objective combines cross-entropy loss (for CoT reasoning) and regression-aware loss (for score prediction). The experiments on multiple LLM-as-a-judge datasets demonstrate that TRACT significantly outperforms existing methods and that both components contribute to the overall performance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in effectively integrating CoT reasoning with regression-aware fine-tuning (RAFT). While RAFT has been proposed to improve numerical output capabilities by using squared error loss, it did not incorporate CoT reasoning, a technique proven useful for increasing explainability and reasoning. Similarly, simply applying standard cross-entropy fine-tuning with CoT has inherent limitations for numerical prediction. TRACT cleverly addresses these limitations by combining both techniques in a two-stage approach, leveraging the strengths of each. The self-generation of CoTs to mitigate distribution shifts is also a useful refinement.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance stems from its potential to improve the accuracy and reliability of automated text evaluation using LLMs.  This is crucial in various applications, including automated grading, research evaluation, and system benchmarking, all of which benefit from more accurate scoring.  The authors show that TRACT consistently outperforms the state-of-the-art Prometheus model, which further highlights the improvement provided. The ablation studies further solidify this point by illustrating the importance of each component.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Statement:** The paper clearly identifies the limitations of existing approaches for LLM-as-a-judge.

    *   **Well-Designed Method:** TRACT is well-designed, combining CoT reasoning and regression-aware fine-tuning in a logical and effective manner.

    *   **Comprehensive Experiments:** The experiments cover a variety of datasets and models, and include ablation studies to validate the importance of each component of TRACT.

    *   **Thorough Analysis:** The analysis of results is thorough, including discussions of distribution shifts, the impact of self-generated CoTs, and sensitivity to hyperparameters.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Computational Cost:** The two-stage fine-tuning process can be computationally expensive, which the paper does not explicitly address.

    *   **Dependence on Generated Data:** Although the method uses self-generated CoTs to reduce the impact of generated data distribution shifts, the initial fine-tuning still relies on GPT-4 generated data, potentially inheriting its biases. It would be great to provide analysis about the impact.

    *   **Ablation Studies:** The ablation study could also have included an evaluation using the standard decoding method for self generated CoTs (CE Loss).
*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field by providing a more accurate and robust method for LLM-as-a-judge. It provides a strong framework that can be extended to incorporate additional techniques, such as more sophisticated self-generation strategies, refined regression losses, or different CoT generation approaches.  The release of code and models will further accelerate adoption and research in this area.
**Rationale for the Score:**

Considering the novelty, significance, strengths, and weaknesses, I assign a score of 8.

TRACT presents a solid contribution to the field of LLM-as-a-judge. While it builds upon existing techniques (CoT reasoning and RAFT), the effective integration of these techniques, coupled with the self-generation refinement, presents a novel approach that yields substantial performance improvements. The comprehensive experiments and analysis support the claims well. The method has limitations like computational cost and reliance on generated data biases that need further investigation; however, the strengths outweigh the weaknesses. TRACT is a strong framework that can be further extended in subsequent research.

### Shaping Shared Languages: Human and Large Language Models' Inductive Biases in Emergent Communication
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.04395v1)
- **Authors**: Tom Kouwenhoven, Max Peeperkorn, Roy de Kleijn, Tessa Verhoef
- **Abstract**: Languages are shaped by the inductive biases of their users. Using a classical referential game, we investigate how artificial languages evolve when optimised for inductive biases in humans and large language models (LLMs) via Human-Human, LLM-LLM and Human-LLM experiments. We show that referentially grounded vocabularies emerge that enable reliable communication in all conditions, even when humans and LLMs collaborate. Comparisons between conditions reveal that languages optimised for LLMs subtly differ from those optimised for humans. Interestingly, interactions between humans and LLMs alleviate these differences and result in vocabularies which are more human-like than LLM-like. These findings advance our understanding of how inductive biases in LLMs play a role in the dynamic nature of human language and contribute to maintaining alignment in human and machine communication. In particular, our work underscores the need to think of new methods that include human interaction in the training processes of LLMs, and shows that using communicative success as a reward signal can be a fruitful, novel direction.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates how shared languages are shaped by the inductive biases of humans and large language models (LLMs) through emergent communication. It employs a classical referential game, comparing artificial languages that evolve in Human-Human, LLM-LLM, and Human-LLM interactions. The results demonstrate that referentially grounded vocabularies emerge in all conditions, enabling successful communication, even between humans and LLMs. Furthermore, the paper finds that languages optimized for LLMs exhibit subtle differences from those optimized for humans. Interestingly, Human-LLM interactions lead to vocabularies that are more human-like than LLM-like. The authors argue that these findings contribute to understanding the role of inductive biases in LLMs and the dynamic nature of human language, highlighting the importance of incorporating human interaction in LLM training.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper builds upon prior work on emergent communication with LLMs, specifically the authors' own previous work [Kouwenhoven et al., 2025]. The primary novel contribution is the introduction of a Human-LLM interaction condition to the referential game paradigm. This allows for a direct comparison of language evolution when shaped by different entities with different learning biases. While the concept of human-AI interaction in language is not entirely new, the controlled experimental setup and the focus on inductive biases in emergent communication do represent a step forward.
*   **Significance:** The paper addresses an important and timely issue: maintaining alignment and mutual understandability between humans and AI systems. The finding that Human-LLM interaction can lead to more human-like languages is significant. It suggests that incorporating human involvement in LLM training, especially through communication-based reward signals, could be a fruitful avenue for future research. It also highlights the limitations of solely relying on AI-generated data for training, as this can lead to drift and a collapse of human-understandable vocabularies.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   The experimental design is well-structured, allowing for controlled comparisons between different conditions.
    *   The use of a classical referential game provides a solid theoretical foundation.
    *   The paper identifies and analyzes several language metrics (TopSim, homonymy, synonymy, etc.) providing a multifaceted view of how languages evolve.
    *   The results are clearly presented and discussed, with appropriate statistical analysis.
    *   The paper offers a compelling argument for the importance of human interaction in LLM training.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The complexity of human cognitive biases is not fully addressed. While inductive biases relating to statistical learning and patterns are explored, the social factors influencing human preferences during these games need deeper investigation.
    *   Generalizability might be limited. The study uses a relatively simple artificial language. The results may not directly translate to the complexities of natural language evolution or human-machine interaction in more realistic settings.
    *   The number of participants and simulations are not enormous, thus limiting the conclusions that may be drawn. The number of Human-LLM simulations is on the lower side to confidently gauge how the languages are shaped for collaboration.
    *   The LLM agents are not updated in real-time, thus limiting the scope of collaboration to a single round. More active collaboration wherein the vocabulary is updated in all turns can reveal additional insights.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence research directions in LLM training, emphasizing the need for human-in-the-loop approaches. It also contributes to the growing body of literature on emergent communication and the role of inductive biases in language evolution. The findings could inform the design of more human-friendly AI systems that are better aligned with human communication preferences.

**Justification for the Score:**

The paper presents a valuable contribution by directly comparing how language is shaped by human and LLM interactions in a controlled setting. However, given that it is an extension of prior work and has some limitations in scope and generalizability, it does not rise to the level of an exceptional or groundbreaking contribution. The results, while interesting, are also somewhat incremental. Therefore, a score reflecting a good, solid contribution with noticeable novelty and significance is justified.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### TableLoRA: Low-rank Adaptation on Table Structure Understanding for Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.04396v1)
- **Authors**: Xinyi He, Yihao Liu, Mengyu Zhou, Yeye He, Haoyu Dong, Shi Han, Zejian Yuan, Dongmei Zhang
- **Abstract**: Tabular data are crucial in many fields and their understanding by large language models (LLMs) under high parameter efficiency paradigm is important. However, directly applying parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) techniques to tabular tasks presents significant challenges, particularly in terms of better table serialization and the representation of two-dimensional structured information within a one-dimensional sequence. To address this, we propose TableLoRA, a module designed to improve LLMs' understanding of table structure during PEFT. It incorporates special tokens for serializing tables with special token encoder and uses 2D LoRA to encode low-rank information on cell positions. Experiments on four tabular-related datasets demonstrate that TableLoRA consistently outperforms vanilla LoRA and surpasses various table encoding methods tested in control experiments. These findings reveal that TableLoRA, as a table-specific LoRA, enhances the ability of LLMs to process tabular data effectively, especially in low-parameter settings, demonstrating its potential as a robust solution for handling table-related tasks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces TableLoRA, a module designed to improve the ability of Large Language Models (LLMs) to understand and process tabular data within the Parameter-Efficient Fine-Tuning (PEFT) paradigm. TableLoRA addresses challenges in table serialization and the representation of 2D structured information as a 1D sequence. It incorporates special tokens for table serialization, a special token encoder, and a 2D LoRA mechanism to encode low-rank information about cell positions. Experimental results on four tabular datasets demonstrate that TableLoRA outperforms vanilla LoRA and other table encoding methods, particularly in low-parameter settings.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The primary novelty of the paper lies in the introduction of a table-specific LoRA module. While LoRA and PEFT techniques are established, TableLoRA tailors LoRA to the specific needs of tabular data. The use of special tokens and the 2D LoRA component to encode cell positions represent novel contributions. However, the individual components (LoRA, special tokens) are not revolutionary, the **combination** of these targeted to tables and its architecture is novel.
*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a relevant problem: how to efficiently fine-tune LLMs for tabular data understanding. Tabular data is ubiquitous, and efficient LLM adaptation is crucial. By improving performance with a low-parameter approach, TableLoRA contributes to making LLMs more accessible and practical for real-world applications. The improvements over vanilla LoRA are significant, and the reduction of the gap between LoRA and full fine-tuning highlights the effectiveness of the module.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear problem definition:** The paper identifies key challenges in applying PEFT to tabular data (serialization, 2D representation).
    *   **Well-defined solution:** TableLoRA is clearly described and motivated.
    *   **Comprehensive experiments:** The paper includes main experiments, control experiments (comparing TableLoRA to common tabular representation learning methods), and ablation studies to analyze the contribution of each component.
    *   **Analyzed different Model settings** The evaluation of several models helps confirm generality of the framework.
    *   **Case study and analysis:** Includes qualitative and ablation analyses
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Scope limitations:** The paper focuses solely on PEFT and LoRA. While this is a strength in terms of focus, it doesn't explore alternative approaches to tabular data understanding with LLMs (e.g. those mentioned in the related works section).
    *   **Generalizability/Scalability** The reliance on specific table serialization may need further study to assess performance over varied types of data that may not conform to the presented tokenization strategies.
    *   **Limited model range:** The limitation of using three models from the Llama family can reduce the impact of the conclusions drawn. A wider range would increase the credibility of results.
    *   **Dependency on Table Serialization** Is there a potential for brittle performance given the reliance on the specific serializations defined?
*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence research in several directions:
    *   **Table-specific PEFT:** Encourages development of specialized PEFT techniques for different data types.
    *   **Structural encoding:** Provides a blueprint for encoding structural information into LLMs.
    *   **Practical applications:** Could be used in various applications involving tabular data, such as data analysis, information extraction, and question answering.
    *   **Research into what constitutes table structure comprehension** Encourages study into what contributes to a tables structure comprehension capabilities.
*   **Justification:** The paper makes a valuable contribution by addressing the challenges of fine-tuning LLMs for tabular data understanding. The TableLoRA module is a novel approach with demonstrated improvements over vanilla LoRA. While the individual components are not groundbreaking, their combination and tailoring to tabular data are significant. However, the paper could benefit from a broader exploration of alternative methods and scalability considerations.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rigorous Rationale:** The score of 7.5 reflects the paper's solid contribution to a relevant problem. The novelty lies in the specific combination and adaptation of existing techniques to create a table-specific LoRA module. The experimental results provide strong evidence for the effectiveness of TableLoRA. The main reason it does not receive a higher score is the limited scope (focus solely on PEFT, reliance on table serialization/tokenization) and that most of the individual components are not necessarily novel on their own. Though a novel architecture, the limitations outlined make this solid, but not field-transforming in its impact.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Speculative MoE: Communication Efficient Parallel MoE Inference with Speculative Token and Expert Pre-scheduling
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.04398v1)
- **Authors**: Yan Li, Pengfei Zheng, Shuang Chen, Zewei Xu, Yunfei Du, Zhengang Wang
- **Abstract**: MoE (Mixture of Experts) prevails as a neural architecture that can scale modern transformer-based LLMs (Large Language Models) to unprecedented scales. Nevertheless, large MoEs' great demands of computing power, memory capacity and memory bandwidth make scalable serving a fundamental challenge and efficient parallel inference has become a requisite to attain adequate throughput under latency constraints. DeepSpeed-MoE, one state-of-the-art MoE inference framework, adopts a 3D-parallel paradigm including EP (Expert Parallelism), TP (Tensor Parallel) and DP (Data Parallelism). However, our analysis shows DeepSpeed-MoE's inference efficiency is largely bottlenecked by EP, which is implemented with costly all-to-all collectives to route token activation. Our work aims to boost DeepSpeed-MoE by strategically reducing EP's communication overhead with a technique named Speculative MoE.
